\begin{document}

\title{\asgard: A Radius-Ordered GRB Afterglow Toolkit for Relativistic Dynamics, Particle Transport, and Observer-time Projection}

\author[0000-0000-0000-0000]{Jia Ren}
\affiliation{Affiliation to be supplied}
\email{email to be supplied}

\begin{abstract}
Multiwavelength \grb\ afterglow fits are degenerate because different local
processes can produce similar \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\).  Density structure,
reverse shocks, inverse-Compton cooling, hadronic losses, secondary pairs, and
observer geometry can all change the same light curve.  We present \asgard, a
Python/Fortran toolkit that records the radius-ordered local state of the
blast-wave dynamics, particles, photons, optional reverse shocks, optional
hadronic/pair feedback, absorption, and observer components.  The emission is
projected to \(t_{\rm obs}\) only after these local quantities have been written.
The supported calculation includes top-hat, Gaussian, and power-law jets;
uniform, \(k=2\) wind, and tabulated media; several electron transport solvers;
forward-shock synchrotron and SSC emission; a one-dimensional shell-averaged
hadronic branch; reverse-shock diagnostics; and component-resolved observer
products.  The central object is the state sequence containing \(\Gamma(R)\),
\(B'(R)\), particle spectra, photon fields, optical depths, and component
luminosities.  This record lets dynamical breaks, angular-projection effects,
cooling transitions, component crossings, and photon attenuation be traced back
to local source terms before observer projection mixes them.  Source-data-backed
figures test the state chain, validation metrics, five-code public-output
benchmarks where assumptions can be matched, and ASGARD-only complex-state
dashboard.  The implementation is bounded: PyBlastAfterglowMag is used through a
compiled repo-local \texttt{pba.out} for the FS and Gaussian-geometry rows, but
not for ASGARD RS/SSC or complex-state claims; formal hadronic feedback is
one-dimensional, \(\chi\)-resolved hadronic transport and IC-mediated
electromagnetic cascades are unsupported, user-defined media do not enter the
Fortran transport kernels, wind profiles with \(k\ne2\) are rejected, and jet
spreading is unsupported.
\end{abstract}

\keywords{Gamma-ray bursts (629) --- Computational methods (1965) --- Astronomy software (1855) --- Radiative processes (2055) --- Non-thermal radiation sources (1119)}

\section{Introduction} \label{sec:intro}

Gamma-ray burst afterglows are commonly interpreted as synchrotron radiation
from nonthermal particles in a relativistic blast wave
\citep{MeszarosRees1997,Sari1998,Granot2002}.  The same observed
\(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\), however, can be produced by more than one physical
history.  A density jump can change the shock energy budget.  A reverse shock can
add a second emitting region.  Inverse-Compton cooling can move the cooling break.
Absorption can remove photons after they are emitted.  Angular structure and
off-axis projection can reshape the same local emission before it reaches the
observer.  The modeling problem is therefore not only to fit a light curve, but
also to identify which local state made the fitted feature.

The need for this diagnostic view has grown with modern afterglow data.  GRB
170817A required structured-jet and off-axis calculations, and it continues to
constrain late-time jet dynamics
\citep{Troja2017GW170817Xray,Hallinan2017GW170817Radio,LambKobayashi2017Structured,Mooley2018WideAngle,Mooley2018Superluminal,Lazzati2018CollimatedJet,Margutti2018GW170817,Alexander2018GW170817,Ghirlanda2019StructuredJet,Troja2019GW170817,Hajela2022GW170817,Katira2025GW170817,Ryan2024}.
GRB 190114C, GRB 190829A, and GRB 221009A made very-high-energy emission,
SSC-dominated interpretations, and narrow structured jets part of routine model
comparison
\citep{MAGIC2019TeV,MAGIC2019IC,HESS2019VHE,HESS2021GRB190829A,LHAASO2023TeVAfterglow,LHAASO2023Beyond10TeV,Laskar2023GRB221009A,OConnor2023GRB221009A}.
These cases show why a method paper must expose the dynamics, particle spectra,
photon targets, attenuation, and observer projection separately when those
objects are used to interpret a broadband feature.

Several public codes define the comparison landscape.  \afterglowpy\ provides
efficient synchrotron flux calculations for structured jets and off-axis
geometries \citep{Ryan2020}.  \jetsimpy\ emphasizes hydrodynamically motivated
thin-shell evolution and lateral structure \citep{Wang2024Jetsimpy}.  \vegas\
gives a recent code-paper reference for jets, media, dynamics, radiation,
observer geometry, examples, performance, and reverse-shock jump conditions
\citep{Wang2025VegasAfterglow}.  \pyblast\ is a modular multiphysics blast-wave
framework for transient counterparts \citep{Nedora2025PyBlastAfterglow}.  ASGARD
is complementary to these tools.  It records the radius-ordered local state of
dynamics, particles, photons, optional reverse shocks, optional hadronic/pair
feedback, absorption, and observer components, so that complex multi-physics
light-curve features can be traced back to their local physical causes before
observer projection mixes them.  Claims outside that stored state, projection
operator, and benchmark evidence are treated as unsupported.

The manuscript follows the logic of the calculation, but it separates the main
physical state from technical solver details.  Section~\ref{sec:contract}
defines the radius-ordered state.  Section~\ref{sec:dynamics} gives the jet,
external medium, and forward-shock dynamics.  Section~\ref{sec:electron} gives
particle and radiation transport, including the electron, synchrotron, SSC, and
absorption state.  Sections~\ref{sec:reverse-shock} and~\ref{sec:hadronic} give
the reverse-shock and hadronic/pair-feedback branches.  Section~\ref{sec:projection}
gives observer projection, and Section~\ref{sec:fitting} records runtime and
reproducibility products.  Section~\ref{sec:verification} states the benchmark
evidence, Section~\ref{sec:discussion} interprets what that evidence can and
cannot show, and Section~\ref{sec:limits} gives the hard model boundaries.

\section{Model Definition and Radius-Ordered State} \label{sec:contract}

\asgard\ treats an afterglow calculation as a radial history of a shocked shell.  The public input specifies the jet, external density, observer, microphysics, frequency and time grids, and the optional hadronic or reverse-shock switches.  The code first turns these inputs into one runtime model and then advances the shock from small to large radius.  Observer products are not evolved as independent variables.  They are assembled only after the local emitting and absorbing material has been computed.

The radius ordering is physical, not only computational.  At a given shock radius \(R\), the dynamics determine the external density \(n(R)\), Lorentz factor \(\Gamma(R)\), swept-up mass \(M_{\rm sw}(R)\), internal energy \(U(R)\), and comoving magnetic field \(B'(R)\).  These quantities define the shell in which electrons are injected and cooled.  The same shell also supplies the synchrotron photon field, self-absorption optical depth, inverse-Compton targets, and, when requested, the \(p\gamma\), Bethe-Heitler, \(pp\), and \(\gamma\gamma\) targets.  Only after these local quantities are known does the observer module apply Doppler factors, equal-arrival-time geometry, distance factors, and attenuation.

This order is required because the observer light curve is many-to-one with respect to the local physics.  A break in \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\) may be produced by blast-wave deceleration, angular structure, a cooling transition, a component crossing, a reverse shock, or local photon absorption.  If the calculation stores only the final flux, those possibilities cannot be separated.  The radius-ordered state instead keeps the quantities that generated the flux visible before they are mixed by projection \citep{Granot2002,vanEerten2010,vanEerten2012,vanEerten2018BlastWaves,Salafia2022JetStructure}.

The calculation then follows five visible steps.  Public inputs define a runtime configuration.  The dynamics module writes the shell state.  The electron and photon modules write the lepton distribution, synchrotron/SSC source terms, and photon-survival factors.  Optional hadronic and reverse-shock modules append their own state only when their switches are active.  The observer module projects the assembled local emissivity and attenuation to \(t_{\rm obs}\).  If a supported electron-photon or hadronic feedback loop is active, the coupled transport is iterated before projection.  The projection step only reads the local state produced by the earlier modules.

For one active radial cell, the state record can be summarized as
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{S}(R)=
\left\{
\begin{array}{l}
\Gamma,M_{\rm sw},U,B',
N_e,n_\gamma,N_p,N_\pi,N_\mu,
\\
Q_e^{\rm sec},\tau_{\gamma\gamma},L_\nu^{\rm comp}
\end{array}
\right\}.
\end{equation}
This notation is a state list, not a claim that every run contains every field.
A one-dimensional leptonic calculation writes the forward-shock dynamics,
electron spectrum, photon field, synchrotron and SSC components, and absorption
factors.  A formal hadronic calculation additionally writes the proton, pion,
muon, neutrino, photon-loss, and secondary-pair fields.  A reverse-shock
calculation writes a separate region-3 state before the same observer-time
projection is applied.  Unsupported combinations are absent from the state
record rather than inferred from partial fields.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_radius_state.pdf}
  \caption{Radius-ordered state for an \asgard\ calculation.  (a) Public inputs are normalized into a fixed sequence: runtime configuration, dynamics, electron and photon state, bounded optional branches, and observer projection.  (b) The blast-wave Lorentz factor and comoving magnetic field are stored on the shock-radius grid before observer products are formed.  (c) The equal-arrival-time mapping uses \(t_{\rm obs}=(1+z)[t_{\rm on}(R)+R(1-\mu)/c]\), so different angular cells map the same local radius sequence to different observer-time sequences.}
  \label{fig:radius-state}
\end{figure*}

The supported observer products are derived from this state record: multi-frequency
fluxes, fixed-time spectra, band-integrated fluxes, exposure-averaged fluxes,
component-resolved fluxes, and diagnostic state tracks.  The component record
contains the total flux and the active physical channels: forward-shock
synchrotron and SSC emission, reverse-shock synchrotron and SSC emission,
cross-zone inverse Compton emission, hadronic synchrotron emission, pair
synchrotron emission, and neutrino outputs.  The diagnostic state exposes the
radius, Lorentz factor, magnetic field, electron spectra, \(\chi\)-resolved
electron fields, hadronic species, photon-survival factors, reverse-shock state,
and secondary reverse-shock event diagnostics when those calculations are active.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig2_forward_api.pdf}
  \caption{From dynamics to observer products in one forward-shock calculation.  (a) The local \(\Gamma(R)\) and \(B'(R)\) state supplies the source terms for the observer calculation.  (b) Multi-frequency light curves, (c) fixed-time spectra, and (d) the SSC flux fraction are assembled from the same radius-ordered state.  The figure is an accounting diagnostic, not an independent fit to a particular burst.}
  \label{fig:forward-api}
\end{figure*}

The public model definition is intentionally bounded.  \asgard\ is not presented
as a replacement for faster light-curve-only tools.  Its purpose in this paper is
to make a coupled afterglow calculation inspectable by preserving the state that
precedes observer projection.  The supported runtime families are listed in
Section~\ref{sec:limits}; physical combinations outside those families are not
interpreted as modeled behavior.

\section{Dynamics and Jet/Medium Setup} \label{sec:dynamics}

The dynamics section defines the blast wave before any radiation is computed.
The forward shock first needs the external density \(n(R)\), the angular energy
profile, and the angular Lorentz-factor profile.  From these inputs the dynamics
module writes \(\Gamma(R)\), swept-up matter, internal energy, and the magnetic
field used by the particle and radiation modules.

\subsection{External media}

The external medium is the gas swept up by the forward shock.  In the dynamics it
enters through the proton number density \(n(R)\).  \asgard\ supports three
families: a uniform medium, a Chevalier--Li \(k=2\) stellar wind, and a tabulated
radial profile.  The same \(n(R)\) is then passed to particle injection, photon
production, absorption, and observer projection at that radius.

\subsubsection{Analytic media}

The analytic media give the upstream density directly.  A uniform medium has a
constant density, while a stellar wind falls as \(R^{-2}\).  The implemented
forms are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n(R) &= n_{\rm ISM},\\
n_{\rm w}(R) &= \frac{A_\ast A_0}{m_pR^2},\\
n_0(R) &=
\begin{cases}
n_{\rm ISM}, & n_{\rm w}(R)\le n_{\rm ISM}/\eta_{\rm w},\\
n_{\rm w}(R), & n_{\rm w}(R)> n_{\rm ISM}/\eta_{\rm w},
\end{cases}\\
R_0 &= \left(\frac{A_\ast A_0}{m_p n_{\rm cap}}\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\rm ISM}\) is the uniform density, \(A_0\) is the Chevalier--Li wind
mass-loading scale, \(m_p\) is the proton mass, and \(\eta_{\rm w}\) sets the
wind-to-ISM switch \citep{ChevalierLi2000}.  If a user supplies a cap density
\(n_{\rm cap}\), the code defines \(R_0\) and holds the wind density at
\(n_{\rm w}(R_0)\) inside that radius.  A uniform medium remains at
\(n_{\rm ISM}\).  This cap is a hard inner density state; a wind profile with
\(k\ne2\) is unsupported.

\subsubsection{Tabulated media and jumps}

A tabulated medium supplies the upstream density from user points.  The table
contains positive radii \(R_i\) and positive densities \(n_i\), with the radii in
strictly increasing order and \(N_\rho\le N_{\rho,\max}\).  Between two adjacent
points, the code uses the local power law
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n(R) &=
\exp\left[
\operatorname{interp}\left(\ln R;\,\ln R_i,\,\ln n_i\right)
\right],\\
n(R) &=
n_i
\left(\frac{R}{R_i}\right)^{q_i},\qquad
q_i\equiv\frac{\ln(n_{i+1}/n_i)}{\ln(R_{i+1}/R_i)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first two table points set the extrapolation below the table, and the last
two set the extrapolation above it.  The table is therefore extended as a power
law at both ends.  A tabulated medium and the Gaussian jump model are separate
input families; they are not combined in one run.

Density jumps are local perturbations of an analytic baseline density.  They are
used only with the analytic medium family.  For several Gaussian structures, the
density is
\begin{equation}
n(R)=n_0(R)
\left[
1+\sum_j
(f_j-1)
\exp\left(
-\frac{(R-R_j)^2}{2(\Delta R_j)^2}
\right)
\right].
\end{equation}
Here \(R_j\) is the center, \(f_j\) is the density factor, and
\(\Delta R_j\equiv w_jR_j\) is the width.  One Gaussian gives the older
single-jump interface.  Values with \(f_j<1\) represent density dips, and they
are allowed only when the total density remains positive.  These Gaussians are
controlled density perturbations for afterglow calculations; this paper does not
claim a unique environment for each Gaussian
\citep{DaiLu2002,NakarGranot2007,UhmZhang2014}.

The medium setup also defines hard input boundaries.  It supports
\(N_J\le N_{J,\max}\) Gaussian structures or \(N_\rho\le N_{\rho,\max}\)
tabulated points.  It rejects nonpositive jump radii, factors, or widths;
nonpositive table radii or densities; tables with fewer than two points; and
tables whose radii are not ordered.  These checks define the supported medium
families, not extra physical assumptions.

\subsection{Jet angular profiles}

The jet profile sets the initial energy and Lorentz factor of each polar
element.  It is an initial condition, not an angular-evolution model.  A top-hat
jet is uniform inside \(\theta_j\) and empty outside it:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{\rm iso}(\theta) &=
\begin{cases}
E_c, & \theta<\theta_j,\\
0, & \theta\ge\theta_j,
\end{cases}\\
\Gamma_0(\theta) &=
\begin{cases}
\Gamma_c, & \theta<\theta_j,\\
1, & \theta\ge\theta_j .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
A Gaussian jet decreases both the isotropic-equivalent energy and
\(\Gamma_0-1\) away from the axis:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{\rm iso}(\theta) &=
E_c\exp\left[-\frac{\theta^2}{2\theta_c^2}\right],\\
\Gamma_0(\theta) &=
1+(\Gamma_c-1)\exp\left[-\frac{\theta^2}{2\theta_c^2}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The Gaussian profile is used only inside the declared outer support
\(\theta_{\max}\).  A power-law jet has a flat core and power-law wings:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{\rm iso}(\theta) &=
E_c
\begin{cases}
1, & \theta\le \theta_c,\\
(\theta/\theta_c)^{-k_E}, & \theta>\theta_c,
\end{cases}\\
\Gamma_0(\theta) &=
\begin{cases}
\Gamma_c, & \theta\le \theta_c,\\
1+(\Gamma_c-1)(\theta/\theta_c)^{-k_\Gamma}, & \theta>\theta_c .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(E_c\) and \(\Gamma_c\) are the on-axis values, \(\theta_c\) is the core
angle, and \(k_E\) and \(k_\Gamma\) set the wing slopes.  The public profile uses
\(k_\Gamma=k_E\) unless a separate Lorentz-factor slope is supplied.  These
profiles feed independent angular elements; the present backend does not evolve
lateral spreading.

\subsection{Forward-shock dynamical variables}

The forward-shock state carries the variables needed by later radiation modules.
The state vector is
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}=(\Gamma,\mathcal{M},U,R),
\end{equation}
and the independent variable is the source-frame on-axis arrival coordinate
\(t_{\rm on}\).  Here \(\Gamma\) is the blast-wave Lorentz factor, \(U\) is the
internal energy, and \(R\) is the shock radius.  The second entry
\(\mathcal{M}\) depends on the dynamics branch: branches 1 and 2 store a swept
number column, while branch 3 stores the swept mass.  This vector is the local
dynamical input to electron injection and radiation.

The coordinate \(t_{\rm on}\) tracks on-axis photon arrival, not laboratory time.
Along the radial sequence,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dt_{\rm on}}{dR} &= \frac{1-\beta}{\beta c},\\
\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}} &= \frac{\beta c}{1-\beta},\\
\beta &= \sqrt{1-\Gamma^{-2}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The second relation is therefore not the lab-frame shock speed.  It is the rate
at which the radius grid is traversed when \(t_{\rm on}\) is the independent
variable.  Off-axis observer times are assembled later from
\(t_{\rm obs}=(1+z)[t_{\rm on}(R)+R(1-\mu)/c]\), so this coordinate is a
dynamical ordering variable rather than the final observer clock.

The magnetic field is set by the local post-shock energy density.  For the
forward shock, the code uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e' &\simeq4(\Gamma^2-1)n(R)m_pc^2,\\
B' &=\left(8\pi\epsilon_B e'\right)^{1/2}
=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n(R)m_pc^2(\Gamma^2-1)\right]^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n(R)\) is the upstream proton density and \(\epsilon_B\) is the magnetic
energy fraction.  The output \(B'(R)\) sets synchrotron cooling, synchrotron
emission, SSC seed production, and magnetic diagnostics.

The dynamics branch also records electron energy scales used for startup and
diagnostics.  With \(t'_{\rm dyn}\equiv\Gamma t_{\rm on}\), these scales are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_c=
\frac{6\pi m_ec}{\sigma_{\rm es} B'^2t'_{\rm dyn}},
\\
\gamma_m=
1+\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}
\frac{p-2}{p-1}
(\Gamma-1).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\gamma_c\) is the synchrotron cooling Lorentz factor, \(\gamma_m\) is the
minimum Lorentz factor of injected nonthermal electrons, \(\epsilon_e\) is the
electron energy fraction, \(f_e\) is the accelerated electron fraction, and \(p\)
is the injected electron index.  If \(\gamma_c>\gamma_m\) and \(p\ge2\), the
dynamics branch estimates the radiated fraction as
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\rm eff}=\epsilon_e\left(\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_c}\right)^{p-2};
\end{equation}
otherwise it uses \(\epsilon_{\rm eff}=\epsilon_e\).  This estimate affects only
the dynamics branch that asks for a radiative efficiency; the electron transport
module still evolves \(N_e\) explicitly.

The first radial grid needs a deceleration scale.  The setup first defines
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{D}_0=
\frac{E_{\rm iso}}{4\pi(\Gamma_0-1)m_pc^2},
\end{equation}
where \(E_{\rm iso}\) and \(\Gamma_0\) are the initial isotropic-equivalent
energy and Lorentz factor of the angular element.  The analytic estimates for
the deceleration radius are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{\rm dec,ISM}
&=\left(\frac{n_{\rm ISM}\Gamma_0}{\mathcal{D}_0}\right)^{-1/3},\\
R_{\rm dec,wind}
&=\frac{3\mathcal{D}_0}{2(A_\ast A_0/m_p)\Gamma_0}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_\ast A_0/m_p\) is the wind number-density normalization.  For wind
media the setup uses \(R_{\rm dec}=\min(R_{\rm dec,ISM},R_{\rm dec,wind})\);
otherwise it uses \(R_{\rm dec}=R_{\rm dec,ISM}\).  The corresponding on-axis
arrival scale is
\[
t_{\rm dec}=\frac{R_{\rm dec}(1-\beta_0)}{\beta_0c},
\qquad
\beta_0=(1-\Gamma_0^{-2})^{1/2}.
\]
For \(\Gamma_0\gg1\), this reduces to
\(t_{\rm dec}\simeq R_{\rm dec}/(2\Gamma_0^2c)\).  The solver uses this scale to
place early radial points across the coasting-to-decelerating transition; it does
not impose a broken power law.

\subsection{Dynamical branches}

The dynamics module provides three ways to evolve the same forward-shock state.
Branch 1 is the radiative-efficiency form often used in afterglow calculations
\citep{BlandfordMcKee1976,HuangDaiLu1999}.  Branch 2 is a legacy
variable-index form.  Branch 3 is the default mass-energy baseline.  All three
branches output \(\Gamma(R)\), swept matter, \(U(R)\), and \(R\); they differ in
how they write the inertia and energy budget.

\subsubsection{Shared equation of state and injection source}

The equation of state sets how the shocked gas stores heat.  Branches 2 and 3
need an adiabatic index that changes from the relativistic to the Newtonian
limit.  \asgard\ evaluates a fixed equation-of-state fit:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u&=\Gamma\beta,\qquad
\Theta=\frac{u}{3}\frac{u+a_\Theta u^2}{1+u+a_\Theta u^2},\\
\zeta&=\frac{\Theta}{a_\zeta+\Theta},\qquad
\hat{\gamma}=
\frac{1}{3}\sum_{r=0}^{N_{\hat\gamma}}a_r\zeta^r .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(u\) is the dimensionless four-velocity, \(\Theta\) is the temperature-like
fit variable, and \(\zeta\) maps that variable to the polynomial domain.  The
coefficients \((a_\Theta,a_\zeta,\{a_r\})\) and the order \(N_{\hat\gamma}\) are
fixed by the relativistic Maxwell--Juttner equation-of-state fit
\citep{Service1986,Peer2012}.  The output is \(\hat{\gamma}(\Gamma)\), not a
fit to any light curve.

The energy-injection source is a prescribed luminosity added to the dynamics.
In source-frame time it is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})=
\begin{cases}
L_0(t_{\rm on}/t_1)^{q}, & t_1\le t_{\rm on}\le t_2,\\
0, & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) are source-frame injection bounds, \(L_0\) is the
luminosity normalization, and \(q\) is the temporal index.  The public
constructor stores the bounds in observer-frame form, and the dynamics divide
them by \(1+z\) before evaluating the source-frame equation.  This term changes
the blast-wave energy budget; it is not a separate spin-down calculation.

The chosen dynamics branch defines where energy injection acts.  Branches 1 and
3 include \(\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})\) in the Lorentz-factor equation.  Branch 2
does not include this source.  Energy injection is therefore part of the local
dynamics, not a correction to the final light curve.

\subsubsection{Swept-medium state variables}

The swept-medium variable records how much external gas has entered the shocked
region.  Branches 1 and 2 evolve a swept number column \(\mu\).  Branch 3
evolves the physical swept mass \(M\).  They obey
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_\mu &\equiv\frac{d\mu}{dt_{\rm on}}
=n(R)R^2\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}},\\
\frac{dM}{dR} &=4\pi n(R)R^2m_p .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The corresponding initial ejecta normalizations are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu_0 &=\frac{E_{\rm iso}}{4\pi(\Gamma_0-1)m_pc^2},\\
M_0 &=\frac{E_{\rm iso}}{(\Gamma_0-1)c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For branches 1 and 2, the reported swept mass is \(M_{\rm sw}=4\pi m_p\mu\).  In
branch 3, \(M\) is already the swept mass in grams, and
\[
\frac{dM}{dt_{\rm on}}=\frac{dM}{dR}\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}.
\]

\subsubsection{Branch 1: radiative-efficiency dynamics}

Branch 1 evolves \(\Gamma\) by balancing swept-up deceleration against optional
energy injection.  Its equations are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}}=
\frac{-(\Gamma^2-1)q_\mu
+\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})/(4\pi m_pc^2)}
{\mu_0+\epsilon_{\rm eff}\mu
+2(1-\epsilon_{\rm eff})\Gamma\mu},
\\
\frac{dU}{dt_{\rm on}}=(\Gamma-1)q_\mu .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The negative term in \(d\Gamma/dt_{\rm on}\) is deceleration by swept-up matter.
The positive term is the optional injected luminosity.  The branch outputs
\(\Gamma\) and \(U\) on the same swept-column state \(\mu\).

\subsubsection{Branch 2: variable-index legacy dynamics}

Branch 2 keeps the swept column \(\mu\) but lets \(\hat{\gamma}\) change the
effective inertia.  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{N}_2&=
\hat{\gamma}(\Gamma^2-1)-(\hat{\gamma}-1)\Gamma\beta^2,
\\
\mathcal{D}_2&=\mu_0+\epsilon_{\rm eff}\mu\\
&\quad
+(1-\epsilon_{\rm eff})\mu
\left[
2\hat{\gamma}\Gamma-(\hat{\gamma}-1)(1+\Gamma^{-2})
\right],
\\
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}}&=-
\frac{\mathcal{N}_2}{\mathcal{D}_2}
q_\mu,\\
\frac{dU}{dt_{\rm on}}&=(\Gamma-1)q_\mu .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Branch 2 is a selectable legacy option for dynamics comparisons.  It is not the
default branch for the publication figures.

\subsubsection{Branch 3: mass-energy baseline}

Branch 3 uses the physical swept mass \(M\).  This makes the mass and energy
budget explicit.  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
X &= \Gamma^2-1,\\
\frac{dM}{dt_{\rm on}} &= \frac{dM}{dR}\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(X\) is a short name for the relativistic kinetic factor.  The Lorentz
factor equation is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{N}_3&=
-\frac{dM}{dR}c^2\Gamma X
(\hat{\gamma}\Gamma-\hat{\gamma}+1)
\\
&\quad
-(\hat{\gamma}X+1)\Gamma(1-\hat{\gamma})
\frac{3U}{R}
+\Gamma^2\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})
\left(\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}\right)^{-1},
\\
\mathcal{D}_3&=
\Gamma^2(M_0+M)c^2
+(\hat{\gamma}^2X+3\hat{\gamma}-2)U .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}}=
\frac{\mathcal{N}_3}{\mathcal{D}_3}\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}.
\end{equation}
The three terms in \(\mathcal{N}_3\) have direct meanings.  The first is the
inertia of newly swept-up mass, the second is work done by the expanding shocked
gas, and the third is the optional injected luminosity.  These terms make branch
3 the default state for the forward-shock calculations.

The internal energy changes by shock heating, adiabatic expansion, and optional
energy injection.  The radial expansion and heating terms are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{E}_R &=
\frac{3}{R}
-\frac{1}{\Gamma}
\left(\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}\right)^{-1}
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}},\\
\mathcal{H}_R &=(1-\epsilon_{\rm eff})(\Gamma-1)
\frac{dM}{dR}c^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The energy equation is
\begin{equation}
\frac{dU}{dt_{\rm on}}=
\left[\mathcal{H}_R-(\hat{\gamma}-1)\mathcal{E}_R U\right]
\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}
+\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on}).
\end{equation}
This equation writes the local thermal budget that later sets electron injection
and the magnetic field.

\subsection{Forward-dynamics integration}

The forward-dynamics integrator turns the branch equations into a stored radial
state.  It reads the public model, fixes the density profile, and integrates
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}=(\Gamma,Y_2,U,R)
\end{equation}
on the \(t_{\rm on}\) grid.  The entry \(Y_2\) is \(\mu\) for branches 1 and 2,
and \(M\) for branch 3.  After each accepted step, the public arrays are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t_{{\rm obs},i}&=(1+z)t_{{\rm on},i},\\
\Gamma_i&=Y_{\Gamma,i},\qquad R_i=Y_{R,i},\\
M_{{\rm sw},i}&=
\begin{cases}
4\pi m_pY_{2,i},
& \text{branches 1 and 2},\\
Y_{2,i},
& \text{branch 3}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These arrays are the dynamics output read by the electron, photon, reverse-shock,
hadronic, and observer modules.  The runtime container and grid-start details are
given in Appendix~\ref{app:projection-runtime}.

The numerical update uses a fourth-order Runge--Kutta step.  For
\(t_{{\rm on},i-1}>0\), the integrator changes variables from \(t_{\rm on}\) to
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
s=\ln(t_{\rm on}/t_{{\rm on},i-1}),
\frac{d\mathbf{y}}{ds}
=t_{\rm on}\frac{d\mathbf{y}}{dt_{\rm on}}
=t_{\rm on}F(t_{\rm on},\mathbf{y}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(F\equiv d\mathbf{y}/dt_{\rm on}\) is the selected dynamics branch.  The
first interval, where \(t_{{\rm on},i-1}=0\), is advanced directly in linear
\(t_{\rm on}\).  This update preserves the radius-ordered state used by later
modules.

The adaptive check compares two integrations of the same interval.  Let
\(\mathbf{y}^{\rm f}\) be the current \(m\)-substep trial and
\(\mathbf{y}^{\rm c}\) the previous \(m/2\)-substep comparison solution.  The
relative error over positive state entries is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\rm RK}^\ast=
\max_{a\in\mathcal{I}_y}
\frac{2|y_a^{\rm f}-y_a^{\rm c}|}
{y_a^{\rm f}+y_a^{\rm c}},
\qquad
\mathcal{I}_y\equiv\{a\,|\,y_a^{\rm f}+y_a^{\rm c}>0\}.
\end{equation}
The subdivision is refined until \(\epsilon_{\rm RK}^\ast<\epsilon_{\rm RK}\).
Each trial must also keep \(\Gamma>1\).  The tolerance \(\epsilon_{\rm RK}\) is
the fixed RK accuracy threshold.  These checks define the relativistic
blast-wave state domain used by the integrator.  A trial outside this domain is
not an accepted radius step.

\subsection{Python binding boundary}

The Python dynamics namespace is only a binding layer.  It exposes the compiled
forward and reverse dynamics kernels to higher-level orchestration.  It does not
add a blast-wave equation, a shock jump condition, or an integration step.  The
physical equations are the compiled forward dynamics described above and the
reverse-shock equations in Section~\ref{sec:reverse-shock}.

\section{Particle and Radiation Transport} \label{sec:electron}

The particle and radiation layer follows the material that produces the
afterglow spectrum.  At each radius, the shock injects nonthermal electrons.
Those electrons cool by synchrotron, inverse-Compton, and adiabatic losses, and
their radiation builds the photon field used by SSC, absorption, and optional
feedback branches.  The one-dimensional solvers return \(N_e(\gamma,R)\),
synchrotron luminosity, seed-photon density, and the break frequencies
\(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and \(\nu_a\).  The two-dimensional path adds downstream
\(\chi\)-resolved electron and photon fields.  The solver choice changes the
discretization, not the physical injection law
\citep{LeVeque2002,JiangShu1996,HesthavenWarburton2008}.

\subsection{Shared electron state, sources, and coordinates}

\subsubsection{Namespace boundary}

The Python electron namespace is only a binding layer.  It selects one compiled
electron module and exposes it to the public API.  It does not add an injection
law, a cooling term, an emissivity formula, or a transport equation.  Unsupported
historical names are absent rather than redirected to another solver.

\subsubsection{Continuum state and solver storage}

The forward-electron state evolves the electron spectrum as the shock moves
outward.  All forward electron solvers start from the same continuity equation,
with radius as the independent variable:
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial N_e}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}
\left(g_RN_e\right)
=Q_e+Q_e^{\rm sec},
\end{equation}
where \(N_e(\gamma,R)\) denotes the differential electron number density,
\(g_R=d\gamma/dR\), and \(Q_e\) is the primary shock source.  The drift \(g_R\)
contains radiative and adiabatic cooling.  The secondary source
\(Q_e^{\rm sec}\) is zero in a separated leptonic calculation.  It is supplied
only when the hadronic-feedback branch feeds Bethe-Heitler, \(pp\), or
\(\gamma\gamma\) secondaries back into the electron equation.

The default implicit solver stores the spectrum on the natural-log energy grid.
With \(x=\ln\gamma\), the cell density is
\begin{equation}
N_x\equiv\frac{dN_e}{dx}=\gamma N_e,
\qquad x=\ln\gamma .
\end{equation}
This is the physical density per \(x\)-bin.  Because \(x\) is a natural
logarithm, no \(\ln 10\) Jacobian appears.  Some kernels store the same particles
in a log-four-velocity coordinate.  The coordinate Jacobian is applied when the
initial condition and sources are projected onto that storage grid.  The
representative implicit shell update is
\begin{equation}
\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}_i\right)\mathbf{N}_{i+1}
=\mathbf{N}_{i}+\Delta R_i\,\mathbf{Q}_{i},
\end{equation}
where the matrix \(\mathbf{A}_i\) is assembled from face drift coefficients.  A
high-to-low-energy sweep solves the implicit step.  The source term is
integrated over cell edges, so the update evolves cell averages, not point
samples.

\subsubsection{Finite-shell source and injection scales}

Electron injection uses the same density \(n(R)\) as the dynamics.  No separate
electron-density law is introduced.  The shell volume is
\begin{equation}
V(R)=\frac{4\pi R^3}{3}.
\end{equation}
The number of swept electrons before the first active radius is
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm init}=\int_0^{R_{\rm ini}}4\pi R^2 n(R)\,dR .
\end{equation}
Thus \(N_{\rm init}=n_{\rm ISM}V(R_{\rm ini})\) for the uniform branch.  For
analytic wind and capped-wind branches the same integral is evaluated over the
corresponding piecewise intervals of \(n(R)\).  A finite shell beginning at
\(R_s\) contributes the radial source
\begin{equation}
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)
\equiv
n(R_s)\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R}.
\end{equation}
This is the number of upstream particles swept per unit radius, evaluated over a
finite shell.  Multiplication by the cell width gives the particle count injected
by that shell.

The injection energy range is set by the minimum and maximum electron Lorentz
factors.  The maximum acceleration energy follows from balancing acceleration
with synchrotron loss:
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{\max}=\frac{3m_ec^2}{\left(8e^3B'\right)^{1/2}} .
\end{equation}
The source is not cut below \(\gamma_{\max}\).  Above \(\gamma_{\max}\), it is
exponentially suppressed:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma\le\gamma_{\max},\\
\exp(1-\gamma/\gamma_{\max}), & \gamma>\gamma_{\max}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}

The injection minimum depends on the electron index \(p\).  For \(p>2\),
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m=1+\frac{p-2}{p-1}
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma-1).
\end{equation}
For \(1<p<2\),
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m=
\left[
\frac{2-p}{p-1}
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma-1)
\gamma_{\max}^{p-2}
\right]^{1/(p-1)}+1.
\end{equation}
For \(p=2\), the normalization is logarithmic:
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m\ln\left(\frac{\gamma_{\max}}{\gamma_m}\right)
=\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma-1).
\end{equation}
The implemented injection model therefore uses the correct branch for \(p\); it
does not apply the \(p>2\) expression outside its domain.

The forward-shock source is a power law with the high-energy cutoff above.  The
normalization over the active support is
\begin{equation}
a_p=f_e\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}-\gamma_m^{1-p}} .
\end{equation}
The local source amplitude is \(A_R\equiv q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_p\).  In a
natural-log gamma cell, the source shape is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_x(x)
&=\exp[(1-p)x]
\mathcal{C}_{\max}[\exp(x)]H[\exp(x)-\gamma_m],\\
Q_{x,j}
&=A_R\,\mathcal I_{x,j},\\
\mathcal I_{x,j}
&=\frac{1}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
S_x(x)\,dx .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(H\) is the Heaviside function.  The output \(Q_{x,j}\) is the injected
cell-averaged source used by the electron transport solver.

\subsubsection{Initial spectrum on the shared grid}

The initial electron spectrum is placed on a natural-log energy grid.  The grid
points are
\begin{equation}
\Delta_g\equiv \frac{\ln(\gamma_H/\gamma_L)}{N_\gamma-1},
\qquad
\gamma_i=\gamma_L\exp[(i-1)\Delta_g],
\end{equation}
where \(\gamma_L\) and \(\gamma_H\) are fixed grid bounds, and \(\gamma_H\) is
chosen from the initial acceleration cutoff.  All solvers start from the same
physical spectrum; only the storage coordinate can change.

For a power-law segment, conversion to the log-gamma density is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma}
&=A\gamma^{-s}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),\\
\frac{dN_e}{dx}=A\gamma^{1-s}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
x&=\ln\gamma .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The initial spectrum depends on the order of \(\gamma_c\) and \(\gamma_m\).  Let
\(N_0\equiv N_{\rm init}\).  In the fast-cooling case, \(\gamma_c<\gamma_m\), the
spectrum is
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
A_{\rm f}\equiv N_0\gamma_c,\\
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma}=
\begin{cases}
A_{\rm f}\gamma^{-2}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma_c\le\gamma<\gamma_m,\\
A_{\rm f}\gamma_m^{p-1}\gamma^{-p-1}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma\ge\gamma_m,\\
0, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
In the slow-cooling case, \(\gamma_m\le\gamma_c\), the spectrum is
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
A_{\rm s}\equiv N_0(p-1)\gamma_m^{p-1},\\
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma}=
\begin{cases}
A_{\rm s}\gamma^{-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma_m\le\gamma<\gamma_c,\\
A_{\rm s}\gamma_c\gamma^{-p-1}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma\ge\gamma_c,\\
0, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Point-value solvers sample these spectra at \(\gamma_i\).  Conservative solvers
store the cell average instead:
\begin{equation}
N_{x,j}^{\rm init}=\frac{1}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\frac{dN_e}{dx}\,dx .
\end{equation}
with the integral split at \(\gamma_m\), \(\gamma_c\), and \(\gamma_{\max}\).  If
a solver stores a different coordinate, the same physical spectrum is transformed
only by the coordinate Jacobian.  The quadrature and coordinate-map formulas are
given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsubsection{Coordinate projection of shell sources}

The forward-shock source is projected into whatever coordinate a solver stores.
Only the coordinate measure changes.  In a general storage coordinate \(y\), the
cell source is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_y(y)&=S_x[x(y)]\,\frac{dx}{dy},\\
Q_{y,j}
&=A_R\,\mathcal I_{y,j},\\
\mathcal I_{y,j}
&=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}
S_y(y)\,dy .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus each solver receives the same injected physical distribution, even when it
stores the distribution in a different coordinate.

\subsubsection{Reverse-shock kinetic source}

Reverse-shock electrons use a kinetic-energy power law.  This is different from
the forward-shock source, which is written as a power law in \(\gamma\).  The
active part of the reverse-shock source is selected by
\begin{equation}
I_m(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma>\gamma_m,\\
0, & \gamma\le\gamma_m .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
In the log-gamma coordinate, the unnormalized density is
\begin{equation}
\Psi_x(\gamma)=\gamma(\gamma-1)^{-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma)I_m(\gamma).
\end{equation}
The cell averages \(\bar\Psi_{x,j}\) are then normalized to the total source
count \(Q\):
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm kin}=Q\,
\frac{\bar\Psi_{x,j}}
{\sum_b \bar\Psi_{x,b}\Delta x_b}.
\end{equation}
In a four-velocity coordinate, the same physical source becomes
\begin{equation}
\Psi_y(y)=\gamma(y)\frac{dx}{dy}
[\gamma(y)-1]^{-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}[\gamma(y)]I_m[\gamma(y)],
\end{equation}
and is normalized in the same way.  The source must have a non-empty active
support.  If no active cell exists, the source is outside its domain; the code
does not add an artificial floor.

\subsubsection{Thermal electron component}

The optional thermal component adds a Maxwell--Juttner electron population.  Its
total electron number is \(N_{\rm th}\).  The shock-front four-speed \(u\) sets
the dimensionless temperature:
\begin{equation}
\Theta(u)=
\frac{u^2(1+a_{\rm MJ}u)}{3(1+u+a_{\rm MJ}u^2)} .
\end{equation}
Here \(a_{\rm MJ}\) is the fixed coefficient in the temperature fit.  The
continuous Maxwell--Juttner shape is
\begin{equation}
\phi_\gamma(\gamma;\Theta)=
\frac{\gamma^2\beta_\gamma e^{-\gamma/\Theta}}
{\Theta K_2(1/\Theta)},
\qquad
\beta_\gamma=(1-\gamma^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
The calculation uses a finite electron grid, so the shape is normalized on that
grid:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{N}_{\rm th}=
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_\gamma-1}
[\phi_\gamma(\gamma_j)+\phi_\gamma(\gamma_{j+1})]
(\gamma_{j+1}-\gamma_j).
\end{equation}
The log-gamma density added to the grid is then
\begin{equation}
\phi_x(\gamma_j)=
\frac{\phi_\gamma(\gamma_j)}{\mathcal{N}_{\rm th}}\,
\gamma_j .
\end{equation}
For a source update, the added log-coordinate source is \(N_{\rm th}\phi_x\).  For
an initial centered population, the added physical energy density is
\(N_{\rm th}\phi_x/\gamma\).  This branch requires \(\Theta>0\), grid points with
\(\gamma_e>1\), and a non-zero thermal normalization.

The shared energy-coordinate maps are storage maps, not new electron physics.
The reference coordinate is \(x_g=\ln\gamma_e\).  Solvers that use a
four-velocity-like coordinate apply the same Jacobian to initial spectra, shell
sources, and cooling face displacements.  The map requires \(\gamma_s>1\) and
\(\gamma_{\max}>\gamma_{\min}\), and its explicit form is given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsection{Shared conservative shell-transport primitives}

The shared transport layer evolves electron cell averages through injection and
cooling.  The one-dimensional path chooses one of two solver families before the
update starts:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{S}\in\{\texttt{fullhide},\texttt{dg}\}.
\end{equation}
The default choice is \texttt{fullhide}.  This selector changes only the
numerical representation.  The physical source, cooling, and adiabatic terms are
assembled before the choice is made.

The finite-volume representative update evolves cell averages.  In a storage
coordinate \(y\), the cell average is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_{y,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dy}\,dy ,
\\
\Delta y_j=y_{j+1/2}-y_{j-1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The particles move through the energy grid because they cool.  Let \(D_j\) be the
radiative part of \(d\ln\gamma_e/dR\) at a node, and let \(D_{\rm ad}\) be the
adiabatic part.  The face speed in the storage coordinate is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{V}_{j+1/2}=
\frac{(D_j+D_{j+1})/2+D_{\rm ad}}
{(dx_g/dy)_{j+1/2}} .
\end{equation}
The numerator is the physical energy drift.  The denominator converts that drift
from \(\ln\gamma_e\) to the stored coordinate \(y\).

The face flux uses the sign of this speed:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}^{n+1}
=\mathcal{V}_{j+1/2}^{+}N_{j+1}^{n+1}
+\mathcal{V}_{j+1/2}^{-}N_j^{n+1}.
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{V}^{+}=\max(\mathcal{V},0)\) and
\(\mathcal{V}^{-}=\min(\mathcal{V},0)\).  The conservative update is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}=N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_j
+\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta y_j}
\left(\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}^{n+1}
-\mathcal{F}_{j-1/2}^{n+1}\right).
\end{equation}
The low-energy face is closed.  Cooled particles therefore remain in the first
cell instead of leaving the finite shell population.  The final positive
projection \(\Pi_+(X)=\max(X,0)\) acts only on solved cell averages.  It does not
mix neighboring bins.  The tridiagonal and triangular sweep details are listed in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

The cooling-break diagnostic finds the energy where cooling and expansion have
the same local scale.  It solves
\begin{equation}
\bar{\Lambda}(\gamma_c)=\frac{1}{R},
\end{equation}
where \(\bar{\Lambda}\) is the face-averaged loss coefficient.  The source scan
uses the same natural-log edge grid and searches the finite support
\(\ln\gamma_m\le x\le \ln(\chi_{\rm sup}\gamma_{\max})\).  Here
\(\chi_{\rm sup}\) is a numerical support parameter, not a second physical
cutoff.  The physical cutoff remains \(\gamma_{\max}\).

The adaptive substep diagnostic compares one full step with two half steps over
the active cell support \(\mathcal{A}\):
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{A}\equiv
\left\{j\,\middle|\,N^{\rm h}_j>0\right\},
\qquad
\epsilon_{\rm step}=
\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}
\frac{|N^{\rm h}_j-N^{\rm f}_j|}
{|N^{\rm h}_j|}.
\end{equation}
where \(N^{\rm f}\) is the full-step solution and \(N^{\rm h}\) is the
two-half-step solution.  A substep is accepted when
\(\epsilon_{\rm step}\le\epsilon_{\rm tol}\).  The tolerance
\(\epsilon_{\rm tol}\) is a numerical accuracy setting, not a microphysical
parameter.  Diagnostic reconstruction, remap interpolation, and positive-source
reconstruction are technical helpers; their formulas are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsection{Characteristic remap primitive}

\subsubsection{Affine characteristic traceback}

The characteristic solver follows the energy from which an electron came before
it cooled.  It uses \(u=1/\gamma_e=\exp(-x)\), because synchrotron cooling is
simple in this variable.  For a global affine cooling law
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a+bu,
\end{equation}
a point that arrives at \(u\) after radial lag \(\ell\) came from
\begin{equation}
u_{\rm back}=
\begin{cases}
u-a\ell, & b=0,\\
(u+a/b)e^{-b\ell}-a/b, & b\ne0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Here \(a\) is the energy-independent part of the drift and \(b\) is the part
proportional to \(u\).  For a piecewise cooling curve, the code writes
\begin{equation}
w(u)=uD(u),\qquad
w(u)=a_r+s_ru\quad {\rm in\ cell}\ r,
\end{equation}
where \(D(u)\) is the supplied loss rate and \(a_r,s_r\) are the linear
coefficients in cell \(r\).  The traced equation in that cell is
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_r+(s_r+b_{\rm ad})u .
\end{equation}
Here \(b_{\rm ad}\) is the adiabatic cooling coefficient.  The same inverse is
used inside one \(u\) cell.  If the traced point reaches a face, the remaining
radial lag continues in the next cell.  With traced arrival faces
\(x_{j\pm1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)\), the conservative remap is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_j[M;\ell]\equiv
\frac{
M[x_{j+1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
-M[x_{j-1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
}{\Delta x_j}.
\end{equation}
This expression means that the new content of cell \(j\) equals the old content
between the two traced faces, divided by the cell width.

A source-coupled remap adds newly injected electrons while old electrons cool
along their characteristics.  A representative update is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}
=\mathcal R_j[M_N;\Delta R]
+\Delta R\,s_Q
\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}}w_m\mathcal R_j[M_Q;s_m\Delta R].
\end{equation}
Here \(M_N\) is the PPM prefix of the old population, \(M_Q\) is the
positive-source prefix, and \(s_Q\) is the source normalization for the current
radial step.  The source-free remap is the same formula with \(s_Q=0\) and one
traced boundary set.  The quadrature weights, semi-Lagrangian split, flux-split
matrix, and centralized support diagnostics are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsection{Implicit finite-volume \texttt{fullhide\_1d} solver}

The \texttt{fullhide\_1d} solver follows the forward-shock electron population on
a finite-volume energy grid.  Each cell stores the number of electrons per cell
width, so the scheme can move electrons between cells without losing the total
number.  The nonthermal branch uses the four-velocity coordinate \(y\) and stores
\begin{equation}
N_{y,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dy}\,dy .
\label{eq:fullhide-cell-average}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{y,j}\) is the cell average, \(y_{j\pm1/2}\) are the cell faces, and
\(\Delta y_j\) is the cell width.  The solver evolves these averages as the shock
moves from one radius to the next.

\subsubsection{Local shell state}

Each radial shell supplies the shock quantities used by the electron solver.  For
shell \(i\), the local state is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{s,i}&=R_{i-1},\\
\bar\Gamma_i&=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2},\\
n_i&=n(R_{s,i}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(R_{s,i}\) is the source radius, \(\bar\Gamma_i\) is the shell-averaged
Lorentz factor, and \(n_i\) is the upstream density.  These quantities set
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_i'&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n_i m_pc^2
\bar\Gamma_i(\bar\Gamma_i-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max,i}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B_i'e^3)^{1/2}},\\
a_{c,i}&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B_i'^2\bar\Gamma_i t_{{\rm obs},i}}
{6\pi m_ec(1+z)},\\
\gamma_{c,i}&=a_{c,i}^{-1},\\
\beta_i&=(1-\bar\Gamma_i^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B_i'\) sets synchrotron losses, \(\gamma_{\max,i}\) sets the high-energy
injection cutoff, \(\gamma_{c,i}\) is the diagnostic cooling Lorentz factor, and
\(\beta_i c\) sets radial cooling rates.  This shell state is the output passed
to the implicit electron update.

\subsubsection{Fixed radial substeps}

The finite-volume solve may split one radial shell into smaller radial substeps
when cooling is fast.  The step estimate compares the fastest synchrotron drift
with the adiabatic drift,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{m,i}&=\frac{R_i+R_{i-1}}{2},\\
a_i&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B_i'^2}
{6\pi m_ec^2\beta_i\bar\Gamma_i},\\
\Delta R_0&=\frac{\eta_{\rm fh}}
{a_i\gamma_{\max,i}+\eta_{\rm ad}/R_{m,i}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a_i\gamma_{\max,i}\) estimates the largest synchrotron drift and
\(\eta_{\rm ad}/R_{m,i}\) estimates the adiabatic drift.  The shell width and
public substep bounds then set the number of fixed substeps.  The exact grid
choice is given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsubsection{Implicit finite-volume update}

One implicit substep treats cooling as flux through the faces of each energy
cell.  On face \(j+1/2\), the coordinate speed is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar D_{j+1/2}&=\frac{D_j+D_{j+1}}{2},\\
v_{j+1/2}&=
\frac{\bar D_{j+1/2}+R^{-1}}
{(dx_g/dy)_{j+1/2}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_j\) is the radiative loss coefficient in cell \(j\).  Positive \(v\)
means motion toward lower electron energy.  With a closed low-energy boundary,
the conservative update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{n+1}&=N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_j
+G_{j+1/2}-G_{j-1/2},\\
G_{j+1/2}&=\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta y_j}
\left(v_{j+1/2}^{+}N_{j+1}^{n+1}
+v_{j+1/2}^{-}N_j^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(v^+=\max(v,0)\) and \(v^-=\min(v,0)\).  When the low boundary is closed,
the update does not introduce a ghost-cell population below the first active
face.  The high-energy loss flux is solved at the new radius instead of being
applied explicitly.  This is the representative implicit update used by the
main text.

\subsubsection{Finite-shell source normalization}

Electron injection is normalized by the number of upstream particles swept by a
finite radial shell.  It is therefore a source per unit radius, not a time rate.
For a substep that begins at radius \(R_s\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
V(R)&=\frac{4\pi R^3}{3},\\
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)&\equiv
n(R_s)\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},\\
a_p(R)&\equiv
f_e\,\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}(R)-\gamma_m^{1-p}(R)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(V(R)\) is the spherical volume, \(q_R\) is the number of upstream electrons
swept per unit radius over the finite shell, and \(a_p\) normalizes the injected
power law.  In the thin-shell limit, \(q_R\) approaches \(4\pi R_s^2n(R_s)\).
For a uniform medium without thermal electrons, the fixed substeps can be merged
into one implicit solve.  For non-uniform density or thermal electrons, the
solver re-evaluates the local density, field, and injection scales on each
substep.  These two execution paths have the same physical source and cooling
terms; their detailed formulas are given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsubsection{Adaptive fullhide substeps}

Adaptive substeps control the radial integration error in the electron spectrum.
For one trial \(\Delta R\), the driver compares one full implicit step with two
half implicit steps.  Let \(N_j^{\rm f}\) and \(N_j^{\rm h}\) be these two
solutions.  The relative difference is measured on non-empty cells:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{I}\equiv
\left\{j\,\middle|\,N_j^{\rm h}>0\right\},
\qquad
\epsilon_{\rm adapt}=
\max_{j\in\mathcal{I}}
\frac{|N_j^{\rm h}-N_j^{\rm f}|}
{N_j^{\rm h}} .
\end{equation}
The accepted state is \(N_j^{\rm h}\), because it uses the smaller radial step.
Empty cells do not set the relative-error scale.  If
\(\epsilon_{\rm adapt}\le\epsilon_{\rm tol}\), the trial step is accepted.  If the
error is larger, the trial step is reduced down to
\((R_i-R_{i-1})/N_{\rm sub,max}\).  Here \(\epsilon_{\rm tol}\) and
\(N_{\rm sub,max}\) are the public electron-substep tolerance and maximum substep
count.  The controller changes only the radial step size; it does not modify the
electron spectrum after the solve.

\subsection{Coupled \texttt{fullhide} electron--photon pass}

\subsubsection{Coupled input state}

The coupled electron--photon pass updates the electron spectrum when photons
also control the cooling or add secondary pairs.  The electron coordinate is
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  At each fixed radial substep, the pass receives the cooling
photon density \(n_\nu^{\rm cool}\) and the secondary pair source
\(Q_{x,j}^{\rm sec}\).  The photon density sets the inverse-Compton cooling
coefficient, while synchrotron self-absorption heating uses the local
synchrotron seed field.  The electron source in one substep is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}=Q_{x,j}^{\rm inj}+Q_{x,j}^{\rm sec},
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm inj}\) is the primary shell source on the same finite
radial shell.  The output of this stage is still a local electron spectrum; the
observer projection is unchanged.

\subsubsection{Coupled triangular solve}

The coupled numerical step uses the same conservative sweep as the separated
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} step.  The only changed quantities are the cooling drift
and the source on the right-hand side.  For one substep, the face drift and
signed step factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
w_{j+1/2}&=\bar D_{j+1/2}+\frac{1}{R},\\
\lambda_{j+1/2}&=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}w_{j+1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\bar D_{j+1/2}\) is the face-averaged radiative cooling coefficient, and
\(1/R\) is the adiabatic drift in log energy.  The high-to-low sweep is the one
defined in Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}; the coupled diagonal and
right-hand-side details are given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The
step output is the cooled electron spectrum after the supplied photon field has
acted on that radial shell.

\subsubsection{Coupled output refresh}

The coupled pass refreshes radiation diagnostics from the final electron
spectrum.  At the last output radius, the code re-evaluates the same synchrotron
optical-depth grid used by the separated \texttt{fullhide} path.  The returned
\(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and \(\nu_a\) arrays therefore describe the coupled state
after electron cooling and secondary injection have been applied.

\subsection{Hybrid thermal \texttt{fullhide} branch}

\subsubsection{Hybrid coordinate and state}

The \texttt{fullhide\_1d\_hz} branch follows the same shell state and the same
finite-volume update as \texttt{fullhide\_1d}.  Its difference is the source
shape when \texttt{thermal\_electrons} is enabled.  It evolves the same cell
average \(N_{y,j}\) defined in Equation~\eqref{eq:fullhide-cell-average}.  The
coordinate is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\varpi_e&=\gamma_e^2-1,\qquad s=\gamma_s^2-1,\\
y&=\ln\!\left(1+\frac{\varpi_e}{s}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(s\) sets the transition scale of the hybrid coordinate.  Radiation calls
still use \(\gamma_e\), but the transported content is stored in \(y\).  Since
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_e(y)&=\left[1+s(\exp(y)-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\frac{dx}{dy}&=\frac{s\,\exp(y)}{2\gamma_e^2},\\
\frac{dN_e}{dy}=\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\gamma_e\frac{dx}{dy}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Hybrid source cells}

The hybrid branch uses the same finite-shell factor \(q_R(R_s,\Delta R)\) to
normalize new electrons.  If thermal electrons are disabled, the source is the
ordinary nonthermal shape written on the \(y\) grid:
\begin{equation}
Q_{y,j}^{\rm S}
=\frac{q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_p(R_s)}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\phi_y(R_s,y)\,dy .
\end{equation}
If thermal electrons are enabled, the same swept electrons are distributed over a
unit-normalized hybrid density \(\phi_{\rm h}\):
\begin{equation}
Q_{y,j}^{\rm h}=
\frac{q_R(R_s,\Delta R)}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}
\phi_{\rm h}(\gamma_e)\,
\gamma_e\frac{dx}{dy}\,dy .
\end{equation}
The Jacobian \(\gamma_e\,dx/dy\) converts the physical density in
\(\gamma_e\) into the transported \(y\) coordinate.

\subsubsection{Hybrid density}

The hybrid density puts newly swept electrons into two parts.  A fraction
\(1-f_e\) is thermal below \(\gamma_m\), and a fraction \(f_e\) is a cutoff
power law above \(\gamma_m\):
\begin{equation}
\phi_{\rm h}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
(1-f_e)\phi_{\rm th}(\gamma), & 1<\gamma<\gamma_m,\\
f_e\phi_{\rm pl}(\gamma), & \gamma\ge\gamma_m .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The two normalized pieces are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\phi_{\rm th}(\gamma)&=
\frac{\gamma\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}\exp(-\gamma/\Theta)}
{I_{\rm th}},\\
\phi_{\rm pl}(\gamma)&=
\frac{\gamma^{-p}\exp(-\gamma/\gamma_{\max})}{I_{\rm pl}},\\
I_{\rm th}(\gamma_m,\Theta)&=
\int_1^{\gamma_m}\gamma\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}
\exp(-\gamma/\Theta)\,d\gamma,\\
I_{\rm pl}(\gamma_m,\gamma_{\max},p)&=
\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}\Gamma\!\left(1-p,\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_{\max}}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(I_{\rm th}\) normalizes the thermal part below \(\gamma_m\), and
\(I_{\rm pl}\) normalizes the nonthermal tail above \(\gamma_m\).  The temperature
is chosen so that the two pieces meet continuously at \(\gamma_m\).

\subsubsection{Temperature continuity solve}

The dimensionless temperature \(\Theta\) is fixed by continuity, not by tuning.
At the joining Lorentz factor \(\gamma_m\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
(1-f_e)\phi_{\rm th}(\gamma_m)
=
f_e\phi_{\rm pl}(\gamma_m).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This condition is solved through the scalar equation
\begin{equation}
F(\Theta)=\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta}+\ln I_{\rm th}-\ln C=0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\ln C=
\ln\!\left[\left(\frac{1}{f_e}-1\right)
\sqrt{\gamma_m^2-1}\right]
+(1+p)\ln\gamma_m\\
+\ln I_{\rm pl}+\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_{\max}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The scalar root update uses the analytic derivative
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
I_2(\gamma_m,\Theta)&=
\int_1^{\gamma_m}\gamma^2\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}
\exp(-\gamma/\Theta)\,d\gamma,\\
\frac{dF}{d\Theta}&=
-\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta^2}
+\frac{I_2}{\Theta^2I_{\rm th}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
\Theta_{r+1}=\Theta_r-\frac{F(\Theta_r)}
{\left.dF/d\Theta\right|_{\Theta_r}} .
\end{equation}
The solved \(\Theta\) sets the thermal width of the hybrid source.  The branch
requires a positive solution of this equation; otherwise the thermal-electron
source is not an accepted local state.

\subsubsection{Hybrid output}

The hybrid branch advances the assembled source with the same implicit
finite-volume system as \texttt{fullhide\_1d}.  Special-function evaluation,
thermal-tail moment subtraction, and root-seeding details are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The final output radius is evaluated from
the final electron spectrum and the same synchrotron optical-depth grid used for
the active shells.

\subsection{Semi-Lagrangian log-\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\)}{gamma} solver}

\subsubsection{SLC state and radial substeps}

The \texttt{slc1\_1d} branch solves the same electron cooling problem with a
semi-Lagrangian remap.  It uses the uniform coordinate
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\) and stores the conservative cell averages \(N_{x,j}\).
The local \(B'\), \(\gamma_m\), \(\gamma_{\max}\), \(\gamma_c\), synchrotron
state, and cooling array are the same as in the log-\(\gamma\) \texttt{fullhide}
path.  The shell is divided into
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_i&=R_i-R_{i-1},\\
L_i&=\max\!\left[
N_{\rm S,min},
\min\!\left(N_{\rm S,max},
\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta R_i}{\Delta R_{\rm S}}\right\rfloor\right)
\right],\\
\Delta R&=\frac{\Delta R_i}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_i\) is the number of radial substeps, \(\Delta R\) is the substep width,
and \(\Delta R_{\rm S}\), \(N_{\rm S,min}\), and \(N_{\rm S,max}\) are fixed
transport controls.

\subsubsection{SLC source and cooling speed}

Each semi-Lagrangian substep needs a source and a cooling drift.  For a substep
from \(R_L\) to \(R_R=R_L+\Delta R\), non-uniform media are sampled at
\begin{equation}
R_m=\frac{R_L+R_R}{2},\qquad n_m=n(R_m),\qquad n_R=n(R_R),
\end{equation}
while a uniform medium uses the shell density.  The injection source in
log-\(\gamma\) cell \(j\) is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}=
\frac{q_R(R_m,\Delta R)a_p(R_m)}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_x(x)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\psi_x(x)&=\gamma_e^{1-p}\mathcal C_{\max}(\gamma_e),\\
\gamma_e&=\exp(x) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\psi_x\) is the nonthermal source shape per unit \(x\).  The cooling speed
at the cell face is the shell cooling array scaled to the midpoint density:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
f_{j+1/2,m}=
\frac{D_{j,m}+D_{j+1,m}}{2}+\frac{1}{R_m},\\
D_{j,m}=D_{j,i-1}\frac{n_m}{n_{i-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{SLC source-split remap}

The semi-Lagrangian update treats cooling as motion across the log-energy grid.
One substep adds half of \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}\), traces cell faces backward along the
cooling characteristics, remaps the conserved content between the two departure
faces, and then adds the remaining half of the source.  The detailed prefix
integral and characteristic-face formulas are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The output of the substep is a new
conservative log-\(\gamma\) spectrum \(N_{x,j}^{n+1}\).

\subsubsection{SLC output diagnostics}

After the last substep, the code converts the transported \(dN_e/dx\) cells to a
centered \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) spectrum with the same conservative exponential
cell-shape conversion used by the other solvers.  The \(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and
\(\nu_a\) arrays are shell-left diagnostics for the active output slots
\(1,\ldots,N_R-1\).  The self-absorption frequency \(\nu_a\) comes from the same
synchrotron optical-depth grid used for the emitted spectrum.

\subsection{Three-level log-\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\)}{gamma} solver}

\subsubsection{t2g state and source}

The \texttt{t2g1\_1d} branch keeps the same log-\(\gamma\) state, source shape,
and shell-left diagnostics as \texttt{slc1\_1d}.  Its difference is the radial
discretization: it uses a three-level implicit upwind solve instead of a
semi-Lagrangian remap.  Each output shell uses the same fixed substep count
\(L_i\), and each substep advances the source radius to
\(R_\ell=R_{\ell-1}+\Delta R\).  The injection source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm T}=
\frac{q_R(R_\ell,\Delta R)a_p(R_\ell)}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_x(x)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
where \(q_R\) and \(a_p\) are evaluated at \(R_\ell\).

\subsubsection{t2g cooling speed}

The three-level solver uses the same density-scaled cooling speed as
\texttt{slc1\_1d}, evaluated at \(R_\ell\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
f_{j+1/2,\ell}=
\frac{D_{j,\ell}+D_{j+1,\ell}}{2}+\frac{1}{R_\ell},\\
D_{j,\ell}=D_{j,i-1}\frac{n(R_\ell)}{n_{i-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The signed face step factor is
\begin{equation}
\lambda_{j+1/2}=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}f_{j+1/2,\ell}.
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{t2g startup sweep}

The first substeps do not yet have two previous spectra.  The branch therefore
starts with backward-Euler sweeps using the same high-to-low triangular primitive
as the fixed-grid \texttt{fullhide} update.  The output of this startup stage is
the pair of history spectra needed by the second-order radial solve.

\subsubsection{t2g BDF2 sweep}

The three-level radial solve uses a second-order BDF2 balance after two history
states are available:
\begin{equation}
\frac{3N_j^{n+1}-4N_j^n+N_j^{n-1}}{2\Delta R}
+\mathcal{L}_j(N^{n+1})=Q_{x,j}^{\rm T},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{L}\) is the one-sided cooling-advection operator encoded by the
face coefficients.  Both the startup and BDF2 phases use the shared high-to-low
sweep in Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}.  The corresponding
diagonal, right-hand-side, and coupling coefficients are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The step output is the updated
log-\(\gamma\) spectrum and its shifted one-step history.

\subsubsection{t2g output diagnostics}

After each accepted substep, the pair \((N^n,N^{n-1})\) is shifted forward.  At
the final substep of each output shell, the code converts \(dN_e/dx\) to a
centered \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) spectrum with the same conservative exponential
cell-shape reconstruction used by the other log-\(\gamma\) drivers.

\subsection{WENO finite-volume solver}

\subsubsection{WENO state and local shock scales}

The \texttt{weno5\_1d} branch moves the electron spectrum with an explicit
finite-volume flux.  It uses the same uniform log-\(\gamma\) coordinate as the
other one-dimensional branches:
\begin{equation}
x=\ln\gamma_e,\qquad
N_{x,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta x}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dx}\,dx .
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{x,j}\) is the electron number per unit \(x\) in cell \(j\).  For the
output shell \(R_{i-1}\rightarrow R_i\), the local shock state is
\begin{equation}
R_{\rm loc}=R_{i-1},\qquad
\bar\Gamma_i=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2},
\end{equation}
where \(R_{\rm loc}\) is the shell-left radius and \(\bar\Gamma_i\) is the
shell-averaged Lorentz factor.  These quantities set the magnetic field, the
upper electron energy, the cooling break, and the shock speed:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B'_i&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n(R_{\rm loc})m_pc^2
\bar\Gamma_i(\bar\Gamma_i-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max,i}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B'_ie^3)^{1/2}},\\
a_{c,i}&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B_i'^2\bar\Gamma_i t_{{\rm obs},i}}
{6\pi m_ec(1+z)},\\
\gamma_{c,i}&=a_{c,i}^{-1},\\
\beta_i&=(1-\bar\Gamma_i^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The corresponding synchrotron cooling coefficient is
\begin{equation}
a_i\equiv \frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B_i'^2}{6\pi m_ec^2\beta_i\bar\Gamma_i}.
\end{equation}
Here \(a_i\gamma_e\) is the synchrotron energy drift per unit radius.  The local
cooling array stores the total radiative drift through
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\gamma_j}{dR}=-D_j\gamma_j .
\end{equation}
The explicit WENO update uses this drift as a face velocity.

\subsubsection{WENO conservative equation and substeps}

The WENO update solves the conservative log-energy equation with an explicit
face flux.  In continuum form,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial N_x}{\partial R}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(v_xN_x\right)+Q_x,\\
v_{x,j+1/2}
&=
\frac{D_j+D_{j+1}}{2}+R^{-1},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_x\) is the injected source per unit radius and \(v_x\) is the drift in
the log-energy coordinate.  The outer high-energy face is extrapolated from the
last interior cooling face.  The first trial radial step is set by the largest
synchrotron and adiabatic drift,
\begin{equation}
\Delta R_{W,0}=
\frac{\eta_W}
{a_i\gamma_{\max,i}+\eta_{\rm ad}/(R_i+R_{i-1})},
\end{equation}
where \(\eta_W\) and \(\eta_{\rm ad}\) are fixed WENO step controls.  The final
substep count also enforces an explicit flux limit.  With
\(v_W=\max_j|v_{x,j+1/2}|\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_i=\max\left[
\left\lfloor\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{\Delta R_{W,0}}\right\rfloor+N_W^+,\,
\left\lceil
\frac{(R_i-R_{i-1})v_W}
{\eta_{\rm CFL,W}\,\Delta x}
\right\rceil
\right],\\
\Delta R=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_W^+\) is the fixed extra-step margin and \(\eta_{\rm CFL,W}\) is the
WENO flux step control.

\subsubsection{WENO finite-shell source}

The explicit WENO source uses the same finite-shell swept-particle count as the
other electron solvers.  Inside a substep, the source radius is first advanced to
\(R_s+\Delta R\), and the density is sampled at that radius.  The injected
cell-average source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm W}=
\frac{q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_{p,i}}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_x(x)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)&\equiv
n(R_s)\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},\\
a_{p,i}&\equiv
f_e\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max,i}^{1-p}-\gamma_{m,i}^{1-p}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(q_R\) is the number of swept upstream electrons per unit radius, and
\(a_{p,i}\) normalizes the injected power law.  The volume is
\(V(R)=4\pi R^3/3\).  For this explicit branch, \(\gamma_m\) and
\(\gamma_{\max}\) stay fixed at the shell-level shock values during the output
shell.

\subsubsection{WENO-Z interface flux}

The WENO-Z step reconstructs the face flux from neighboring cell fluxes.  The
flux variable is \(F=v_xN_x\).  Because the equation contains
\(+\partial F/\partial x\), positive \(v_x\) moves particles toward lower \(x\).
Thus positive \(v_x\) uses the right-biased stencil, and negative \(v_x\) uses
the left-biased stencil.  For either bias, the face flux is
\begin{equation}
F_{i+1/2}=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\omega_k\hat f_k .
\end{equation}
With linear weights \(d=(1/10,3/5,3/10)\), the WENO-Z weights are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_5&=|\beta_0-\beta_2|,\\
\alpha_k&=d_k\left[1+
\left(\frac{\tau_5}{\beta_k+\epsilon_W}\right)^2\right],
\\
\omega_k&=\frac{\alpha_k}{\sum_m\alpha_m}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\hat f_k\) are the three fifth-order candidate fluxes, \(\beta_k\) are the
Jiang--Shu smoothness indicators, and \(\epsilon_W\) is the smoothness-weight
floor.  Their explicit forms and boundary stencil bookkeeping are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The reconstructed \(F_{i+1/2}\) is the
flux used by the explicit radial update.

\subsubsection{WENO Runge--Kutta update}

The explicit radial step applies the WENO flux with a third-order
strong-stability-preserving Runge--Kutta update.  Define
\begin{equation}
\lambda_W=\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x},
\qquad
\mathcal{D}_j(N)=F_{j+1/2}(N)-F_{j-1/2}(N),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{D}_j\) is the net flux entering cell \(j\).  The three
Runge--Kutta stages use the same substep source \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm W}\); their
coefficients are given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The step output
is the post-substep conservative spectrum \(N_{x,j}^{n+1}\).

\subsubsection{WENO output diagnostics}

After each substep, the stored conservative spectrum is projected to
\(N_x\ge0\).  This projection acts only on the transported electron cell
averages.  It does not change a light curve after transport.  At the last
substep in an output
shell, the same exponential cell-shape reconstruction used by the log-\(\gamma\)
solvers gives
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j
=\frac{N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}}{\gamma_j}.
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}\) is the reconstructed log-energy cell content.  The
radiation arrays written during shell preparation are shell-left fields used to
build the cooling seed.  The final output point has no following shell, so the
WENO driver refreshes \(L_\nu^{\rm syn}\) and \(n_\nu^{\rm syn}\) from the final
transported electron spectrum and the final dynamics-grid field.

\subsection{Direct characteristic-integration solver}

\subsubsection{Characteristic state}

The \texttt{charint\_1d} solver follows electron cooling by tracing
characteristics backward in energy.  It evolves the same conservative
log-\(\gamma\) cell averages as the other one-dimensional solvers:
\begin{equation}
N_{x,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dx}\,dx,
\qquad x=\ln\gamma_e,
\end{equation}
where \(N_{x,j}\) is the stored electron content per unit \(x\).  A new cell gets
the old electron content from the interval reached by the backward
characteristic.

\subsubsection{Characteristic local shock scales}

Each output shell first supplies the local shock state.  For
\(R_{i-1}\rightarrow R_i\), the solver uses
\begin{equation}
R_{\rm loc}=R_{i-1},\qquad
\Gamma_{\rm sh}=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2},\qquad
n_{\rm ext}=n(R_{\rm loc}),
\end{equation}
where \(R_{\rm loc}\) is the shell-left radius and \(n_{\rm ext}\) is the upstream
density.  The magnetic field and maximum electron Lorentz factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B'&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n_{\rm ext}m_pc^2
\Gamma_{\rm sh}(\Gamma_{\rm sh}-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B'e^3)^{1/2}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the cooling Lorentz factor and shock speed are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_c&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B'^2\Gamma_{\rm sh}t_{{\rm obs},i}}
{6\pi m_ec(1+z)},\\
\gamma_c&=a_c^{-1},\\
\beta_{\rm sh}&=(1-\Gamma_{\rm sh}^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The minimum injection Lorentz factor uses the same \(\gamma_m\) solution as the
other forward-shock branches.  Its available energy per accelerated electron is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma_{\rm sh}-1)
\end{equation}
in units of \(m_ec^2\).  These shell quantities set the diagnostics and the first
trial substep.  In a non-uniform density profile, the same \(B'\),
\(\gamma_{\max}\), and \(\gamma_m\) formulae are re-evaluated at
\begin{equation}
R_m=\frac{R_i+R_{i-1}}{2},
\qquad n_m=n(R_m) .
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Characteristic radial substeps}

The characteristic map is accurate only if the radial lag is short enough to
resolve cooling.  Without adaptive substeps, the step length is set by the shorter
of the high-energy synchrotron and adiabatic cooling lengths:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm syn}&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B'^2}{6\pi m_ec^2\beta_{\rm sh}\Gamma_{\rm sh}},\\
\Delta R_{\rm ch}&=\frac{\eta_{\rm CFL}}
{a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}+\eta_{\rm ad}/R_m},\\
N_i^{\rm ch}&=\left\lceil\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{\Delta R_{\rm ch}}\right\rceil,\\
L_i&=\max\left[
N_{\rm char,min},
\min\left(N_{\rm char,max},N_i^{\rm ch}\right)
\right],\\
\Delta R&=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}\) estimates the fastest synchrotron drift,
\(\eta_{\rm ad}/R_m\) estimates the adiabatic drift, and \(L_i\) is limited by
the declared characteristic substep bounds.

\subsubsection{Characteristic adaptive acceptance}

Adaptive characteristic substeps choose the radial lag from a local density and
radius-change measure.  The allowed trial interval is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_{\min}&=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{N_{\rm char,max}},\\
\Delta R_{\max}&=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{N_{\rm char,min}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and a trial step is accepted when
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_R\le\eta_{\rm tol}\epsilon_{\rm sub}
\end{equation}
or when the lower bound has been reached.  For a trial beginning at \(R_s\), a
uniform medium uses
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_R=\Delta R/R_s,
\end{equation}
whereas density jumps use
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_R=\max\left[
\frac{\Delta R}{R_s},
\frac{|n(R_s+\Delta R)-n(R_s)|}{n(R_s)}
\right].
\end{equation}
This criterion selects the radial quadrature of the characteristic map.  It does
not change the density profile.

\subsubsection{Characteristic finite-shell source}

The characteristic source is normalized by the finite shell swept at the substep
radius.  At evaluation radius \(R_s\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n_s&\equiv n(R_s),\\
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)&=
n_s\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},\\
a_p(R_s)&=f_e\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}(R_s)-\gamma_m^{1-p}(R_s)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(q_R\) is the number of swept upstream electrons per unit radius,
\(a_p\) normalizes the accelerated power law, and \(V(R)=4\pi R^3/3\).  The
source placed on the \(x\) grid is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm C}=\frac{q_Ra_p}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_{\rm C}(x)\,dx,
\end{equation}
where \(q_R=q_R(R_s,\Delta R)\), \(a_p=a_p(R_s)\), and
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\psi_{\rm C}(x)&=
\begin{cases}
\gamma_e^{1-p}\mathcal C_{\max}(\gamma_e),
&\gamma_e\ge\gamma_m,\\
0,&\gamma_e<\gamma_m,
\end{cases}\\
\gamma_e&=\exp(x) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The high-energy cutoff is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma_e)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma_e\le\gamma_{\max},\\
\exp(1-\gamma_e/\gamma_{\max}), & \gamma_e>\gamma_{\max}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Thus the source is zero below \(\gamma_m\), follows \(\gamma_e^{-p}\) above
\(\gamma_m\), and has a continuous turnover above \(\gamma_{\max}\).

\subsubsection{Characteristic cooling map}

The characteristic map is simple in \(u=1/\gamma_e\).  In the synchrotron-only
branch, one substep obeys
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\gamma_e}{dR}=-a_{\rm syn}\gamma_e^2-b\gamma_e,
\qquad b=R_s^{-1}.
\end{equation}
This gives
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_{\rm syn}+bu .
\end{equation}
For a face that arrives at \(u\) after radial lag \(\ell\), the departure point is
\begin{equation}
u_{\rm back}=
\begin{cases}
u-a_{\rm syn}\ell, & b=0,\\
(u+a_{\rm syn}/b)e^{-b\ell}-a_{\rm syn}/b, & {\rm otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The general cooling branch uses the same idea after the total loss array has
been assembled from synchrotron, inverse-Compton, and self-absorption terms.  In
a non-uniform external medium, the shell loss array is rescaled by
\(n_s/n_{\rm ext}\).  The code then applies a piecewise affine map in
\(u=1/\gamma_e\), with the detailed cell-edge tracing given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The output of the map is the pair of
departure faces for each arrival cell.

\subsubsection{Characteristic conservative remap}

The remap conserves electron number by integrating between the two traced faces.
Old electrons are integrated over the full radial lag \(\Delta R\).  Newly
injected electrons are integrated along the same characteristic with a clustered
Gauss rule.  The prefix-integral formula and quadrature weights are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The remap output is the next conservative
log-\(\gamma\) spectrum \(N_{x,j}^{n+1}\).

\subsubsection{Characteristic output diagnostics}

After each output shell, the solver converts the stored log-energy cells back to
a centered physical spectrum:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\bigg|_j
=\frac{N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}}{\gamma_j}.
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}\) is the local exponential reconstruction of
\(dN_e/dx\).  The reported break frequencies are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_m=\frac{3eB'\gamma_m^2}
{4\pi m_ec\Gamma_{\rm sh}(1-\beta_{\rm sh})(1+z)},\\
\nu_c=\frac{3eB'\gamma_c^2}
{4\pi m_ec\Gamma_{\rm sh}(1-\beta_{\rm sh})(1+z)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The self-absorption diagnostic comes from the final synchrotron optical-depth
grid:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_\nu(\nu_a)&=1,\\
\nu_a^{\rm obs}&=\frac{\nu_a}
{\Gamma_{\rm sh}(1-\beta_{\rm sh})(1+z)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Discontinuous-Galerkin solver}

\subsubsection{DG state and nodal representation}
