The \texttt{dg\_1d} solver represents the electron spectrum with local
polynomials instead of one value per cell.  It uses the same coordinate \(y\) as
the finite-volume branch and evolves the conservative density
\begin{equation}
N_y(y,R)=\frac{dN_e}{dy}
=\frac{dN_e}{dx_g}\frac{dx_g}{dy}.
\end{equation}
Here \(x_g\) is the log-energy coordinate used for radiation diagnostics.  On an
element \(K_a=[y_{a,L},y_{a,R}]\), we map position to
\(r\in[-1,1]\) through \(y=y_a+\Delta y_ar/2\).  The local polynomial is
\begin{equation}
N_y^h(y,R)\big|_{K_a}=
\sum_{\ell=0}^{p_{\rm DG}}N_{a\ell}(R)L_\ell(r),
\end{equation}
where \(L_\ell\) are Lagrange polynomials on Legendre--Gauss--Lobatto nodes and
\(p_{\rm DG}\) is the DG polynomial order.  At each shell, the mesh is rebuilt
around the active break positions \(\ln\gamma_m\),
\(\ln\gamma_c\), and \(\ln\gamma_{\max}\).  Each active interval is
split into \(N_{\rm seg}\) elements.  Breaks outside the electron grid, or closer
than the declared mesh-separation tolerance, are not used as element boundaries.

\subsubsection{DG shell scales and active support}

The DG solver needs a fixed diagnostic grid and a moving active grid.  The fixed
diagnostic grid is the shared four-velocity grid with scale \(\gamma_{\rm sc}\).
Its upper edge is chosen from a high-energy coverage estimate:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\max}^{\rm grid}
&=\eta_{\rm tail}
\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B'_{\rm ref}e^3)^{1/2}},\\
B'_{\rm ref}&=(8\pi\epsilon_B e'_{\rm ref})^{1/2},\\
e'_{\rm ref}
&=\kappa_{\rm FS}\Gamma_{\rm end}(\Gamma_{\rm end}-1)n(R_1)m_pc^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\Gamma_{\rm end}\) is the last dynamics-grid Lorentz factor,
\(\eta_{\rm tail}\) sets the high-energy coverage, and \(\kappa_{\rm FS}\) is the
forward-shock energy-density factor used for this diagnostic grid estimate.  At
each output shell, the solver uses
\begin{equation}
\bar\Gamma_i=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2}
\end{equation}
and computes the current shell break values:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_i'&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n(R_{i-1})m_pc^2
\bar\Gamma_i(\bar\Gamma_i-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max,i}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B_i'e^3)^{1/2}},\\
\gamma_{m,i}&=\gamma_m\!\left[
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\bar\Gamma_i-\!1),
\gamma_{\max,i},p\right],\\
a_{c,i}&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B_i'^2\bar\Gamma_i t_{{\rm obs},i}}
{6\pi m_ec(1+z)},\\
\gamma_{c,i}&=a_{c,i}^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These values set the local break-frequency diagnostics and the injection source
for each DG substep.

\subsubsection{DG active-support remesh}

The active DG mesh tracks the part of the electron spectrum that contains source
or surviving high-energy electrons.  It always includes the current injection,
cooling, and cutoff breaks when those breaks lie inside the electron domain.  It
also keeps a high-energy tail when the old DG state still carries the moment
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{M}_2(x_0)\equiv
\int_{\gamma_e\ge\exp(x_0)}N_y\gamma_e^2\,dy .
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal{M}_2\) is a high-energy electron moment used only to choose the
active DG mesh.  The active-edge threshold formula is given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The output is a DG element set built around
\(\gamma_{m,i}\), \(\gamma_{c,i}\), and \(\gamma_{\max,i}\).

\subsubsection{DG radial substeps and density jumps}

The DG radial grid is refined near density jumps.  Away from jumps, the output
shell is divided into \(N_{\rm DG}\) regular substeps.  Near a Gaussian density
structure, the branch cuts candidate steps at declared jump markers and limits
the local radial width by the jump width and density-gradient scale.  The marker,
window, and gradient formulas are given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.
The output is a sequence of radial substeps that does not cross a sharp density
change in one DG solve.

\subsubsection{DG finite-shell source and cooling}

The DG source is normalized so that its polynomial representation carries the
same injected electron number as the finite-cell source.  At a DG substep radius
\(R_s\), the solver re-evaluates \(n(R_s)\), \(B'\), \(\gamma_{\max}\), and
\(\gamma_m\) unless the medium is strictly uniform.  The source entering the
implicit DG step is
\begin{equation}
Q_y^{\rm D}(R_s)=
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_p(R_s)\,\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}.
\end{equation}
Here \(q_R\) is the finite-shell swept-particle source, \(a_p\) is the power-law
normalization, and \(\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}\) is normalized by a conservative DG
integral described in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  The cooling array is
computed once from the shell radiation field.  For a non-uniform medium, it is
scaled locally as
\begin{equation}
D_j(R_s)=
D_j(R_{i-1})\frac{n(R_s)}{n(R_{i-1})}.
\end{equation}
The DG substep solves
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial N_y}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
\left[
-\frac{D(R_s)+R_s^{-1}}
{dx_g/dy}N_y
\right]
=Q_y^{\rm D}(R_s).
\end{equation}
The velocity term contains both radiative cooling and the adiabatic drift
\(R_s^{-1}\).

\subsubsection{DG output projection}

After each output radius, the moving DG polynomial is projected back to the fixed
diagnostic grid.  The projection uses cell-overlap quadrature:
\begin{equation}
\bar N_{y,j}^{\rm out}=
\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\sum_a\int_{[y_{j-1/2},y_{j+1/2}]\cap K_a}
N_y^h(y)\,dy .
\end{equation}
The reported electron spectrum is then
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j=
\frac{N_{y,j}^{\rm exp}}
{\gamma_j(dx_g/dy)_j},
\end{equation}
where \(N_{y,j}^{\rm exp}\) is the center value of the local exponential
reconstruction of the fixed-grid cell average.  The solver requires both the DG
integral and the projected fixed-grid integral to remain positive:
\begin{equation}
\int N_y^h\,dy>0,\qquad
\sum_j\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\bigg|_j
\gamma_j\Delta x_j>0,
\end{equation}
as state-domain conditions for the DG output.  The final output radius is
refreshed through the same synchrotron optical-depth grid used for \(\nu_a\), so
the returned \(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and \(\nu_a\) arrays describe the final
electron spectrum.

\subsubsection{DG source projection}

DG source projection puts the finite-shell electron source on the polynomial
mesh.  A source written on the natural-log grid, \(Q_{x_g}\), is first converted
to the DG coordinate \(y\):
\begin{equation}
Q_y(y)=Q_{x_g}[x_g(y)]\frac{dx_g}{dy}.
\end{equation}
Here \(x_g=\ln\gamma_e\), so \(Q_y\,dy\) and \(Q_{x_g}\,dx_g\) contain the same
number of injected electrons.  The solver then projects \(Q_y\) onto the local
Legendre basis and evaluates the source at the LGL nodes.  The forward source
uses the shared cutoff power law.  The reverse-shock kinetic source uses the
normalized \((\gamma_e-1)^{-p}\) shape.  Both normalizations are fixed by the
shell-integrated injected electron number, and the output is the nodal source
\(Q_{a,i}\) used by the DG transport step.  The projection integrals are given
in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsubsection{DG implicit transport solve}

The implicit DG step moves electrons through the \(y\) coordinate by cooling.
The conserved density obeys
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial N_y}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(v_yN_y\right)&=Q_y,\\
v_y&=-\frac{D_{\rm rad}+D_{\rm ad}}
{dx_g/dy}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(D_{\rm rad}\) and \(D_{\rm ad}\) are positive radiative and adiabatic loss
coefficients.  The forward path uses \(D_{\rm ad}=R^{-1}\).  The reverse path
uses \(R^{-1}\) before crossing and the stored post-crossing volume loss
\((d\ln V_3/dR)/3\) after crossing.  In the public \texttt{dg\_1d} path, each
element advances this equation with a fully implicit nodal block,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal A_a N_a^{n+1}
=N_a^n+\Delta R\,Q_a+\mathcal F_a^{\rm up},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal A_a\) is a \((p_{\rm DG}+1)\times(p_{\rm DG}+1)\) DG transport
block and \(\mathcal F_a^{\rm up}\) is the upwind face contribution.  Radiative
and adiabatic losses usually give \(v_y<0\), so the blocks are solved from high
energy to low energy.  If any node has \(v_y>0\), the same flux operator is
assembled as one global implicit system.  In four-velocity DG coordinates the
first low-energy face is closed, so cooled particles that leave the finite
energy range are kept in the first element.  The output is a conservative nodal
DG spectrum at the next radius; the block entries and face terms are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsubsection{DG conservative remap and positivity}

DG remapping changes the polynomial mesh while preserving electron number.  If a
new coordinate \(y_{\rm n}\) and an old coordinate \(y_{\rm o}\) represent the
same \(\gamma_e\), the remap enforces
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm n}(y_{\rm n})\,dy_{\rm n}
=N_{\rm o}(y_{\rm o})\,dy_{\rm o},
\end{equation}
and checks the conserved total
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm tot}=\sum_a\frac{\Delta y_a}{2}
\sum_i w_iN_{a,i}.
\end{equation}
After projection or a radial solve, an element-local positivity operator keeps
the element average fixed while reducing only the high-order departure from that
average when a node becomes negative.  The optional Jackson or Fejer modal
operation acts only on high-order modal coefficients in flagged DG elements.  It
changes the DG representation of the electron spectrum, not the observer
light-curve assembly.  The output is a non-negative conservative DG state; the
local formulas are given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsubsection{DG characteristic projector}

The DG characteristic projector maps conserved electron content between old and
new elements.  It uses the local affine loss law
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\gamma_e}{dR}=-a_{\rm rad}\gamma_e^2-b_{\rm ad}\gamma_e,
\end{equation}
where \(a_{\rm rad}\) is the radiative coefficient and \(b_{\rm ad}\) is the
adiabatic coefficient.  The inverse map gives the departure energy for a chosen
arrival energy, then projects the upstream DG polynomial onto the arrival
element.  Content whose characteristic exits below the first four-velocity
element is stored as low-energy mass and written back into the first element.
This projector is a conservative remap and diagnostic advance inside the DG
kernel.  The public \texttt{dg\_1d} forward light-curve path uses the fully
implicit block solve above for shell-local transport.  The affine forward and
inverse maps are listed in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

\subsection{Cooling assembler}

\subsubsection{Radial loss state}

The cooling assembler gives each electron node a radial energy-loss rate.  It
combines synchrotron cooling, inverse-Compton cooling, and
synchrotron-self-absorption heating before the transport solver adds adiabatic
drift.  With \(x=\ln\gamma_e\), the stored radial loss coefficient is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}&=-D_i\gamma_i,\\
v_{x,i+1/2}
&=\frac{D_i+D_{i+1}}{2}+R^{-1},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_i>0\) means cooling and positive \(v_x\) moves the distribution toward
lower \(\gamma_e\).  Synchrotron cooling uses the physical coefficient
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm syn}&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B'^2}{6\pi m_e c^2\,\beta\Gamma},\\
\left.\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}\right|_{\rm syn}
&=-a_{\rm syn}\gamma_i^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B'\) is the comoving magnetic field and \(\beta\Gamma c\) converts
comoving cooling rates to radial rates.

\subsubsection{Cooling-branch assembly}

The assembler has four supported cooling branches.  Photon-mediated terms use
the conversion \(dt'/dR=(\beta\Gamma c)^{-1}\).  Let \(A_i^{\rm IC}\) be the
tabulated inverse-Compton and Klein--Nishina coefficient from the seed photon
field, \(A_i^{\rm abs}\) be the synchrotron-self-absorption heating coefficient,
and \(Y_i\) be an analytic Compton factor.  The stored loss coefficient is
\begin{equation}
D_i=
\begin{cases}
a_{\rm syn}\gamma_i, & \mathrm{synchrotron\ only},\\[0.6ex]
\left[a_{\rm syn}+
\dfrac{A_i^{\rm IC}-A_i^{\rm abs}}{\beta\Gamma c}
\right]\gamma_i, & \mathrm{tabulated\ IC/KN},\\[2ex]
\left[a_{\rm syn}(1+Y_i^{\rm N})
-\dfrac{A_i^{\rm abs}}{\beta\Gamma c}
\right]\gamma_i, & \mathrm{Nakar},\\[2ex]
\left[a_{\rm syn}(1+Y_i^{\rm F})
-\dfrac{A_i^{\rm abs}}{\beta\Gamma c}
\right]\gamma_i, & \mathrm{Fan}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly}
\end{equation}
The tabulated branch uses the local photon field directly.  The Nakar and Fan
branches replace that photon integral by analytic estimates of the Compton
factor.

\subsection{Nakar analytic Compton closure}

The Nakar branch estimates inverse-Compton cooling from the synchrotron
luminosity below the Klein--Nishina threshold.  For electron node \(i\), the
threshold frequency and sub-threshold luminosity are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat\nu_i&=\frac{m_e c^2}{h\gamma_i},\\
L_i^{<}&=
\int_{\nu_{\min}}^{\min(\hat\nu_i,\nu_{\max})}
L_\nu^{\rm syn}\,d\nu,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
following the Nakar-style prescription \citep{NakarAndoSari2009}.  The assembler
then converts this luminosity to
\begin{equation}
Y_i^{\rm N}=
\frac{L_i^{<}}
{(4\pi R^2 c)(4\Gamma^2 n m_p c^2)},
\end{equation}
where the denominator is the shocked-shell energy flux scale.  The seed-frequency
integral uses the fixed logarithmic Gauss order \(N_{\rm G}\) in every frequency
cell.

\subsection{Fan Klein--Nishina Compton closure}

The Fan branch estimates the Klein--Nishina correction from the fraction of
synchrotron photons below the electron-dependent threshold
\citep{FanPiran2008}.  For each electron node,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\eta_{\rm rad}&=\min\left[1,\left(\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_c}\right)^{p-2}\right],\\
\hat\gamma_i&=
\left(\frac{m_e c^2}{hC_\nu B'\gamma_i}\right)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_M&=\gamma_{\max},\\
C_\nu&=\frac{3e}{4\pi m_e c}.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:synch-frequency-coeff}
\end{equation}
Here \(\eta_{\rm rad}\) is the radiative electron-energy fraction,
\(\hat\gamma_i\) is the electron Lorentz factor whose synchrotron photons enter
the Klein--Nishina threshold for node \(i\), and \(C_\nu B'\gamma^2\) is the
synchrotron characteristic frequency.  The analytic Compton factor is the
positive solution of
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Xi_i&=\eta_{\rm rad}\eta_i^{\rm KN}\frac{\epsilon_e}{\epsilon_B},\\
Y_i^{\rm F}(1+Y_i^{\rm F})&=\Xi_i,\\
Y_i^{\rm F}=
\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+4\Xi_i}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The remaining quantity is \(\eta_i^{\rm KN}\), the synchrotron photon-energy
fraction below the Klein--Nishina limit for node \(i\).  The code evaluates this
fraction from the fast- or slow-cooling broken power-law spectrum.  It gives
\(\eta_i^{\rm KN}=0\) when the threshold lies below the lowest relevant electron
break and \(\eta_i^{\rm KN}=1\) when it lies above \(\gamma_M\).  Between those
limits, the analytic integrals over the broken spectrum are listed in
Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.  The output of the branch is \(Y_i^{\rm F}\)
for each electron node.  Synchrotron-self-absorption appears with the opposite
sign in Equation~\eqref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly} because absorbed
synchrotron photons heat the same electron grid.

\subsection{Cooling-branch boundary}

The cooling branches have a hard physics boundary.  Joint hadronic feedback uses
only the tabulated IC/KN path, because the secondary source
\(Q_{e,R}^{\rm sec}\) and the attenuated photon field must be evaluated against
the same local \(N_e,n_\gamma\) state.  An analytic \(Y\) closure would separate
these two objects.  The public Python cooling modes expose the synchrotron-only,
tabulated IC/KN, and Nakar-style branches.  The Fan-style branch is an internal
Fortran closure.

\subsection{Synchrotron-self-absorption heating kernel}

\subsubsection{SSA target photon state}

The SSA heating kernel measures how absorbed synchrotron photons heat the
electron grid.  It computes \(A_i^{\rm abs}\) in
Equation~\eqref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly} from the local seed photon
density \(n_\nu\).  For an electron with Lorentz factor \(\gamma_i\), the
frequency scales are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_B&=\frac{eB'}{2\pi m_e c},&
\nu_{\min,i}&=\frac{\nu_B}{\gamma_i},\\
\nu_{u,i}&=\frac{3eB'\gamma_i^2}{4\pi m_e c},&
B_Q&=\frac{m_e^2 c^3}{e\hbar},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:ssa-frequency-scales}
\end{equation}
where \(\nu_B\) is the gyrofrequency, \(\nu_{\min,i}\) is the low-frequency
cutoff of the fit, \(\nu_{u,i}\) is the synchrotron turnover scale for that
electron, and \(B_Q=m_e^2c^3/(e\hbar)\) with \(\hbar=h/(2\pi)\).

\subsubsection{SSA single-particle fit}

The single-particle absorption fit gives the heating cross section used in the
frequency integral.  It is split at \(\nu_{u,i}\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_{L,i}&=\kappa_1\frac{B_Q}{B'}
(\chi_{\rm L}\nu_{\min,i})^{5/3},\\
S_{H,i}&=\kappa_2\frac{B_Q}{B'}\gamma_i^{-3},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_i(\nu)=
\begin{cases}
S_{L,i}\nu^{-5/3},
& \nu_{\min,i}<\nu\le\nu_{u,i},\\[2ex]
S_{H,i}\dfrac{\nu_B}{\nu}\exp(-\nu/\nu_{u,i}),
& \nu>\nu_{u,i},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\kappa_1\), \(\kappa_2\), and \(\chi_{\rm L}\) are the fixed coefficients
of the adopted synchrotron-absorption asymptotic fit.  They belong to the
single-particle kernel and are not additional physical parameters.  The heating
coefficient returned to the cooling assembler is
\begin{equation}
A_i^{\rm abs}
=c\int_{\nu_{\min,i}}^{\nu_{\max}}
h\nu\,n_\nu\,\Sigma_i(\nu)\,d\nu .
\end{equation}
This integral counts the photon energy absorbed per unit time by electrons at
\(\gamma_i\).

\subsubsection{SSA quadrature}

The SSA quadrature evaluates the heating integral on the local photon-frequency
grid.  It separates the low-frequency power-law part of \(\Sigma_i\) from the
high-frequency exponential part at \(\nu_{u,i}\).  Complete frequency cells use a
logarithmic Gauss rule of order \(N_{\rm G}\), while only the boundary cells at
\(\nu_{\min,i}\) and \(\nu_{u,i}\) are clipped.  The shared radiation
interpolation supplies \(n_\nu\) inside each cell.  The output is the heating
coefficient \(A_i^{\rm abs}\) passed to the cooling assembler; the prefix-sum and
high-frequency quadrature formulas are given in Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.

\subsection{Grid-level inverse-Compton cooling kernel}

\subsubsection{IC seed photons and kinematics}

The grid-level inverse-Compton kernel computes the cooling coefficient
\(A_i^{\rm IC}\) from the local seed photon spectrum.  For an electron at
\(\gamma_i\), the dimensionless seed and scattered photon energies are
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_0=\frac{h\nu_0}{m_e c^2},\qquad
\epsilon=\frac{h\nu}{m_e c^2},
\end{equation}
with \(\xi=4\gamma_i\epsilon_0\).  The largest allowed scattered energy is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon\le
\epsilon_{\max}=\frac{4\gamma_i^2\epsilon_0}
{1+4\gamma_i\epsilon_0},
\end{equation}
and the Jones variable is
\begin{equation}
q=\frac{\epsilon}{\xi(\gamma_i-\epsilon)} .
\end{equation}
Allowed low-frequency Thomson scatterings,
\(\nu_0/(4\gamma_i^2)<\nu\le\nu_0\), use
\begin{equation}
G_{\rm L}=\frac{\nu}{\nu_0}-\frac{1}{4\gamma_i^2},
\end{equation}
and the remaining allowed scatterings use the Jones kernel
\(G_{\rm J}(q,\xi)\) from Equation~\eqref{eq:ssc-kernel}.  These variables set
which seed photons cool each electron node and define the kernel used in
\(A_i^{\rm IC}\).

\subsubsection{IC loss coefficient}

The loss coefficient passed to the cooling assembler is
\begin{equation}
A_i^{\rm IC}=
\frac{3h\sigma_{\rm es}}{4m_e c\gamma_i^3}
\int d\nu_0\,\frac{n_{\nu_0}}{\nu_0}
\int d\nu\,\nu\,G_{\rm loss}(\nu,\nu_0,\gamma_i),
\end{equation}
where \(G_{\rm loss}\) is the branch-selected kernel above.  This coefficient is
positive for inverse-Compton cooling, and it is the quantity inserted into
Equation~\eqref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly}.

\subsubsection{Coupled-budget IC coefficient}

The coupled electron--photon budget path evaluates the same IC kernel on the
log-spaced seed and scattered-frequency grids.  It returns the auxiliary
coefficient \(A_i^{\rm IC,b}\) used by the implicit cooling solve in the joint
feedback calculation.  This path changes the numerical accumulation of the same
physical IC loss kernel; it does not define a separate cooling law.  The
log-frequency seed grid and Simpson-weighted budget coefficient are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.

\subsection{Synchrotron emissivity quadrature}

\subsubsection{Local synchrotron emissivity}

The synchrotron emissivity is computed from the transported electron spectrum.
Using the standard single-particle kernel,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_\nu^{\rm syn}(R)&=
\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3B'}{m_e c^2}
\int N_e(\gamma_e,R)
F\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_c}\right)d\gamma_e,\\
\nu_c&=C_\nu B'\gamma_e^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(C_\nu\) is defined in Eq.~(\ref{eq:synch-frequency-coeff}).  Self-absorption
is applied after the emitted luminosity has been assembled
\citep{RybickiLightman1979,Sari1998,Granot2002}.

\subsubsection{Fixed-grid emissivity quadrature}

The fixed-grid emissivity branch integrates over the transported electron grid.
The fast paths replace \(F(x)\) by the fixed single-particle fit \(\Phi(x)\)
given in Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.  On \(y_i=\ln\gamma_i\) with spacing
\(\Delta y\), Simpson weights \(w_i^{\rm S}\), and
\(N_i=dN_e/d\gamma_e(\gamma_i)\), the emitted shell luminosity is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,{\rm em}}&=
C_{\rm syn}B'\frac{\Delta y}{3}
\sum_i w_i^{\rm S}N_i\gamma_i\\
&\quad\times
\Phi\!\left(\frac{\nu}{C_\nu B'\gamma_i^2}\right),\\
C_{\rm syn}&=\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3}{m_e c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here the factor \(N_i\gamma_i\,dy\) is the electron number in a logarithmic
energy interval.  The output of this quadrature is the unabsorbed synchrotron
luminosity passed to the self-absorption and observer-projection stages.

\subsection{Synchrotron self-absorption shell transfer}

\subsubsection{Self-absorption depth}

The self-absorption depth is the optical depth of one shocked shell to its own
synchrotron photons.  The shell already stores its total electron spectrum, so
the calculation uses an electron column rather than a volume emissivity.  For
adjacent electron points,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_i=\frac{N_i}{\gamma_i^2},\qquad
\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}=\frac{\gamma_i+\gamma_{i+1}}{2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(N_i=dN_e/d\gamma_e\), \(q_i\) is the derivative variable used by the
absorption operator, and \(\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}\) is the midpoint Lorentz factor of
the cell face.  The shell optical depth is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_\nu&=
\frac{C_\tau B'}{4\pi R^2\nu^2}
\sum_i
\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}^2
\left(q_i-q_{i+1}\right)\\
&\quad\times
\Phi\!\left[
\frac{\nu}{C_\nu B'\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}^2}
\right],\\
C_\tau&=\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3}{8\pi m_e^2 c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(R\) is the shock radius, \(B'\) is the comoving magnetic field, and
\(\Phi\) is the same synchrotron kernel used for emission.  The difference
\(q_i-q_{i+1}\) is the finite-volume form of the electron-spectrum derivative in
the self-absorption coefficient.  The output \(\tau_\nu(R)\) sets both the
escaping synchrotron luminosity and the self-absorption frequency.

\subsubsection{Finite-cell transfer and seed photons}
\label{sec:syn-shell-transfer}

The finite-cell transfer factor converts emitted synchrotron power into escaping
power.  For one uniform emitting and absorbing shell, the transfer factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\psi(\tau_\nu)&=
\begin{cases}
1,&\tau_\nu=0,\\
\dfrac{1-\exp(-\tau_\nu)}{\tau_\nu},&\tau_\nu>0,
\end{cases}
\label{eq:finite-cell-transfer}\\
L_\nu^{\rm syn}&=L_{\nu,{\rm em}}\psi(\tau_\nu),\\
n_\nu^{\rm syn}&=\frac{L_\nu^{\rm syn}}{4\pi R^2 c h\nu}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_{\nu,{\rm em}}\) is the luminosity before absorption, \(L_\nu^{\rm syn}\)
is the escaping synchrotron luminosity, and \(n_\nu^{\rm syn}\) is the photon
number density used by the inverse-Compton kernel.  The optically thin limit is
the explicit value \(\psi(0)=1\).  The adaptive solution for \(\nu_a\) below uses
the same \(\tau_\nu\) grid, so the transfer output and the absorption-break
diagnostic come from the same shell state.

\subsection{Cyclotron-bin synchrotron extension}

The cyclotron-bin extension supplies the low-frequency radiation from mildly
relativistic electrons.  Electrons above the transition \(\gamma_{\rm cyc}\)
still use the synchrotron quadrature.  Electrons below it keep their stored
number in the same electron grid, but their radiated power is deposited into the
frequency bin that contains the fundamental cyclotron frequency.  For one
electron segment,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta N_i=\frac{N_i+N_{i+1}}{2}(\gamma_{i+1}-\gamma_i),
\qquad
\bar\gamma_i=\frac{\gamma_i+\gamma_{i+1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta N_i\) is the number of electrons in the segment and
\(\bar\gamma_i\) is its midpoint Lorentz factor.  The branch places their power
at
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_0&=\frac{\nu_B}{\bar\gamma_i},\\
\nu_B&=\frac{eB'}{2\pi m_e c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\nu_B\) is the nonrelativistic gyrofrequency.  The luminosity added to
the selected bin is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta L_{\nu_k,{\rm em}}&=
\frac{\Delta N_i}{\Delta\nu_k}
\frac{4}{3}\sigma_{\rm es}cU'_B
\bar\gamma_i^2
\left(1-\bar\gamma_i^{-2}\right),\\
U'_B&=\frac{B'^2}{8\pi},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section and
\(\Delta\nu_k\) is the bin width.  This added power is a shell radiation output,
not a change to the evolved electron spectrum.  The same factor
\(\psi(\tau_{\nu_k})\) converts it into the escaping luminosity and the local
seed photon density.

\subsection{Radiation interpolation and quadrature helpers}
\label{sec:positive-field-interpolation}

The radiation interpolation helper reads tabulated shell radiation fields between
frequency bins.  The same rule is used for emissivity, luminosity, and photon
density.  Positive adjacent endpoints define a local power law, while a segment
with a nonpositive endpoint is read as a linear segment of the same table.  The
logarithmic quadrature helper then chooses support points for SSA heating, IC
cooling, and SSC integrals.  Its reduced grid keeps support near the
low-frequency self-absorbed part while still reaching \(\nu_{\max}\).  The
interpolation formula and logarithmic Gauss rule are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}; the helper only samples the stored radiation
field and does not change the shell state.

\subsection{SSA heating segment quadrature}

The self-absorption heating segment adds absorbed photon energy back to the
electrons in one frequency interval.  For a local photon density \(n_\nu\), the
heating rate is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_{\rm SSA}=
\int_{\nu_0}^{\nu_1}\sigma_{\rm SSA}(\nu)n_\nu h\nu c\,d\nu .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm SSA}\) is the self-absorption cross section used by the same
electron segment.  The output \(H_{\rm SSA}\) is the local energy return that
enters the electron energy-space update.  The low- and high-frequency branches
of \(\sigma_{\rm SSA}\), the cutoff shape, and the logarithmic quadrature rule
are given in Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.

\subsection{Self-absorption frequency solver}

The self-absorption frequency is the shell frequency where the synchrotron
optical depth reaches unity.  It is defined by
\begin{equation}
\tau_\nu(\nu_a)=1 .
\end{equation}
Because \(\nu_a\) does not usually sit on the stored radiation grid, the solver
evaluates \(\tau_\nu\) off-grid from the same electron spectrum and absorption
kernel used for the shell transfer.  On each electron cell it works in
\(x=\ln\gamma\) and reconstructs \(N_e\) and \(q=N_e/\gamma^2\).  The output
\(\nu_a(R)\) is a diagnostic of the local spectral break.  If the evaluated
frequency interval does not contain \(\tau_\nu=1\), the reported value is the
nearest interval boundary and should be read as a limit.  The adaptive quadrature,
search bounds, and log-frequency root update are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.

\subsection{Synchrotron polarization kernel}

The synchrotron polarization kernel is a local emissivity diagnostic.  It splits
the single-electron synchrotron power into two linear-polarization components,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
P_\perp&\propto\frac{F(x)+G(x)}{2},\\
P_\parallel&\propto\frac{F(x)-G(x)}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(P_\perp\) and \(P_\parallel\) are the two linear-polarization emissivity
components and \(x\) is the usual synchrotron frequency ratio.  The functions
\(F(x)\), \(G(x)\), and their quadrature branches are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.  This helper does not change the
self-absorbed luminosity.

The local polarization fraction weights the single-electron components by the
electron distribution.  At each frequency,
\begin{equation}
\Pi_\nu=
\frac{\int N_e(\gamma_e)[P_\perp(x)-P_\parallel(x)]d\gamma_e}
{\int N_e(\gamma_e)[P_\perp(x)+P_\parallel(x)]d\gamma_e},
\end{equation}
and the local diagnostic components are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,\perp}&=\frac{1+\Pi_\nu}{2}L_\nu^{\rm syn},\\
L_{\nu,\parallel}&=\frac{1-\Pi_\nu}{2}L_\nu^{\rm syn}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These components are local shell diagnostics.  The present public observer
interface does not provide a full polarization projection product.

The \(\chi\)-resolved shell diagnostic tracks how absorption accumulates through
ordered downstream zones.  The cumulative path is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\nu,\le b}&=\sum_{a=1}^b\tau_{\nu,a},\\
\tau_{\nu,\le b}(\nu_{{\rm a},b})&=1.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the shell-reduced spectra are log-\(\chi\) integrals,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,{\rm sh}}&=\sum_a \chi_a\Delta\eta\,L_{\nu,a},\\
n_{\nu,{\rm sh}}&=\sum_a \chi_a\Delta\eta\,n_{\nu,a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The output remains a shell-level record of intrinsic polarization and absorption;
it is not a sky-image or Stokes-parameter observer calculation.

\subsection{Adaptive radiation-grid resampling}

The adaptive radiation-grid module selects support points for a synchrotron
quadrature.  It does not evolve, renormalize, or change the transported electron
spectrum \(N_e\).  Its input is the already computed list of pairs
\((\gamma_i,N_{e,i})\), and its output is a smaller list used only by the
emissivity integral.  The direct wrapper
\texttt{electron\_radiation\_grid} is compiled with the electron common kernels,
but the inspected production light-curve kernels do not call this wrapper.  The
module is therefore documented as a standalone helper.

The selected radiation grid preserves the ends of the positive emitting support
and places extra quadrature points near curvature in the electron spectrum.
Power-law portions of \(N_e(\gamma)\) need little support, while cooling breaks,
injection turnovers, and cutoffs need denser sampling.  The helper uses
logarithmic coordinates only to choose support points; it returns selected
indices and never replaces the physical electron spectrum.  The active-support
scan, curvature estimator, and point-selection rule are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved downstream electron state}

The \(\chi\)-resolved forward-electron branch records where electrons sit behind
the forward shock.  The transport coordinate is the finite downstream mass
coordinate \(q\), while \(\chi\) is a derived diagnostic coordinate used to
display and project the state.  For gas with adiabatic index \(\hat\gamma\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_{\rm sh}&=\frac{\hat\gamma+1}{\hat\gamma-1},\\
q_\ast&=1-\left(1-\frac{1}{r_{\rm sh}}\right)^{r_{\rm sh}},\\
\Delta q&=\frac{q_\ast}{N_\chi},\qquad
q_{a+1/2}=a\Delta q .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a\) labels downstream cells and \(q=0\) is the shock front.  In the
default two-dimensional finite-volume branch, the evolved cell average is
\begin{equation}
U_{ia}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta y_i\Delta q}
\int_{\Delta y_i}\int_{\Delta q_a}
\frac{\partial^2 N_e}{\partial y\,\partial q}\,dq\,dy ,
\end{equation}
where \(y\) is the four-velocity coordinate defined above.  The shell-reduced
spectrum is recovered by
\begin{equation}
N_i(R_j)=\sum_{a=1}^{N_\chi}U_{ia}(R_j)\Delta q .
\end{equation}
The characteristic and optional pulsar-wind branches use \(x=\ln\gamma_e\) on
the same \(q\) domain.  The initialization and coordinate-map details are given
in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

The downstream geometry assigns each \(q\) cell a radius, speed, and Lorentz
factor before cooling and emission are evaluated.  The map connects the
ultra-relativistic Blandford--McKee limit to the planar strong-shock limit, but
the transported electron content remains on \(q\).  The local magnetic fraction
may be constant or may decay with the comoving age \(t'_a\):
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{B,a}=\epsilon_{B,\min}
 +(\epsilon_B-\epsilon_{B,\min})
\left(1+\frac{t'_a}{t_B}\right)^{\alpha_t}.
\end{equation}
When magnetic decay is inactive, \(\epsilon_{B,a}=\epsilon_B\).  The local
comoving energy density and magnetic field are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e'_a&=r_{\rm sh}\Gamma_f(\Gamma_f-1)n(R)m_p c^2,\\
B'_a&=\left(8\pi\epsilon_{B,a}e'_a\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n(R)\) is the upstream external density at the shock.  The output
\((r_a,\Gamma_a,B'_a)\) sets the downstream cooling, synchrotron emission,
self-absorption, and diffusion coefficients.

The downstream radiation state gives each cell its own synchrotron luminosity and
self-absorption depth.  For adjacent electron bins in cell \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar\gamma_i&=\frac{\gamma_i+\gamma_{i+1}}{2},\\
E_{ia}&=\frac{N_{ia}+N_{i+1,a}}{2}(\gamma_{i+1}-\gamma_i),\\
A_{ia}&=\bar\gamma_i^2
\left(\frac{N_{ia}}{\gamma_i^2}
-\frac{N_{i+1,a}}{\gamma_{i+1}^2}\right),\\
\nu_{ia}&=C_\nu B'_a\bar\gamma_i^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(E_{ia}\) is the electron number in the segment, \(A_{ia}\) is the
self-absorption difference, and \(\nu_{ia}\) is the characteristic synchrotron
frequency.  With \(w_a=\Delta q\), the cell emissivity and optical depth are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,a}^{\rm em}&=C_{\rm syn}B'_a
\sum_iE_{ia}\Phi\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_{ia}}\right),\\
\tau_{\nu,a}&=
\frac{C_\tau B'_a w_a}{4\pi R^2\nu^2}
\sum_iA_{ia}\Phi\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_{ia}}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The transferred luminosity and photon density are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,a}&=L_{\nu,a}^{\rm em}\psi(\tau_{\nu,a}),\\
n_{\nu,a}&=\frac{L_{\nu,a}}{4\pi R^2 c h\nu}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\psi\) is the finite-cell transfer factor defined in
Eq.~(\ref{eq:finite-cell-transfer}).  The output of this stage is the local
downstream radiation state supplied to photon-history cooling and later observer
projection.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved photon-history cooling}

The photon-history field tells a downstream cell which earlier synchrotron
photons can reach it before the cooling update.  For each radial interval
\([R_{j-1},R_j]\), the wrapper constructs a causal comoving light-cone field on
the finite \(q\) grid.  A source cell \(s=(m,a)\) contributes to a target cell
\(t=(j,b)\) only if it is behind the target and inside the target light cone.
The construction then passes the resulting photon field to
Eq.~(\ref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly}).

The source-to-target frequency mapping uses the relative motion of the two
downstream cells.  With
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{st}&=\frac{\beta_t-\beta_s}{1-\beta_t\beta_s},\\
{\cal D}_{st}&=\frac{1+\beta_{st}}{(1-\beta_{st}^2)^{1/2}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
a target frequency \(\nu'\) samples the source spectrum at
\(\nu'_s=\nu'/{\cal D}_{st}\).  The source field is read only from positive
stored luminosity and photon-density bins.  Absorption along the source-to-target
path is stored in the survival factor \({\cal A}_{\nu'}\), which includes local
SSA and local \(\gamma\gamma\) opacity on the reduced cooling grid.  The overlap,
positive-field interpolation, and path-product details are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

The history contribution adds attenuated source radiation to the target cooling
field.  For target cell \(t\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta L_{\nu',t}^{\rm hist}
&=w_s{\cal D}_{st}^3
{\cal A}_{\nu'} Q_{L,s}\!\left(\frac{\nu'}{{\cal D}_{st}}\right),\\
\Delta n_{\nu',t}^{\rm hist}
&=w_s{\cal D}_{st}^2
{\cal A}_{\nu'} Q_{n,s}\!\left(\frac{\nu'}{{\cal D}_{st}}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(w_s\) is the finite overlap of the source cell with the target light cone,
and \(Q_{L,s}\) and \(Q_{n,s}\) are the stored source luminosity and photon-density
fields.  The factor \({\cal D}_{st}^3\) transforms luminosity, while
\({\cal D}_{st}^2\) transforms photon density.  Adding these terms to the local
synchrotron seed gives the cell-wise cooling field,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu',b}^{\rm cool}(R_j)
&=L_{\nu',b}^{\rm local}(R_{j-1})
+P_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}(R_j),\\
n_{\nu',b}^{\rm cool}(R_j)
&=n_{\nu',b}^{\rm local}(R_{j-1})
+n_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}(R_j).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
on the reduced cooling-frequency grid.  The cooling assembler then returns
\(D_{ib}\) in \(d\gamma_i/dR=-D_{ib}\gamma_i\), using the same synchrotron,
IC/KN, Nakar, Fan, and self-absorption terms as the one-dimensional forward
solver.  The streamed light-cone update is given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  These history arrays enter only the local
cooling calculation.  Observer-time projection still uses the stored synchrotron
luminosity, optical depth, radius, Lorentz factor, and volume-weight arrays
described below.

\subsection{Downstream \texorpdfstring{\(q\)}{q}-space advection--diffusion}

The \(q\)-space transport step moves electrons through the finite downstream
layer before their energy is cooled.  For one electron-energy bin \(i\), the
cell average \(U_i(q,R)\) obeys a conservative radial equation,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\!\left[A_q(q,R)U_i\right]
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\!\left[
\mathcal K_i(q,R)\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial q}
\right]
+S_i(q,R),\\
\mathcal K_i(q,R)
&=\frac{\kappa_i(q,R)}{\beta_{\rm sh}c}
\left(\frac{\partial q}{\partial s}\right)^2,\\
A_q(q,R)&=\frac{(3-k)(q_\ast-q)}{R}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_q\) is the downstream drift caused by expansion, \(\mathcal K_i\) is
the Bohm-diffusion coefficient after the coordinate change from shock-frame
distance \(s\) to \(q\), \(k\) is the external density index in
\(n\propto R^{-k}\), and \(\beta_{\rm sh}\) is the shock speed.  The speed
\(A_q\) vanishes at the active outer face \(q=q_\ast\).  The solver therefore
advects and diffuses electrons only inside the resolved downstream layer.

The shock-front source places newly accelerated electrons into the first
downstream cell.  In the discrete state,
\begin{equation}
U_{ia}^{\rm src}-U_{ia}^{n}
=\frac{Q_i(R,\Delta R)}{\Delta q}\,\delta_{a1},
\end{equation}
where \(a\) labels the downstream cell and \(Q_i(R,\Delta R)\) is the finite
radial source in electron-energy bin \(i\).  The factor \(\delta_{a1}\) puts the
source at the shock front.  After summing over \(q\), this source gives the same
electron number as the one-zone forward-shock source.

The conservative \(q\)-transport update combines drift, diffusion, and the
shock-front source before the energy update.  On a face,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_{a+1/2}^{+}&=\max(A_{a+1/2},0),\\
A_{a+1/2}^{-}&=\min(A_{a+1/2},0),\\
{\cal K}_{i,a+1/2}
&=\frac{\kappa_{i,a+1/2}}{\beta_{\rm sh}c}
\left(\frac{\partial q}{\partial s}\right)^2_{a+1/2},\\
{\cal H}_{i,a+1/2}
&={\cal H}^{A}_{i,a+1/2}-{\cal H}^{K}_{i,a+1/2},\\
{\cal H}^{A}_{i,a+1/2}
&=A_{a+1/2}^{+}U_{ia}^{n+1}
+A_{a+1/2}^{-}U_{i,a+1}^{n+1},\\
{\cal H}^{K}_{i,a+1/2}
&={\cal K}_{i,a+1/2}
\frac{U_{i,a+1}^{n+1}-U_{ia}^{n+1}}{\Delta q}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The face flux \({\cal H}_{i,a+1/2}\) is positive in the direction of increasing
\(q\).  The implicit cell equation is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_{ia}&=\frac{Q_i(R,\Delta R)}{\Delta q}\delta_{a1},\\
R_{ia}&=U_{ia}^{n}+S_{ia},\\
\Delta_a{\cal H}_i
&={\cal H}_{i,a+1/2}-{\cal H}_{i,a-1/2},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}
&=R_{ia}-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta q}\Delta_a{\cal H}_i .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This tridiagonal solve conserves the downstream electron content except for the
explicit shock-front source and any supported outer escape boundary.  The direct
implicit branch applies this joint \(q\) solve before the energy solve.  The
split finite-width and characteristic diagnostic branches use the same
conservative remap and diffusion operator around the energy update.  Detailed
face tracing, metric construction, and matrix-boundary forms are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.  By default the outer diffusive flux is
closed; the optional pulsar-wind research branch can instead use an absorbing
outer boundary.

\subsection{Downstream energy-space update}

The energy-space update cools each downstream column after the \(q\)-transport
step.  The input is the downstream cell average \(U_{ia}\) and the local cooling
coefficient \(D_{ia}\), which is computed from the synchrotron, IC, SSA, and
history photon fields in cell \(a\).  In the default four-velocity branch, the
face displacement is the sum of radiative cooling and adiabatic drift:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_j&=R_j-R_{j-1},\\
J_{i+1/2}&=(dx_g/dy)_{i+1/2},\\
\bar D_{i+1/2,a}&=\frac{D_{ia}+D_{i+1,a}}{2},\\
\eta_{i+1/2,a}
&=\Delta R_j\bar D_{i+1/2,a}
+\ln\!\left(\frac{R_j}{R_{j-1}}\right),\\
{\cal F}_{i+1/2,a}
&=\frac{\eta_{i+1/2,a}}{J_{i+1/2}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(J_{i+1/2}\) is the grid Jacobian and \({\cal F}_{i+1/2,a}\) is the signed
face displacement in the transported energy coordinate.  The logarithmic term is
the integrated adiabatic loss, \(d\ln\gamma_e/dR=-1/R\).  The conservative
tridiagonal solve is the same face-flux system used by the one-dimensional
\texttt{fullhide} kernel, but it is applied independently in each downstream
column \(a\).  Its output is the cooled \(U_{ia}\) used by the radiation and
observer-state arrays.

The log-gamma branch uses the natural logarithmic coordinate
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  In each downstream column, the local energy-space speed and
Courant factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\xi_{i+1/2,a}
&=D_{i+1/2,a}+\frac{1}{R},\\
\lambda_{i+1/2,a}
&=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}\xi_{i+1/2,a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(D_{i+1/2,a}\) contains radiative cooling, and \(1/R\) is the adiabatic
term in log-gamma coordinates.  The branch solves the resulting one-sided
implicit system from high to low energy.  The non-negative projection
\(\Pi_+(X)=\max(X,0)\) is applied only to solved cell averages; it does not mix
neighboring energy bins.  The backward-sweep coefficients are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.

The characteristic energy branch follows cooling trajectories in
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  Each \(q\) column uses the same piecewise cooling map as the
one-dimensional characteristic solver, but the source term is supplied only to
the shock-front column:
\begin{equation}
S_{ia}^{x}=S_i^x\delta_{a1}.
\end{equation}
When affine synchrotron cooling is selected, the characteristic obeys
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\gamma_e}{dR}
&=-a_{{\rm syn},a}\gamma_e^2-\frac{\gamma_e}{R},\\
a_{{\rm syn},a}
&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_a'^2}{6\pi m_e c^2\beta_{\rm sh}\Gamma_{\rm sh}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first term is synchrotron cooling in cell \(a\), and the second term is the
global adiabatic loss.  Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering
cross section.  If the loss is not affine, the tabulated array \(D_{ia}\)
supplies the piecewise characteristic map.

The optional stochastic-transport branch changes the adiabatic term from a
global shell value to a local downstream value.  It uses
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{d\ln\gamma_e}{dR}\right|_{\rm ad}
=
\frac{1}{3\beta_f}
\left(\frac{2\beta_a}{r_a}
+\left.\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial r}\right|_{a}\right),
\end{equation}
where \(r_a\) and \(\beta_a\) are the local downstream radius and velocity from
the bridged profile.  A positive stochastic-acceleration normalization adds a
zero-flux diffusion step in \(x\),
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial U_a}{\partial R}
=
\frac{\alpha_{\rm st}}{R}
\frac{\partial^2 U_a}{\partial x^2},
\end{equation}
applied half before and half after the cooling solve.  The detailed implicit
matrix and edge conditions are given in Appendix~\ref{app:electron-solvers}.
This branch is a research transport option for the \(\chi\)-resolved electron
path.  It is not a general pulsar-wind-nebula or cosmic-ray propagation model.

\subsubsection{Returned \texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved state}

The returned \(\chi\)-resolved state is the electron state seen by observer
projection.  After each radius is stored, the wrapper converts the transported
cell average \(U_{ia}\) to \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) with the coordinate Jacobian.  It
also sums over \(q\) to recover the ordinary shell-level spectrum.  For each
downstream cell, the stored projection arrays are \(r_a\), \(\Gamma_a\),
\(\Delta q\), \(B'_a\), the unabsorbed synchrotron luminosity, the local seed
field, and the cell optical depth.

The returned \(\chi\)-resolved state has a hard scope.  If the user requests only
the one-dimensional shell state, the code recomputes the shell synchrotron field
from the summed spectrum.  If direct \(\chi\)-resolved projection is requested,
the code keeps local luminosities and optical depths so that foreground
absorption can be evaluated during observer projection.  SSC, hadronic
transport, pair-cascade feedback, and reverse-shock coupling remain shell-level
or one-dimensional.  The \(\chi\)-resolved electron wrapper does not make those
channels \(q\)-local.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig3_transport_projection.pdf}
  \caption{Electron and photon state diagnostics before observer projection.
  (a) The one-dimensional \(N_e(\gamma_e,R)\) state remains visible on the radius
  grid.  (b) Radius slices of \(\gamma_e^2 dN_e/d\gamma_e\) show the transported
  electron spectrum.  (c) A two-dimensional electron run stores the downstream
  \(\chi\)-resolved volume weight.  (d) The same run stores the synchrotron
  optical-depth field used by the \(\chi\)-resolved projection.  The figure
  documents the exposed state used by the implemented solver paths; it is not a
  convergence proof for every electron transport option.}
  \label{fig:electron-transport}
\end{figure*}

Thermal electrons have a narrow solver boundary.  They are implemented only in
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} and \texttt{fullhide\_1d\_hz}; other electron solvers
reject thermal-electron transport.  Adaptive electron substeps are implemented
only for selected one-dimensional separated paths, where one full radial step is
compared with two half steps.  Joint electron-photon coupling uses fixed substeps
because the secondary source and cooling seed are iterated with the hadronic
stage.

\subsection{Photon Fields and Absorption} \label{sec:photon}

Photon fields are the radiation targets used by cooling, SSC, absorption, and
hadronic interactions.  The electron solver supplies the local synchrotron
emissivity and, when enabled, the SSC emissivity.  The photon module stores these
fields on comoving frequency grids before any observer projection mixes radii or
angles.

\subsubsection{Comoving target photon state}

The comoving target photon state is the photon density read by the next local
radiation process.  The input is the transported electron spectrum and the local
synchrotron emissivity.  The output is a seed-frequency grid, a synchrotron
photon field, an optional SSC photon field, a hadronic target field, and the seed
arrays used by synchrotron self-absorption and SSC absorption.  In a separated
electron-photon run, the target photon density is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n_{\nu'}^{\rm tar}
&=n_{\nu'}^{\rm syn}
+I_{\rm SSC}\,n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC},\\
I_{\rm SSC}
&=
\begin{cases}
1, & \text{SSC target active},\\
0, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm syn}\) and \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}\) are comoving photon
number densities per unit frequency at frequency \(\nu'\).  The switch
\(I_{\rm SSC}\) decides whether forward SSC photons enter the target field.  In
the joint branch, the \(p\gamma\), Bethe--Heitler, and \(\gamma\gamma\) survival
factors act on \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm tar}\) before the next electron solve.  Thus the
stored target field is the local photon input to cooling and feedback, not an
observer-frame spectrum.

\subsubsection{Radiation-kernel boundary}

The radiation-kernel boundary connects the stored photon state to local kernel
evaluations.  It is not a separate physical model.  The public products are the
SSC luminosity \(L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}\), the local pair-absorption factor
\(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma\gamma}\), and the external EBL factor
\(\mathcal{A}_{\rm EBL}\).  The scattering kernel, the pair-production cross
section, the EBL table, and the photon-field normalization are defined by the
physical equations below.  The compiled-kernel binding names are an ABI detail
and are recorded in Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.

\subsubsection{Radiation interpolation reuse}

Radiation interpolation gives every photon quadrature the same local spectrum
between frequency nodes.  Let \(X=\ln\nu'\) and \(Y=\ln n_{\nu'}\).  If both
endpoint photon densities are positive, the cell is reconstructed as a straight
line in \((X,Y)\), which is a local power law in frequency.  If either endpoint
is non-positive, the cell is reconstructed linearly in \(n_{\nu'}\).  This rule
returns the sampled comoving photon density used by cooling, SSC, and absorption.
Non-increasing frequency intervals are outside the supported grid domain.

\subsubsection{Composite Simpson radiation quadrature}

Composite Simpson quadrature supplies the numerical weights for uniform SSC
integrals.  The physical variables are logarithmic: \(x=\ln\nu_s'\) for seed
photons or \(x=\ln\gamma\) for electrons.  On a uniform grid with an odd number
of support points, \(x_i=x_1+(i-1)\Delta x\), the branch uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\int_{x_1}^{x_N} f(x)\,dx
&\simeq
\frac{\Delta x}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_i^{\rm S} f_i,\\
w_i^{\rm S}
&=
\begin{cases}
1, & i=1 \ \text{or}\ i=N,\\
4, & i \ \text{even},\\
2, & i \ \text{odd and}\ 1<i<N .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(f_i=f(x_i)\), and \(w_i^{\rm S}\) is only a quadrature weight.  It does
not change the electron spectrum or the seed photon density.  The rule returns
the seed-frequency and electron-energy sums used by the uniform-grid SSC
luminosity below; grids outside this Simpson layout are outside that branch.

\subsection{Uniform-grid SSC emissivity}

\subsubsection{Jones--Klein--Nishina variables}

Forward SSC emission comes from local electrons scattering the local
synchrotron photon field.  The uniform-grid branch evaluates this process on the
same logarithmic electron and seed-frequency grids used above.  For an emitted
comoving frequency \(\nu'\) and a seed frequency \(\nu_s'\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\epsilon&=\frac{h\nu'}{m_ec^2},&
\epsilon_s&=\frac{h\nu_s'}{m_ec^2},\\
b&=4\gamma\epsilon_s,&
q&=\frac{\epsilon}{b(\gamma-\epsilon)},\\
\eta&=bq=\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma-\epsilon}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\epsilon\) is the emitted photon energy in units of \(m_ec^2\),
\(\epsilon_s\) is the seed-photon energy in the same units, and \(\gamma\) is
the electron Lorentz factor.  The variables \(q\) and \(\eta\) locate the
scattering event inside the Klein--Nishina kernel.  For seed photons with
\(\nu_s'<\nu'\), the allowed electron energies satisfy
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma&>\gamma_{\rm IC},\\
\gamma_{\rm IC}
&\equiv
\frac{1}{2}\left[\epsilon+
\left(\epsilon^2+\frac{\nu'}{\nu_s'}\right)^{1/2}\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first line gives the kinematic lower limit on \(\gamma\).  The
Jones--Klein--Nishina factor used in the low-seed-frequency integral is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
G(q,\eta)
&=2q\ln q+(1+2q)(1-q)\\
&\quad+\frac{\eta^2(1-q)}{2(1+\eta)} .
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:ssc-kernel}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Uniform-grid Simpson integration}

The uniform SSC luminosity is the sum of two seed-frequency ranges.  The first
range has \(\nu_s'<\nu'\), where the full Jones--Klein--Nishina factor is used.
With \(n_s\equiv n_{\nu_s'}^{\rm tar}\), this contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm IC}(\nu')&=\frac{3}{4}ch\sigma_{\rm es}\nu',\\
L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},<}
&=C_{\rm IC}
\int_{\nu_s'<\nu'} d\ln\nu_s'\,n_s
\int d\ln\gamma\,\frac{N_e(\gamma)}{\gamma}
G(q,\eta).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section, \(n_s\) is
the target photon density, and \(N_e(\gamma)\) is the transported electron
spectrum.  The second range has \(\nu_s'\ge\nu'\).  In this range the branch
uses high-energy electron tail moments to evaluate the elastic-scattering part,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm es}(\nu_s')
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\nu_s'}{\nu'}\right)^{1/2},\\
\Phi_{\rm es}(\gamma,\nu_s')
&=\frac{\nu'}{\nu_s'}-\frac{1}{4\gamma^2},\\
L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},>}
&=C_{\rm IC}
\int_{\nu_s'\ge\nu'}d\ln\nu_s'\,n_s
\int_{\gamma>\gamma_{\rm es}}d\ln\gamma\,
\frac{N_e(\gamma)}{\gamma}
\Phi_{\rm es}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Both logarithmic integrals use the Simpson weights above.  Their sum gives the
local SSC luminosity and the photon density stored for later absorption and
feedback,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}
&=L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},<}+L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},>},\\
n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}
&=\frac{L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}}{4\pi R^2ch\nu'}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus the module returns both the emitted SSC spectrum and the comoving SSC
photon density at each radius.  It does not change the electron distribution;
electron cooling by SSC is handled by the transport step.

\subsection{Nonuniform-grid SSC emissivity}

The nonuniform-grid SSC branch uses the same scattering kernel on an electron
grid with unequal cell widths.  This branch is needed when the transported
electron spectrum is stored on adaptive or solver-specific \(\ln\gamma\) cells.
Let \(x=\ln\gamma\), and let \(N_x=dN_e/dx\).  In cell \(i\), bounded by
\(x_{i-1/2}\) and \(x_{i+1/2}\), the branch reconstructs
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_x(x)&\simeq N_{x,i}+s_i(x-x_i),\\
x_i&\equiv \frac{x_{i-1/2}+x_{i+1/2}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(s_i\) is the local minmod-limited slope.  The reconstruction supplies
the electron density sampled inside the SSC quadrature; it does not modify the
transported electron state.

The nonuniform branch changes the quadrature support, not the Compton physics.
For \(\nu_s'<\nu'\), it integrates the same \(G(q,\eta)\) kernel over every
electron cell above the kinematic lower limit,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
I_i^{<}(\nu',\nu_s')
&=\int_{x_{\rm L}}^{x_{i+1/2}}
N_x(x)\,\frac{G[q(x),\eta(x)]}{\gamma(x)^2}\,dx,\\
x_{\rm L}&=\max[x_{i-1/2},\ln\gamma_{\rm IC}].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The seed-frequency integral and the cell integral use the fixed Gauss rule
defined in Appendix~\ref{app:photon-kernels}.  For \(\nu_s'\ge\nu'\), the
branch uses precomputed high-energy moments of the reconstructed cells,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_a(>x_{\rm L})&=\int_{x_{\rm L}}^\infty N_x(x)\gamma(x)^{-a}\,dx,\\
I^{>}(\nu',\nu_s')
&=\frac{\nu'}{\nu_s'}M_2(>x_{\rm L})-\frac{1}{4}M_4(>x_{\rm L}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The returned products are again \(L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}\) and
\(n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}\).  The branch requires strictly increasing electron cell
edges and the same seed photon state \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm tar}\); it does not define
a different SSC model \citep{Jones1968,BlumenthalGould1970}.

\subsection{Finite-cell photon transfer}

\subsubsection{Shell transfer factor}

Finite-cell photon transfer converts a local optical depth into the escaping
luminosity of a uniformly emitting shell cell.  A photon born inside an
absorbing cell samples, on average, less material than a photon crossing the
same cell from the outside.  ASGARD therefore uses the cell-average factor
\(\psi(\tau)\) defined in Eq.~(\ref{eq:finite-cell-transfer}), with the
continuous limit \(\psi(0)=1\).  For synchrotron self-absorption the argument is
\[
\tau_\nu^{\rm loc}=\alpha_\nu'\Delta r',
\]
where \(\alpha_\nu'\) is the comoving absorption coefficient and \(\Delta r'\)
is the comoving shell width.  For local \(\gamma\gamma\) absorption the same
factor is evaluated with
\[
\tau_{\gamma\gamma}^{\rm loc}(\nu')
=\Delta r'\int \sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu',\tilde\nu')
n_{\tilde\nu'}^{\rm tar}\,d\tilde\nu' .
\]
Thus the transfer step changes only the escaping photon luminosity and the
stored photon density.  It does not change the photon-production kernel or the
transported electron spectrum.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\gamma\)}{gamma-gamma} shell absorption}

\subsubsection{Soft-photon cell measure}

Pair absorption removes a high-energy photon by colliding it with the local
target photon field.  The kernel first turns the seed-frequency grid into
dimensionless photon energies and cell measures.  For a photon of comoving
frequency \(\nu_1'\) and a target-frequency cell
\((\nu_j',\nu_{j+1}')\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\epsilon_1&=\frac{h\nu_1'}{m_ec^2},\\
\nu_{2,j}'&=(\nu_j'\nu_{j+1}')^{1/2},\\
\epsilon_{2,j}&=\frac{h\nu_{2,j}'}{m_ec^2},\\
\Delta\nu_j'&=\nu_{2,j}'\Delta\ln\nu' .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\epsilon_1\) and \(\epsilon_{2,j}\) are photon energies in units of
\(m_ec^2\), while \(\Delta\nu_j'\) is the frequency width represented by the
logarithmic target cell.  The target density sampled in that cell is
\(n_{\nu_{2,j}'}^{\rm tar}\).

\subsubsection{Angular pair-production kernel}

The pair-production cross section depends on the collision angle.  The shell
kernel averages over the local photon directions with midpoint angles,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta\mu&=\frac{2}{N_\mu},\\
\mu_a&=-1+\left(a-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Delta\mu,\\
z_a&=\frac{1-\mu_a}{2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
so that the pair-production invariant is
\begin{equation}
s_{aj}\equiv\epsilon_1\epsilon_{2,j}z_a .
\end{equation}
The physical threshold is \(s_{aj}>1\).  Cells with \(s_{aj}\ge s_{\max}\) are
outside the finite tabulated evaluation window; \(s_{\max}\) is not a physical
threshold.  The cross section is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_s&=(1-s^{-1})^{1/2},\\
\Xi(\beta_s)&=(3-\beta_s^4)\ln\frac{1+\beta_s}{1-\beta_s}
-2\beta_s(2-\beta_s^2),\\
\sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(s)&=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{3\sigma_{\rm es}}{16}(1-\beta_s^2)\Xi(\beta_s), & s>1,\\
0, & s\le1 .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.  The branch
\(\sigma_{\gamma\gamma}=0\) for \(s\le1\) is the physical pair-production
threshold.

\subsubsection{Shell optical-depth assembly}

The shell optical depth sums the target photons over frequency and angle.  For
a shell at radius \(R_i\) and Lorentz factor \(\Gamma_i\), with
\(\beta_i=(1-\Gamma_i^{-2})^{1/2}\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta'_{\gamma\gamma,i}&=\frac{R_i}{\eta_{\gamma\gamma}\Gamma_i},\\
W_{ia}&=\Delta\mu(1-\beta_i\mu_a),\\
n_j&=n_{\nu_{2,j}'}^{\rm tar}(R_i),\\
P_{ia}&=\sum_j\sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(s_{aj})n_j\Delta\nu_j',\\
\tau_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu_1',R_i)
&=\frac{\Delta'_{\gamma\gamma,i}}{2}
\sum_{a=1}^{N_\mu}W_{ia}P_{ia}
+\tau_{\rm ex}(\nu_1',R_i).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta'_{\gamma\gamma,i}\) is the comoving shell depth,
\(\eta_{\gamma\gamma}\) is its fixed geometry factor, \(W_{ia}\) is the angular
weight, and \(n_j\) is the target photon density in cell \(j\).  The factor
\(1/2\) performs the average over \(-1\le\mu\le1\), and
\(\tau_{\rm ex}\) adds any optical depth supplied by another local module.  The
returned finite-cell survival factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu_1',R_i)
&=\psi[\tau_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu_1',R_i)] .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This factor attenuates photons emitted throughout the absorbing cell.  It is not
the foreground-screen factor \(e^{-\tau}\).  The output is the local
\(\gamma\gamma\) attenuation used by the photon assembly
\citep{GouldSchreder1967,Svensson1987}.

\subsection{Head-on path-opacity helper}

The head-on helper supplies a path optical depth for photon-history transport.
It is not the shell-emission attenuation above.  It keeps only the head-on
invariant \(s_j^{\rm ho}\equiv\epsilon_1\epsilon_{2,j}\).  For a comoving path
length \(\Delta x'\), it computes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\gamma\gamma}^{\rm ho}(\nu_1')=
\Delta x'
\sum_j \sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(s_j^{\rm ho})
n_{\nu_{2,j}'}^{\rm tar}\Delta\nu_j' .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This helper returns a path opacity for a ray segment.  It does not replace the
angle-averaged finite-cell survival calculation used for shell emission.

\subsection{External EBL attenuation}

\subsubsection{Table boundary}

External EBL attenuation is absorption by background photons along the
cosmological path to the observer.  It acts after photons have left the local
blast-wave shells, so it is not part of the shell photon density, electron
cooling, or \(\gamma\gamma\) feedback.  The default Domínguez et al. table
\citep{Dominguez2011EBL} gives an optical-depth grid.  Its rows are photon
energies \(E_i\), and its columns are source redshifts \(z_j\).  The code uses
the corresponding observed frequencies
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_i=\frac{E_i}{h}, \qquad
\tau_{\rm EBL}(\nu_i,0)=0 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The second relation is the zero-distance boundary.  It states that a source at
\(z=0\) has no cosmological EBL path in this table.

\subsubsection{Redshift and frequency interpolation}

The EBL table gives only discrete redshifts and discrete photon energies.  For a
source redshift between \(z_j\) and \(z_{j+1}\), the code first forms the
redshift weight
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_z&=\frac{z-z_j}{z_{j+1}-z_j},\\
\tau_i(z)&=(1-\lambda_z)\tau_{ij}
+\lambda_z\tau_{i,j+1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\tau_{ij}\) is the tabulated optical depth at \((\nu_i,z_j)\).  The code
then interpolates in observed frequency.  For
\(\nu_i\le\nu_{\rm obs}\le\nu_{i+1}\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_\nu&=\frac{\nu_{\rm obs}-\nu_i}{\nu_{i+1}-\nu_i},\\
\tau_{\rm EBL}(\nu_{\rm obs},z)=
(1-\lambda_\nu)\tau_i(z)+\lambda_\nu\tau_{i+1}(z).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Below the lowest tabulated gamma-ray energy, the returned optical depth is zero.
For \(z<0\), for \(z\) above the largest tabulated redshift, or for
\(\nu_{\rm obs}\) above the largest tabulated frequency, the selected EBL model
does not define an attenuation factor.

\subsubsection{External attenuation factor}

The EBL output is a single multiplicative factor for observer-side fluxes.  It is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{A}_{\rm EBL}(\nu_{\rm obs},z)
=\exp[-\tau_{\rm EBL}(\nu_{\rm obs},z)].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This factor is an external observer-side product.  It can multiply a projected
flux after local transfer, but it does not change any local particle or photon
state.

\subsection{Observer-side photon component assembly}

\subsubsection{Component ordering}

Observer-side photon assembly is component accounting after the local radiation
modules have finished.  The forward synchrotron luminosity is always present in a
standard forward-shock run, and forward SSC is present only when the SSC solver is
active.  Reverse-shock synchrotron, reverse SSC, reverse hadronic emission, and
cross-zone inverse-Compton emission enter only when the reverse-shock state
supplies those luminosities.  Forward hadronic gamma-ray, Bethe--Heitler,
hadronic inverse-Compton, and pair-production components enter only when the
one-dimensional hadronic branch supplies them.  Thus the active set is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal C}_{\rm act}\subset
\{&{\rm FS\,syn},{\rm FS\,SSC},{\rm FS\,had},{\rm FS\,BH},
{\rm FS\,HIC},{\rm FS\,pair},\\
&{\rm RS\,syn},{\rm RS\,had},{\rm RS\,SSC},{\rm cross\,IC}\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
An absent component is not reconstructed during observer assembly.

\subsubsection{Distance and attenuation prefactor}

The distance and attenuation prefactor converts each active local luminosity into
a radial flux grid before equal-arrival-time projection.  For a channel
\(a\in{\cal C}_{\rm act}\), let \(\widetilde L_{\nu'}^a(R)\) be the local
spectral luminosity after the channel's own shell transfer.  The assembly step
uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{P}_{\rm loc}(\nu,R)&=
\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}\,
\mathcal{A}_{\rm loc}(\nu,R),\\
\Delta F_\nu^a(R)&=
\mathcal{P}_{\rm loc}(\nu,R)\,\widetilde L_{\nu'}^a(R),\\
F_\nu^{\rm rad}(R)&=
\sum_{a\in{\cal C}_{\rm act}}\Delta F_\nu^a(R).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(d_L\) is the luminosity distance and \(\mathcal{A}_{\rm loc}\) is the
local photon-survival factor returned by the pair-absorption kernel, including
active extra optical depths such as Bethe--Heitler photon loss and pair-cascade
opacity.  The EBL table factor is external to this local prefactor.  The
projection module then maps each \(\Delta F_\nu^a(R)\) and the summed
\(F_\nu^{\rm rad}(R)\) onto observer time.

\section{Reverse Shocks and Density-Jump Secondary Shocks} \label{sec:reverse-shock}

\subsection{Primary ejecta state and activation}

\subsubsection{Physical role}

The primary reverse shock heats the original ejecta shell.  It is distinct from
the forward shock, which heats the external medium.  This branch therefore needs
a finite shell width, a region-4 upstream density, and a rule for deciding when a
magnetized upstream flow can form a shock.  Its outputs are the region-3 mass,
thermal energy, volume, magnetic field, and electron-injection scale.

\subsubsection{Activation boundary}

The reverse-shock branch is active only when the input model supplies an engine
duration.  That duration fixes the initial shell width, so without it the code has
no upstream region-4 density for the reverse shock.  We write the initial ejecta
Lorentz factor as \(\Gamma_0\); the runtime field \(\eta_0\) stores the same
quantity.

\subsubsection{Ejecta shell state}

The ejecta shell state gives the baryon density and inertia seen by the reverse
shock.  For an engine duration \(\Delta t_{\rm eng}\), \asgard\ uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta_0&\equiv c\Delta t_{\rm eng},\\
M_{\rm ej}&\equiv \frac{E_{\rm iso}}{(1+\sigma)\Gamma_0c^2},\\
\Delta(R)&\equiv \max\left(\Delta_0,\frac{R}{\Gamma_0^2}\right),\\
n_4(R)&\equiv \frac{M_{\rm ej}}
{4\pi m_pR^2\Gamma_0\Delta(R)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta_0\) is the initial shell width, \(\Delta(R)\) includes radial
spreading, \(M_{\rm ej}\) is the baryonic ejecta mass, and \(n_4\) is the
upstream baryon density in region 4.  The total ejecta energy is \(E_{\rm iso}\).
When \(\sigma>0\), only the fraction \((1+\sigma)^{-1}\) is carried by baryonic
rest mass.  The unshocked shell still has matter plus ordered-field inertia, so
\asgard\ uses \(n_4\) for shock heating and electron injection, but uses
\(\tilde n_4\equiv(1+\sigma)n_4\) when the unprocessed magnetized ejecta enters
the inertia budget.

\subsubsection{Shock-front relative Lorentz factor}

The reverse-shock electron source is set by the local relative Lorentz factor at
the shock front.  If \(\Gamma_2\) is the shocked-shell Lorentz factor and
\(\Gamma_0\) is the unshocked ejecta Lorentz factor, then
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_2&=(\Gamma_2^2-1)^{1/2},\\
u_0&=(\Gamma_0^2-1)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{34}&=\Gamma_2\Gamma_0-u_2u_0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(u_2\) and \(u_0\) are the four-speeds of the shocked shell and unshocked
ejecta.  The output \(\gamma_{34}(R)\) sets the reverse-shock injection energy
scale for region-3 electrons.

\subsubsection{Magnetized activation criteria}

Magnetized ejecta form a reverse shock only after two local conditions are true.
The region-2 pressure must be high enough to push against the upstream ordered
field, and the upstream flow must be super-fast-magnetosonic in the
reverse-shock frame.  The pressure condition is written as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
P_2&\equiv
\frac{(4\Gamma_2+3)(\Gamma_2-1)n_1m_pc^2}{3},\\
\sigma_{\rm cr}&\equiv \frac{2P_2}{n_4m_pc^2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(P_2\) is the region-2 pressure estimate and \(n_1\) is the external
upstream density.  The active condition is
\begin{equation}
\sigma_{\rm cr}\ge\sigma,\qquad u_{4s}^2>\sigma ,
\end{equation}
where \(u_{4s}\) is the region-4 upstream four-speed in the reverse-shock frame.
The first inequality is a pressure criterion, while the second is the
fast-magnetosonic criterion.  For \(\sigma\le0\), these magnetized activation
checks do not delay the hydrodynamic reverse shock.

\subsubsection{Waiting-state inertia}

The waiting state lets magnetized ejecta add inertia before they add
reverse-shock heat.  Before the activation checks pass, \(dM_3/dR=0\), no
region-3 thermal energy is added, no reverse-shock electrons are injected, and
the ordered region-3 field is zero.  The decelerating shocked shell still feels
the causally connected part of the unshocked ejecta,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_{\rm w}&\equiv
M_{\rm ej}(1+\sigma)\frac{\Gamma_0}{\Gamma_0-1}f_{\rm f},\\
\beta_{\rm f}&\equiv \left(\frac{\sigma}{1+\sigma}\right)^{1/2},\\
f_{\rm f}&\equiv \min\left[
1,\,
\frac{R\beta_{\rm f}}
{\Gamma_0^2(1-\beta_4\beta_{\rm f})\beta_4\Delta(R)}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_{\rm w}\) is the waiting ejecta inertia, \(\beta_{\rm f}\) is the
fast-magnetosonic speed in units of \(c\), \(f_{\rm f}\) is the connected shell
fraction, and \(\beta_4\) is the region-4 speed.  The waiting-stage Lorentz
factor obeys
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dR}
=-\frac{u_2^2\,dM_2/dR}
{M_{\rm w}+[\epsilon_2+2(1-\epsilon_2)\Gamma_2]M_2}.
\end{equation}
This equation uses the forward swept mass \(M_2\) and radiative efficiency
\(\epsilon_2\), but it contains no reverse-shock mass or heat source.  When the
two activation conditions first become true within a radial step, \asgard\ locates
that event and starts the active reverse-shock state there.  The reverse-shock
framework follows early-afterglow thin-shell/reverse-shock treatments and the
magnetized-ejecta notation used in Zhang--Kobayashi-type calculations, while
using \vegas\ only as a jump-condition comparison backend
\citep{SariPiran1999,Kobayashi2000,ZhangKobayashi2005,PeerWijers2006,Giannios2008,Wang2025VegasAfterglow}.

\subsection{Finite-strength MHD jump solver}

\subsubsection{Cold upstream variables}

The MHD jump solver gives the compression and heat made by a magnetized reverse
shock.  The upstream ejecta is cold and carries a transverse ordered magnetic
field.  The local relative Lorentz factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
g&\equiv\gamma_{34},\qquad
\hat{\gamma}\equiv \frac{4}{3}+\frac{1}{3g}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(g\) sets the strength of the reverse shock, and \(\hat{\gamma}\) is the
local adiabatic-index proxy used in the jump relation.  For
\(\sigma>\sigma_{\rm cut}\), the downstream shock-frame four-speed is found from
\(y=u_d^2\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_3y^3+a_2y^2+a_1y+a_0&=0,\\
u_d&=y^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The coefficients \(a_i(g,\hat{\gamma},\sigma)\) are the fixed finite-strength MHD
jump coefficients given in Appendix~\ref{app:reverse-shock-technical}.  The
quantity \(\sigma_{\rm cut}\) only selects the algebraic branch used by the
implementation; it is not a physical magnetization boundary.

\subsubsection{Cubic root selection}

The cubic root selection chooses the finite positive downstream four-speed.  The
solver reduces the polynomial to a depressed cubic and takes the root
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y&=2a_c\cos\left(\psi_c-\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)-\frac{b_c}{3},\\
u_d&=y^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The symbols \(b_c\), \(a_c\), and \(\psi_c\) are the depressed-cubic variables
defined in Appendix~\ref{app:reverse-shock-technical}.  Other algebraic roots are
not used by the shell dynamics.

\subsubsection{Compression ratio}

The compression ratio follows from the upstream and downstream shock-frame
four-speeds.  Once \(u_d\) is known, the upstream four-speed and compression are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_u&=\left[(1+u_d^2)(g^2-1)\right]^{1/2}+u_dg,\\
r_\sigma&=\frac{u_u}{u_d}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(u_u\) is the upstream four-speed in the shock frame.  The output
\(r_\sigma\) compresses the ordered upstream magnetic field and sets the mass flux
through the active primary reverse shock.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Low-\(\sigma\)}{Low-sigma} hydrodynamic branch}

The low-\(\sigma\) branch uses the finite-strength hydrodynamic limit.  For
\(\sigma\le\sigma_{\rm cut}\), \asgard\ uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y_{\rm h}&=
\frac{(g-1)(\hat{\gamma}-1)^2}
{-\hat{\gamma}(\hat{\gamma}-2)(g-1)+2},\\
u_d&=y_{\rm h}^{1/2},\\
r_0&=4g .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The heat helper returns \(g-1\) for exactly unmagnetized ejecta.  For
\(0<\sigma\le\sigma_{\rm cut}\), it evaluates the finite-\(\sigma\) enthalpy and
approaches \(g-1\) as \(\sigma\) decreases.  The active dynamics do not use the
ultra-relativistic \(4g+3\) compression approximation.  This MHD jump is used only
by the primary ejecta reverse shock.  The density-jump secondary branches below
remain hydrodynamic diagnostics and do not carry ordered-field compression or
magnetic enthalpy feedback.

\subsection{Comoving region-3 field and heat state}

\subsubsection{Upstream ordered field}

The upstream ordered field is the magnetic field carried by the unshocked ejecta
before it crosses the reverse shock.  All magnetic fields in this subsection are
comoving.  For the region-4 baryon density \(n_4\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\rho_4&\equiv m_pn_4,\\
B'_4&\equiv (4\pi\sigma\rho_4c^2)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\rho_4\) is the comoving baryon mass density and \(B'_4\) is the ordered
upstream field before compression.  If \(\sigma=0\), this ordered component is
zero.

\subsubsection{Turbulent and ordered region-3 field}

The region-3 magnetic field has a turbulent part made from shocked heat and an
ordered part compressed from the ejecta field.  During active pre-crossing,
\asgard\ stores
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_{\rm t}^{\prime 2}&\equiv 8\pi\epsilon_{B,r}\frac{U_3}{V_3},\\
B'_{\rm o}&=r_\sigma B'_4 \qquad \hbox{before crossing},\\
B'_3&=\left(B_{\rm t}^{\prime 2}+B_{\rm o}^{\prime 2}\right)^{1/2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(U_3\) and \(V_3\) are the region-3 internal energy and comoving volume.
The total field \(B'_3\) is reported to the reverse-shock radiation state and is
used for the local region-3 synchrotron cooling scale.  Only the ordered part
\(B'_{\rm o}\) contributes to the magnetic enthalpy term in the bulk dynamics.
In the waiting state, no reverse-shock heat is added and \(B'_3=0\).

\subsubsection{Post-crossing ordered-field advection}

The post-crossing ordered field is carried after the reverse shock has crossed
the ejecta shell.  The stored crossing value evolves with the region-3 volume and
radius as
\begin{equation}
B'_{\rm o}(R)=B'_{{\rm o},\times}
\frac{V_{3,\times}}{V_3(R)}
\frac{R}{R_\times}.
\end{equation}
Here \(R_\times\), \(V_{3,\times}\), and \(B'_{{\rm o},\times}\) are the radius,
volume, and ordered field stored at shell crossing.  The crossing field is an
event-register state.  It is not fitted after the light curve is computed.

\subsubsection{Magnetic enthalpy inertia}

The magnetic enthalpy inertia is the part of the ordered field that pushes back on
the shell dynamics.  For the ordered component,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_B&\equiv \frac{B_{\rm o}^{\prime 2}}{8\pi},\\
E_B&\equiv p_BV_3 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The inertia added to the \(\Gamma_2\) equation is
\begin{equation}
M_B=\frac{E_B+p_BV_3}{c^2}+\frac{p_BV_3}{\Gamma_2^2c^2}.
\end{equation}
The first term is the magnetic enthalpy contribution.  The second term is the
finite-\(\Gamma_2\) pressure contribution kept by the shell equation.

\subsubsection{Heat state from the jump}

The heat state from the jump gives the thermal energy available to region-3
electrons.  \asgard\ uses the dimensionless heat
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\zeta_3&\equiv\frac{U_3}{M_3c^2},\\
\gamma_u&\equiv(1+u_u^2)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_d&\equiv(1+u_d^2)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_3\) is the shocked ejecta mass, while \(u_u\) and \(u_d\) are the
upstream and downstream shock-frame four-speeds from the jump solution.  During
active pre-crossing,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\zeta_3&=\frac{h_d-1}{\hat{\gamma}},\\
h_d&\equiv (1+\sigma)\frac{\gamma_u}{\gamma_d}-r_\sigma\sigma .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus \(\zeta_3\), not the unmagnetized estimate \(\gamma_{34}-1\), sets the
reverse-shock heat when magnetization is active.  The thermal-response factor in
the Lorentz equation is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_3=
\begin{cases}
1, & \sigma=0,\\
\zeta_3/(\gamma_{34}-1), & \sigma>0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The MHD jump therefore changes two pieces of the primary reverse-shock dynamics:
the explicit magnetic inertia \(M_B\), and the coupling between shocked-ejecta
heat and bulk motion through \(\mathcal R_3\).  After shell crossing, the code
uses the evolved ratio \(U_3/(M_3c^2)\) and sets this pre-crossing response term
to zero.

\subsection{Primary reverse-shock ODE}

\subsubsection{State vector and normalization}

The primary reverse-shock ODE evolves one contact discontinuity shared by the
forward-shocked medium and the reverse-shocked ejecta.  The state vector stores
the bulk motion and the region-3 thermodynamic state,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Y_{\rm R}&\equiv
\left(\Gamma_2,R,M_2,\frac{M_3}{M_{\rm ej}},
\frac{U_3}{M_{\rm ej}c^2},\frac{V_3}{V_0},\ldots\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the ellipsis denotes density-jump secondary-shock registers.  The forward
swept mass and the volume normalization are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dM_2}{dR}&=4\pi R^2m_pn_1,\\
V_0&\equiv \frac{M_{\rm ej}}{n_{3,0}m_p}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_1\) is the external density and \(V_0\) is only the comoving-volume
normalization used by the integrator.  The first six components therefore map to
physical derivatives as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dY_1}{dt}&=\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dt},&
\frac{dY_2}{dt}&=\frac{dR}{dt},&
\frac{dY_3}{dt}&=\frac{dM_2}{dt},\\
\frac{dY_4}{dt}&=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}}\frac{dM_3}{dt},&
\frac{dY_5}{dt}&=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}c^2}\frac{dU_3}{dt},&
\frac{dY_6}{dt}&=\frac{1}{V_0}\frac{dV_3}{dt}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The exported state is physical after this normalization is undone.

\subsubsection{Pre-crossing mass flux}

The pre-crossing mass flux tells how fast unshocked ejecta enters region 3.
Before shell crossing, the reverse shock sweeps baryons from the region-4 density
\(n_4\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dM_3}{dt}&=
4\pi R^2m_p\,\Gamma_0 n_4\,\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}
\frac{dR}{dt},\\
\frac{dR}{dt}&=\frac{\beta_2c}{1-\beta_2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}\) is the speed of the reverse-shock front relative
to the unshocked ejecta in the lab-frame radial coordinate.  This source is zero
in the waiting state and after shell crossing.
