\subsubsection{Reverse-shock speed}

The reverse-shock front speed is obtained from the upstream shock-frame
four-speed.  The lab-frame reverse-shock speed is
\begin{equation}
\beta_{\rm rs}=\beta_4-\Delta\beta_{\rm rs},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}
=\frac{u_u}
{\Gamma_0(\Gamma_0\gamma_u-u_0u_u)}.
\end{equation}
Here \(u_0=(\Gamma_0^2-1)^{1/2}\), while \(u_u\) and \(\gamma_u\) are supplied by
the MHD jump solution.  This definition ties the mass flux directly to the
shock-front jump state.

\subsubsection{Active pre-crossing Lorentz equation}

The active pre-crossing Lorentz equation balances forward swept mass, reverse
shock loading, thermal inertia, magnetic inertia, and active secondary-shock
inertia.  In this branch,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal A_{\rm pre}
&\equiv(\Gamma_2^2-1)n_1+\mathcal B_{\rm pre},\\
\mathcal B_{\rm pre}
&\equiv(\Gamma_2\gamma_{34}-\Gamma_0)\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}
\Gamma_0\tilde n_4,\\
\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dR}
=-\frac{4\pi R^2m_p\mathcal A_{\rm pre}}{\mathcal I_{\rm pre}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\tilde n_4=(1+\sigma)n_4\) is the inertial density of unprocessed
magnetized ejecta.  The first term in \(\mathcal A_{\rm pre}\) is forward-shock
deceleration, while \(\mathcal B_{\rm pre}\) is reverse-shock loading.  The
inertia denominator is
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
\mathcal I_{\rm pre}={}&
[\epsilon_2+2(1-\epsilon_2)\Gamma_2]M_2
+M_3+M_B+M_s \\
& +(1-\epsilon_3)\zeta_3M_3
+(1-\epsilon_3)\Gamma_2\mathcal R_3M_3\mathcal G_{34}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_s\) is the effective inertia of active hydrodynamic secondary
reverse-shock branches.  It is zero when no density-jump branch is active.  The
last factor is the derivative of \(\gamma_{34}\) with respect to
\(\Gamma_2\),
\begin{equation}
\mathcal G_{34}=
\Gamma_0(1-\beta_4\beta_2)
-\frac{\Gamma_0\beta_4}{\Gamma_2^2\beta_2}.
\end{equation}
This equation is used only during active pre-crossing.  Waiting and
post-crossing states use the separate equations stated above and below.

\subsubsection{Reverse radiative fraction}

The reverse radiative fraction controls how much shocked-ejecta heat remains in
the inertia term.  \asgard\ computes it with the same cooling diagnostic as the
forward dynamics,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{c,3}&=\frac{6\pi m_ec}
{\sigma_{\rm es} B_3^{\prime 2}\Gamma_2t},\\
\gamma_{m,3}&=1+
\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}\zeta_3,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_3=\epsilon_{e,r}\min\left[
1,\left(\frac{\gamma_{m,3}}{\gamma_{c,3}}\right)^{p_r-2}
\right].
\end{equation}
Here \(t\) is the source-frame time used by the dynamics, \(B'_3\) is the total
region-3 field returned by the jump and heat state, and \(p_r\), \(f_{e,r}\), and
\(\epsilon_{e,r}\) are the reverse-shock electron parameters.

\subsubsection{Thermal and volume update}

The thermal update adds shock heat and subtracts adiabatic work in region 3:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dU_3}{dt}=\zeta_3c^2\frac{dM_3}{dt}
-(\hat{\gamma}_{3}-1)\frac{U_3}{V_3}q_{V,3},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{{\rm th},3}&=1+\zeta_3,\\
\hat{\gamma}_{3}&=
\frac{4\gamma_{{\rm th},3}+1}{3\gamma_{{\rm th},3}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\gamma_{{\rm th},3}\) is the thermal Lorentz-factor proxy and
\(\hat{\gamma}_3\) is the region-3 adiabatic index.  The volume update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dV_3}{dt}&=\frac{1}{r_\sigma n_4m_p}\frac{dM_3}{dt}
+q_{V,3},\\
q_{V,3}&\equiv V_3\left(\frac{3}{R}\frac{dR}{dt}
-\frac{1}{\Gamma_2}\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dt}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first term adds newly shocked volume, and \(q_{V,3}\) gives the bulk
expansion of the already shocked region.  In the waiting state both terms are
zero.  After crossing, only the expansion term remains.

\subsubsection{Post-crossing bulk update}

The post-crossing bulk update removes the active reverse-shock source.  After
crossing, \(dM_3/dt=0\), no new reverse-shock heat is added, and the stored
\(U_3/V_3\) thermal state controls post-crossing electron cooling.  The bulk
Lorentz factor follows
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dR}
=-\frac{u_2^2\,dM_2/dR}
{[\epsilon_2+2(1-\epsilon_2)\Gamma_2]M_2+M_3+M_B+M_s}.
\end{equation}
This equation keeps the swept forward mass, shocked ejecta mass, magnetic
inertia, and active secondary-shock inertia in the denominator.  It does not add
new region-3 mass.

\subsection{Crossing and activation event splitting}

\subsubsection{Physical events}

The reverse-shock integrator has to stop at physical branch changes.  A
magnetized ejecta shell can first be in a waiting state, because the pressure and
fast-mode criteria for a reverse shock are not yet satisfied.  Once these two
criteria are satisfied, the code starts the active reverse shock.  The active
branch then ends when all ejecta mass has crossed the reverse shock.  The event
split therefore separates waiting, active pre-crossing, and post-crossing
evolution.  It prevents one Runge--Kutta step from using source terms from two
different physical branches.

\subsubsection{Mass-fraction independent variable}

The mass-fraction variable measures how much ejecta has passed through the
reverse shock.  During the active pre-crossing branch, \asgard\ uses
\(q\equiv M_3/M_{\rm ej}\) as the step variable, so shell crossing is the fixed
endpoint \(q=1\).  If the physical right-hand side gives \(dY/dt\), the
Runge--Kutta stages use
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dY}{dq}
&=\frac{dY/dt}{d(M_3/M_{\rm ej})/dt},\\
\frac{dt}{dq}
&=\left[\frac{d(M_3/M_{\rm ej})}{dt}\right]^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Y\) is the reverse-shock state vector and \(t\) is the source-frame time.
This coordinate lands directly on \(q=1\).  The post-crossing branch starts from
that state and therefore receives no new shocked ejecta mass.

\subsubsection{Log-time single-branch stages}

The log-time variable gives the integrator a scale-free step size away from an
event.  For a branch whose equations do not change inside the step, \asgard\
evolves \(s=\ln(t/t_0)\), so that
\begin{equation}
\frac{dY}{ds}=t\frac{dY}{dt}.
\end{equation}
Here \(t_0\) is the time at the start of that stage.  The same log-time form is
used for waiting, active pre-crossing, and post-crossing intervals, but only when
the interval does not cross an activation or crossing event.

\subsubsection{Event-state register}

The event-state register is an algorithmic record, not a new blast-wave
variable.  It selects one of three equation sets: waiting, active pre-crossing,
or post-crossing.  It also carries the current total region-3 magnetic field
\(B'_3\) and the frozen crossing vector \(\mathbf C_\times\).  The register is
not fitted to the data and is not projected as an observer component.  Its output
is a diagnostic of which physical branch produced the local reverse-shock state.

\subsubsection{Crossing-state write}

The crossing-state vector stores the physical state at shell crossing.  Before
crossing it has no physical content.  At \(q=1\), the first post-crossing
right-hand-side evaluation writes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf C_\times={}&(t_\times,R_\times,e_{3,\times},\Gamma_{2,\times},
U_{3,\times},V_{3,\times},\\
&M_{3,\times},\gamma_{m,\times},B'_{{\rm o},\times})\\
={}&\left(t,R,\frac{U_3}{V_3},\Gamma_2,U_3,V_3,
M_3,\gamma_{m,3},B'_{\rm o}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(B'_{{\rm o},\times}\) is the ordered reverse-shock field at crossing.
Later post-crossing steps read this vector and do not rewrite it.  The vector
sets the post-crossing thermal state, the reverse-electron injection scale at
crossing, and the ordered-field advection used after the ejecta has been fully
processed.

\subsubsection{Crossing and activation split}

The crossing split keeps one integration interval from mixing active and
post-crossing equations.  If a pre-crossing log-time interval reaches \(q=1\),
\asgard\ locates the event, evolves to that point with the active reverse-shock
equations, writes \(\mathbf C_\times\) once, and evolves the remaining part with
the post-crossing equations.  The waiting-to-active transition uses the same
logic.  A waiting trial is first evaluated at the target time.  If the pressure
and fast-mode criteria become true inside the interval, the activation time is
located before active reverse-shock source terms are applied.  Thus waiting
evolution adds inertia but no region-3 heat, no reverse electrons, and no
ordered region-3 field.

\subsubsection{Single-branch refinement}

The single-branch refinement test checks the RK solution only inside one active
branch.  It compares two successive RK resolutions through
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\rm RK,RS}=\max_a\frac{2|a_N-a_{2N}|}{a_N+a_{2N}},
\end{equation}
with \(a\in\{\Gamma_2,R,M_2\}\) for log-time steps and
\(a\in\{\Gamma_2,R,M_2,t\}\) for the \(q\)-step.  Event points are excluded from
this order check because the physical equations change at the event.

\subsection{Reverse electron injection contract}

The reverse-electron source is tied to newly shocked ejecta mass.  It is not a
source-frame time rate.  During a radial interval, the source first computes how
many ejecta electrons have just crossed the reverse shock,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Q_R^{\rm r}(R)&\equiv
f_{e,r}\frac{1}{m_p}\frac{dM_3}{dR},\\
\gamma_m^{\rm r}(R)&=1+
\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}\left[\gamma_{34}(R)-1\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Q_R^{\rm r}\) is the radial injection rate, \(f_{e,r}\) is the fraction of
shocked ejecta electrons placed in the nonthermal population, and
\(\gamma_{34}\) is the relative Lorentz factor between the shocked material and
the unshocked ejecta.  The region-3 heat variable
\(\zeta_3=U_3/(M_3c^2)\) remains a stored thermal diagnostic and enters
post-crossing cooling; the transported electron source uses the shock-front
energy scale above.

The reverse-electron spectrum is normalized on the natural-log grid
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  For one cell in \(x\), \asgard\ uses the kinetic-energy power
law
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal C_{\max}^{\rm r}(\gamma_e)&=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma_e\le\gamma_{\max}^{\rm r},\\
\exp(1-\gamma_e/\gamma_{\max}^{\rm r}),&
\gamma_e>\gamma_{\max}^{\rm r},
\end{cases}\\
Q_x^{\rm r}(x;R)&=
\begin{cases}
A_r(R)\,\gamma_e(\gamma_e-1)^{-p_r}
\mathcal C_{\max}^{\rm r}(\gamma_e),
& \gamma_e>\gamma_m^{\rm r},\\
0, & \gamma_e\le\gamma_m^{\rm r}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The normalization \(A_r\) is fixed by
\(\int Q_x^{\rm r}\,dx=Q_R^{\rm r}\).  This form keeps the kinetic factor
\(\gamma_e-1\) at mildly relativistic energies and includes the Jacobian
\(d\gamma_e/dx=\gamma_e\).  If a log-four-velocity transport coordinate is
selected internally, the source is transformed with the corresponding
\(dx/dy\).  Reverse electron transport may use either \texttt{fullhide\_1d} or
\texttt{dg\_1d}; both paths use the same radial source.  The reverse
synchrotron luminosity is computed with the same selected synchrotron kernel as
the forward shock.  Optional reverse SSC and cross-zone IC are added only during
observer assembly.  Reverse hadronic full-chain flags reuse the formal
one-dimensional hadronic kernels with the reverse magnetic field, reverse seed
photons, reverse shell energy, and reverse baryon target density.

\subsection{Reverse radius-output sweep}

The reverse radius-output sweep inserts the electron source on each saved radial
interval.  For the interval \([R_{i-1},R_i]\), \asgard\ reconstructs the local
state at the shell midpoint,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar\Gamma_{2,i}&=\frac{\Gamma_{2,i}+\Gamma_{2,i-1}}{2},\\
\bar B'_{3,i}&=\frac{B'_{3,i}+B'_{3,i-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\bar\Gamma_{2,i}\) sets the local shock energy and \(\bar B'_{3,i}\) sets
the local synchrotron loss rate.  The midpoint relative Lorentz factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar u_{2,i}&=(\bar\Gamma_{2,i}^2-1)^{1/2},\\
u_0&=(\Gamma_0^2-1)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{34,i}
&=\frac{\bar\Gamma_{2,i}^2+\Gamma_0^2-1}
{\Gamma_0\bar\Gamma_{2,i}+\bar u_{2,i}u_0}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\Gamma_0\) is the initial ejecta Lorentz factor.  The injection break and
synchrotron-limited cutoff for this radial interval are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{m,i}^{\rm r}&=
1+\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\gamma_{34,i}-1),\\
\gamma_{\max,i}^{\rm r}
&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8e^3\bar B'_{3,i})^{1/2}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The factor \((p_r-2)/(p_r-1)\) is evaluated with the reverse-shock index
\(p_r\).  The cutoff \(\gamma_{\max,i}^{\rm r}\) is the synchrotron-loss limit
set by the local total region-3 field.

The crossing radius sets the last interval with fresh reverse electrons.  If
shell crossing lies inside the output interval, only the pre-crossing part
receives a source:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_{\rm inj}&=\min(R_i,R_\times)-R_{i-1},\\
\mathcal Q^{\rm inj}_{R,i}
&=f_{e,r}\frac{M_{3,{\rm hi}}-M_{3,i-1}}
{m_p\Delta R_{\rm inj}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_{3,{\rm hi}}=M_{3,\times}\) when \(R_i>R_\times\), and
\(M_{3,{\rm hi}}=M_{3,i}\) otherwise.  Intervals entirely after crossing set
\(\mathcal Q^{\rm inj}_{R,i}=0\).  Post-crossing transport can still cool and
expand the stored electron spectrum, but it does not inject new reverse-shock
electrons.

\subsection{Reverse cooling assembly}

The reverse cooling assembly converts local radiation losses into a radial
energy-space speed.  The transport solver uses the positive coefficient
\(D_i\equiv-\gamma_i^{-1}(d\gamma_i/dR)_{\rm rad}\).  For synchrotron-only
cooling,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm syn}(R)&=
\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_3^{\prime 2}}{6\pi m_ec^2\,\beta_2\Gamma_2},\\
D_i^{\rm syn}&=a_{\rm syn}\gamma_i,\qquad
\left.\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}\right|_{\rm syn}
=-D_i^{\rm syn}\gamma_i .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section used throughout
the manuscript, \(B'_3\) is the total reverse-shock magnetic field, and
\(\beta_2\Gamma_2\) converts the comoving cooling rate to a radial rate.  This
mode uses no inverse-Compton cooling term.

The local IC/KN mode adds inverse-Compton losses from the reverse synchrotron seed
field.  If \(A_i^{\rm IC}\) is the local comoving IC loss coefficient in electron
cell \(i\), then
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
D_i^{\rm IC}&=
\left(a_{\rm syn}+\frac{A_i^{\rm IC}}{\beta_2\Gamma_2c}\right)\gamma_i,\\
\left.\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}\right|_{\rm rad}
&=-D_i^{\rm IC}\gamma_i .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This mode is local in the electron and photon grids; it changes the cooling rate
before either the finite-volume or DG update is called.

The Nakar-style Compton mode writes inverse-Compton cooling as a multiplicative
factor on the synchrotron loss.  \asgard\ defines
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e_{\rm N}'&=4\Gamma_2^2 n_4m_pc^2,\\
Y_i^{\rm N}&=\frac{A_i^{\rm N}}{4\pi R^2c\,e_{\rm N}'},\\
D_i^{\rm N}&=a_{\rm syn}(1+Y_i^{\rm N})\gamma_i .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_4\) is the unshocked ejecta baryon density and \(A_i^{\rm N}\) is the
seed-synchrotron power integrated over the Thomson part of the spectrum.  The
same Compton factor reduces the local injection cutoff to
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{\max,{\rm IC}}^{\rm r}
\equiv \gamma_{\max}^{\rm r}(1+Y_{\max}^{\rm N})^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
All three modes return a positive \(D_i\).  The next transport step uses that
coefficient together with the reverse-shell expansion rate.

\subsection{Reverse finite-volume transport and substeps}

The reverse finite-volume step moves the electron spectrum through energy space
as the shell expands and radiates.  The adiabatic coefficient is \(a_{\rm ad}=1/R\)
before shell crossing.  After crossing, it is measured from the stored region-3
volume:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm ad}&=\frac{1}{3}\frac{d\ln V_3}{dR},\\
a_i^{\rm ad}&\simeq
\frac{\ln(V_{3,i}/V_{3,i-1})}{3(R_i-R_{i-1})}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(V_{3,\times}\) used when the output interval contains the crossing point.
Here \(a_{\rm ad}\) gives the radial adiabatic cooling rate of the stored
reverse-electron spectrum.

The conservative coordinate \(y\) is the log-four-velocity coordinate used by the
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} branch.  If \(x=\ln\gamma_e\) and
\(j_y=dx/dy\), the face speed is
\begin{equation}
v_{y,i+1/2}=
\frac{D_{i+1/2}+a_{\rm ad}}{j_{y,i+1/2}},
\end{equation}
and the finite-volume update advances
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial N_y}{\partial R}
+
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
\left[-v_yN_y\right]
=Q_y ,
\end{equation}
where \(N_y\) is the cell density in the conservative energy coordinate,
\(Q_y\) is the reverse-shock source in that coordinate, and \(D_{i+1/2}\) is the
face value of the selected radiative cooling coefficient.  This is the same
face-flux equation as the forward shell solver, with the forward source replaced
by the reverse radial source.

The radial substep size is set by the fastest local cooling and expansion rate.
For the primary reverse shock in the \texttt{fullhide\_1d} branch, \asgard\ uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{m,i}&=\frac{R_i+R_{i-1}}{2},\\
a_i^{\rm p}&=\frac{2}{3R_{m,i}},\\
\Delta R_{\rm cand}
&=\frac{C_R}{a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}+a_i^{\rm p}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(R_{m,i}\) is the radial midpoint, \(a_i^{\rm p}\) is the primary
adiabatic estimate, and \(C_R\) is a fixed radial-step control.  The secondary
reservoir and reacceleration branches use
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_i^{\rm s}&=\frac{1}{R_{i-1}},\\
\Delta R_{\rm cand}
&=\frac{C_R}{a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}+a_i^{\rm s}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a_i^{\rm s}\) is the corresponding secondary-branch adiabatic estimate.
The accepted substep count for the \texttt{fullhide\_1d} branch is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_c&=\left\lfloor\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{\Delta R_{\rm cand}}\right\rfloor,\\
N_{\rm sub}&=\max\left[N_{\min},\min(N_{\max},N_c)\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The \texttt{dg\_1d} branch uses \(N_{\rm DG}\) substeps and the DG transport
formulas given above.  The quantities \(C_R\), \(N_{\min}\), \(N_{\max}\), and
\(N_{\rm DG}\) are fixed transport controls, not fitted physical parameters.

\subsection{Post-crossing characteristic remap}

The post-crossing characteristic remap evolves only the electrons that were
already shocked before shell crossing.  After \(R_\times\), the reverse-shock
source is set to zero.  The code stores the spectrum as
\(N_x=dN_e/dx\) with \(x=\ln\gamma_e\), traces each current cell edge back to
the crossing-time map, and conservatively places the old electron content into
the current grid.  This step changes electron energies by cooling and expansion;
it does not add new particles.

\subsubsection{Synchrotron affine map}

The synchrotron characteristic map is analytic in inverse energy.  For
\(u\equiv1/\gamma_e\), synchrotron cooling plus expansion gives
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_{\rm syn}+a_{\rm ad}u .
\end{equation}
If \(u_{\rm now}\) is a current cell edge and the radial step is \(\Delta R\),
the edge at the previous radius is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\eta_u&=\frac{a_{\rm syn}}{a_{\rm ad}},\qquad
E_u=\exp(-a_{\rm ad}\Delta R)
\quad(a_{\rm ad}\ne0),\\
u_{\rm prev}&=
\begin{cases}
u_{\rm now}-a_{\rm syn}\Delta R, & a_{\rm ad}=0,\\
(u_{\rm now}+\eta_u)E_u-\eta_u, & a_{\rm ad}\ne0 .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The code converts \(u_{\rm prev}\) back to \(x_{\rm prev}=-\ln u_{\rm prev}\).
It then composes this edge with the map stored at crossing.  The output is the
old \(x\)-interval that feeds the current cell.

\subsubsection{IC/KN piecewise-affine map}

The IC/KN characteristic map allows the cooling coefficient to vary with electron
energy.  In one energy cell \(h\), the code fits the inverse-energy speed with
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=\alpha_h+\beta_hu .
\end{equation}
The same affine solution is then applied until the traced edge reaches a cell
boundary; after that point the next cell coefficients are used.  This is a
piecewise characteristic map for radiative cooling, not a new emission model.

The conservative remap uses the traced edges to integrate the old log-energy
content over the old interval:
\begin{equation}
N_{x,i}^{\rm new}
=\frac{1}{\Delta x_i}
\int_{x_{{\rm old},i-1/2}}^{x_{{\rm old},i+1/2}}
N_x^{\rm old}(x)\,dx .
\end{equation}
The low-energy boundary is closed during this post-crossing remap.  The final
diagnostic \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) is reconstructed from \(N_x\) using
\(dN_e/d\gamma_e=N_x/\gamma_e\).  Thus the post-crossing branch preserves the
stored electron content subject only to cooling, expansion, and the finite
transport grid.

\subsection{Secondary-reservoir electron transport}

The secondary-reservoir electron path records electrons made by density-jump
reverse-shock branches.  Each branch \(j\) has its own processed mass \(M_j\),
comoving volume \(V_j\), magnetic field \(B'_j\), injection scale
\(\gamma_{m,j}\), and electron spectrum.  The branch is a diagnostic reservoir
tied to a density-jump event.  It uses the same reverse-electron transport
machinery as the primary reverse shock, but it is not a new independent emission
model.

\subsubsection{Reservoir source and volume loss}

The reservoir source adds electrons only when branch \(j\) processes new mass.
On the output interval \([R_{i-1},R_i]\), the radial source and volume coefficient
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal Q^{s}_{R,j}
&=f_{e,r}\frac{M_{j,i}-M_{j,i-1}}
{m_p(R_i-R_{i-1})},\\
a_{V,j}&=
\frac{\ln(V_{j,i}/V_{j,i-1})}
{3(R_i-R_{i-1})}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal Q^{s}_{R,j}\) is the electron source per radius and \(a_{V,j}\)
is the reservoir expansion coefficient.  If either endpoint volume is not
positive, no volume-loss coefficient is applied on that interval.  The same
finite-volume or DG reverse-electron step then advances the branch spectrum with
synchrotron cooling in \(B'_j\).  A branch with no positive processed mass leaves
its spectrum and luminosity at zero.

\subsubsection{Reservoir radiation product}

The reservoir radiation product is computed from each evolved branch spectrum.
For every active branch, the code evaluates the same synchrotron and SSA kernels
used elsewhere in the paper.  The reported observer-frame absorption break is
\begin{equation}
\nu_a^{\rm obs}=
\frac{\nu_a'}
{\Gamma_2(1-\beta_2)(1+z)} .
\end{equation}
Here \(\nu_a'\) is the comoving SSA frequency of one reservoir, and
\(\Gamma_2(1-\beta_2)\) gives the on-axis Doppler denominator used by this
diagnostic output.  The branch-summed secondary luminosity is then
\begin{equation}
L_{\nu'}^{\prime,s}=\sum_jL_{\nu',j}^{\prime,s}.
\end{equation}
This product is a synchrotron luminosity history for observer assembly.  It does
not add a separate secondary-reservoir SSC, hadronic, or pair-feedback branch.

\subsection{Reacceleration diagnostic}

The reacceleration diagnostic follows old electrons when a later density-jump
branch shocks material from an earlier reservoir.  The diagnostic has a fixed
order.  It transfers only the parent mass that is still available, boosts the
transferred spectrum by the new shock compression and relative motion, applies a
diffusive-shock acceleration remap, and then injects only the remaining fresh
mass.  This ordering separates reprocessed electrons from newly shocked
electrons in the same branch spectrum.

\subsubsection{Parent mass transfer}

The parent mass transfer is limited by the stored reservoir mass.  If branch
\(j\) has parent label \(\pi_j\), the transferred mass and seed spectrum are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}&=\min(\Delta M_j,M_{\pi_j}^{\rm av}),\\
f_j^{\rm tr}&=\frac{\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}}{M_{\pi_j}^{\rm av}},\\
N_{\log,j}^{s}(\gamma)&=f_j^{\rm tr}N_{\log,\pi_j}(\gamma).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_{\pi_j}^{\rm av}\) is the parent mass still available for transfer,
\(\Delta M_j\) is the mass processed by branch \(j\) in this interval, and
\(N_{\log,j}^{s}\) is the seed log-energy spectrum for the new branch.  A
transfer is made only when the branch is dissipative, \(\gamma_{m,j}>1\) and
\(\Gamma_{43,j}>1\), and the parent reservoir has positive available mass.
Otherwise the branch receives no parent seed spectrum.

\subsubsection{Compression boost}

The compression boost moves the transferred spectrum to the energy scale of the
new shock.  The branch boost is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_j^{c}&=
\begin{cases}
r_j, & r_j>1,\\
1, & r_j\le1,
\end{cases}\\
g_j&=\Gamma_{43,j}(r_j^{c})^{1/3},\\
N_{\log,j}^{b}(\gamma)
&=N_{\log,j}^{s}(\gamma/g_j).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(r_j\) is the stored compression factor, \(r_j^c\) is the compression used
by the diagnostic, and \(\Gamma_{43,j}\) is the relative Lorentz factor across
the secondary shock.  The shift conserves particle number in log-energy space.
If the stored compression is not greater than unity, the compression part of the
boost is not applied.

\subsubsection{Diffusive-shock acceleration remap}

The diffusive-shock acceleration remap converts the boosted seed spectrum into a
power-law tail.  For \(p_r>2\), \asgard\ uses
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN_{j}^{a}}{d\gamma}
=(p_r-1)\gamma^{-p_r}
\int_1^\gamma
\frac{dN_j^{b}}{d\tilde\gamma}
\tilde\gamma^{p_r-1}\,d\tilde\gamma,
\qquad p_r>2 .
\end{equation}
Here \(N_j^b\) is the boosted seed spectrum and \(N_j^a\) is the reaccelerated
spectrum.  The lower limit \(\tilde\gamma=1\) states that the integral starts at
the physical low-energy edge of the electron grid.  The condition \(p_r>2\) is a
hard boundary of this diagnostic branch.

\subsubsection{Energy accounting}

The energy accounting records how much electron energy is carried by the seed and
reaccelerated spectra.  The branch energies are computed from the same
log-energy content:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_j^{s}&=\int(\gamma-1)m_ec^2N_{\log,j}^{s}\,d\ln\gamma,\\
E_j^{a}&=\int(\gamma-1)m_ec^2N_{\log,j}^{a}\,d\ln\gamma .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(E_j^s\) is the seed energy and \(E_j^a\) is the reaccelerated energy.
The available parent mass is reduced by \(\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}\), and the new
branch gains that transferred mass.  The remaining dissipative mass,
\(\Delta M_j-\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}\), is inserted through the fresh kinetic source
defined above.  The output arrays \(E_j^s\) and \(E_j^a\) are diagnostics of the
reprocessing budget; they are not extra radiative components.

\subsection{Density-jump secondary-shock solver}

Secondary reverse shocks are diagnostic branches tied to density-jump events.
They are not independent emission components.  Each branch asks a local question:
can the rising side of an external-density bump drive a new reverse shock into
material that has already passed through the forward shock?  The branch is active
only when the new shock produces more thermal energy than adiabatic compression
of the upstream reservoir would produce.

\subsubsection{Event-window density}

The event-window density selects the rising side of one Gaussian bump.  For a
Gaussian jump \(j\), \asgard\ defines
\(\Delta R_j=w_jR_j\) and uses only the rising side:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_j-\eta_J\Delta R_j&\le R<R_j,\\
x_j&=\frac{R-R_j}{\Delta R_j},\\
n_j^{\rm ex}(R)&=n_{\rm b}(R)(f_j-1)\exp(-x_j^2/2).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\rm b}\) is the baseline external density, \(f_j\) is the jump factor,
and \(\eta_J\) is the fixed event-window control used by the scanner.  The total
upstream density entering the contact problem is
\begin{equation}
n_1=n_{\rm b}+\sum_{\ell}n_\ell^{\rm ex},
\end{equation}
while \(n_j^{\rm ex}\) is the excess density selected for branch \(j\).  Both
quantities are number densities in \({\rm cm^{-3}}\), not dimensionless
multipliers.

\subsubsection{Secondary inertia contribution}

The secondary inertia contribution adds active density-jump branches to the bulk
equation.  For the relativistically hot secondary gas, \asgard\ uses
\(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}=4/3\).  Let \((M_s^b,U_s,V_s)\) be the summed baryonic mass,
thermal energy, and volume of the active secondary branches.  Their comoving
pressure and enthalpy enter
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p'_s&=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)\frac{U_s}{V_s},\\
M_s&=\frac{M_s^bc^2+U_s+p'_sV_s}{c^2}
+\frac{p'_sV_s}{\Gamma_2^2c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_s\) is the effective secondary inertia used in the primary reverse-shock
bulk equation.  If no secondary branch has positive mass, heat, and volume, this
term is zero.

\subsubsection{Region-4 upstream state}

The region-4 upstream state is the material into which the secondary reverse
shock propagates.  For the first secondary branch, this upstream is the
pre-bump forward-shock downstream state:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n_{\rm pre}=n_1-n_j^{\rm ex},
\qquad
r_2=4\Gamma_2,
\\
n'_4=r_2 n_{\rm pre}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\rm pre}\) removes the selected excess density from the total upstream
density, and \(r_2=4\Gamma_2\) is the relativistic strong-shock density factor
used by this diagnostic branch.  The corresponding internal energy and pressure
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e'_4&=(\Gamma_2-1)n'_4m_pc^2,
\qquad
p'_4&=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)e'_4 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
If a previous secondary branch has finite mass, energy, and volume, that parent
branch supplies the local upstream state instead:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n'_4&=\frac{M_p}{m_pV_p},\\
e'_4&=\frac{U_p}{V_p},
\qquad
p'_4=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)e'_4 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_p\), \(U_p\), and \(V_p\) are the parent branch mass, internal energy,
and comoving volume.  This chained upstream state is used only when all three are
positive.

\subsubsection{Contact pressure root}

The contact pressure root sets the Lorentz factor of the secondary contact
surface.  For a trial contact Lorentz factor \(g_c\), the pressure behind the
new forward shock in the bump is
\begin{equation}
p'_2(g_c)=
\hat\gamma_{\rm h}(g_c^2-1)n_1m_pc^2,
\end{equation}
while the pressure behind the secondary reverse shock is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p'_3(g_c)&=
p'_4+\hat\gamma_{\rm h}(\Gamma_{43}^2-1)(e'_4+p'_4),\\
\beta_c&=(1-g_c^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\Gamma_{43}&=g_c\Gamma_4(1-\beta_c\beta_4).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Gamma_4\) and \(\beta_4\) describe the region-4 upstream state, and
\(\Gamma_{43}\) is the relative Lorentz factor between the contact and region 4.
The solver searches the physical interval \(1<g_c<\Gamma_4\) for
\(p'_2(g_c)-p'_3(g_c)=0\).

The secondary branch starts only if the contact solution gives a compressive
shock and a positive excess heat source.  The local source test is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\chi&=\frac{n'_3}{n'_4},\\
e'_{3,{\rm hot}}&=3p'_3,\\
e'_{\rm ad}&=e'_4\chi^{4/3},\\
S_j&=e'_{3,{\rm hot}}-e'_{\rm ad}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The event scanner opens or closes branch \(j\) where \(S_j\) changes sign inside
the selected rising side of the density bump.  If \(S_j\le0\), the interval adds
no new secondary shocked mass or heat.

\subsubsection{Shock-frame compression}

The shock-frame compression gives the density jump across the secondary reverse
shock.  For a trial laboratory shock speed \(\beta_s\), the upstream and
downstream velocities in the shock frame are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{4s}&=\frac{\beta_4-\beta_s}{1-\beta_4\beta_s},
\qquad
\beta_{3s}=\frac{\beta_c-\beta_s}{1-\beta_c\beta_s},\\
u_{is}&=\Gamma_{is}|\beta_{is}|\quad (i=3,4).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The stored diagnostic \(\beta_{\rm rs}\) is this laboratory-frame root
\(\beta_s\).  Baryon-flux continuity gives the compression
\begin{equation}
\chi\equiv\frac{n'_3}{n'_4}=\frac{u_{4s}}{u_{3s}} .
\end{equation}
The solver searches only compressive states with \(\chi>1\).  The accepted root
also satisfies the shock-frame momentum-flux equation
\begin{equation}
w'_4u_{4s}^2+p'_4=w'_3u_{3s}^2+p'_3,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
w'_4&=n'_4m_pc^2+e'_4+p'_4,\\
e'_3&=\frac{p'_3}{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1},
\qquad
w'_3=\chi n'_4m_pc^2+e'_3+p'_3 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(w'_4\) and \(w'_3\) are the upstream and downstream enthalpy densities.
This closure assumes relativistically hot gas in the secondary diagnostic
branch.  It is not a general finite-temperature Taub solve.  Once contact
balance fixes \(p'_3\), mass and momentum flux determine only the shock speed and
compression.

\subsubsection{Dissipated-energy source}

The dissipated-energy source decides whether branch \(j\) receives new shocked
mass and heat.  If no compressive momentum root exists, the branch is inactive.
If a root exists, new heat is deposited only when
\begin{equation}
\Delta e'_j=e'_{3,{\rm hot}}-e'_4\chi^{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}}>0,
\end{equation}
which subtracts the adiabatically compressed upstream internal energy from the
newly shocked hot-gas state.  If this inequality is not satisfied, the branch can
only carry its already stored material through adiabatic expansion.

The active branch then adds mass, heat, and volume through radial source terms.
The shocked-volume and expansion-volume terms are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dV_{q,j}}{dR}&=\frac{1}{n'_3m_p}\frac{dM_j}{dR},\\
\frac{dV_{e,j}}{dR}
&=V_j\frac{d}{dR}\ln\left(\frac{R^3}{\Gamma_2}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(q\) denotes newly shocked volume and \(e\) denotes expansion of stored
branch material.  The branch source terms are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dM_j}{dR}
&=4\pi R^2n'_4m_p\Gamma_2
\frac{\beta_2-\beta_{\rm rs}}{\beta_c},\\
\frac{dU_j}{dR}
&=\Delta e'_j\frac{dV_{q,j}}{dR}
-(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)\frac{U_j}{V_j}
\frac{dV_{e,j}}{dR},\\
\frac{dV_j}{dR}
&=\frac{dV_{q,j}}{dR}+\frac{dV_{e,j}}{dR}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These equations add processed mass, dissipated internal energy, and comoving
volume to branch \(j\).

The dissipated-energy diagnostic also records the injection scale of the newly
heated electrons.  For \(p_r>2\),
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{m,j}=1+
\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{\Delta e'_j}{n'_3m_ec^2}.
\end{equation}
The branch is accepted only when this injection scale stays below the local
synchrotron cutoff.  The ODE state stores the cumulative branch mass, heat,
volume, dissipated energy, and \(\gamma_{m,j}\)-weighted dissipated energy.  These
stored quantities are diagnostics and inputs to the branch electron source; they
do not add a separate inertia term beyond \(M_s\).

\subsection{Secondary event windows and observer diagnostics}

The secondary event windows locate where a density bump can drive a hot
secondary reverse shock.  A branch is active only when the newly shocked gas is
hotter than the same gas would be after adiabatic compression.  ASGARD tests this
condition with
\begin{equation}
S_j(R)=e'_{3,j}-e'_{4,j}\chi_j^{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}}.
\end{equation}
Here \(e'_{3,j}\) is the shocked hot-gas energy density, and
\(e'_{4,j}\chi_j^{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}}\) is the upstream energy density after
adiabatic compression by the shock-frame compression \(\chi_j\).  The branch is
open where \(S_j>0\).  It is closed where \(S_j\le0\).

\subsubsection{Event-root scanner}

The event-root scanner turns the sign of \(S_j\) into start and end radii.  For
each adjacent pair of stored primary reverse-shock states, it scans the part of
the rising bump contained in
\([R_j-\eta_J\Delta R_j,R_j)\).  The scan uses no wider than
\(\Delta R_j/N_J^{\rm scan}\) per bracket.  Inside each bracket the code
linearly interpolates the ODE state \(Y\), the contact Lorentz factor
\(\Gamma_2\), and the observer time \(t_{\rm obs}\).  A sign change in \(S_j\)
is then bisected in radius.  The output of this step is the event record
\((R_{j,\rm start},R_{j,\rm end},t_{j,\rm start},t_{j,\rm end})\).  If a branch
is still open at the last sampled state, the endpoint is the last stored
physical state.

\subsubsection{Dimensional branch state}

The branch state records the mass, heat, and volume produced inside each event
window.  The ODE variables are dimensionless, so the output layer restores their
physical units as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_j&=Y_{m,j}M_{\rm ej},\\
U_j&=Y_{u,j}M_{\rm ej}c^2,\\
V_j&=Y_{v,j}V_0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Y_{m,j}\), \(Y_{u,j}\), and \(Y_{v,j}\) are the stored branch entries,
\(M_{\rm ej}\) is the ejecta mass scale, and \(V_0\) is the fixed reverse-shock
volume scale used by the ODE state.  These quantities are public diagnostics.
They also set the secondary-reservoir electron source when that reservoir is
enabled.

\subsubsection{Dissipation-weighted diagnostics}

The shock diagnostics are weighted by newly dissipated energy.  Old inactive
mass should not set the average contact Lorentz factor or injection scale at a
later output radius.  With \(E^q_{j,i}\) denoting the cumulative shocked energy
in branch \(j\) at radius \(R_i\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta E_{j,i}&=E^q_{j,i}-E^q_{j,i-1},
\qquad
\langle Q\rangle_i&=
\frac{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}Q_{j,i}}
{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Q_{j,i}\) can be the contact Lorentz factor, the shock Lorentz factor,
the compression ratio, or the injection Lorentz factor.  The denominator is the
new heat added at the current output radius.  If no branch dissipates new heat,
the code leaves the weighted diagnostic at its initialized value.

\subsubsection{Combined secondary state}

The combined secondary state is the branch sum returned to the one-dimensional
output layer.  ASGARD first adds the physical reservoirs,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_s^b&=\sum_j M_j,\\
U_s&=\sum_j U_j,\\
V_s&=\sum_j V_j,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and then computes the effective magnetic field, pressure, and enthalpy density:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B'_s&=\left(8\pi\epsilon_{B,r}\frac{U_s}{V_s}\right)^{1/2},\\
p'_s&=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)\frac{U_s}{V_s},\\
w'_s&=\frac{M_s^b c^2+U_s}{V_s}+p'_s .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B'_s\) is the effective field for secondary synchrotron diagnostics,
\(p'_s\) is the hot-gas pressure, and \(w'_s\) is the combined enthalpy density.
If \(V_s\le0\), no combined secondary state is reported at that radius.

\subsubsection{Break-frequency diagnostics}

The break-frequency diagnostics translate the combined secondary state into the
two synchrotron scales plotted for the density-jump component.  The injection
break uses the same dissipation weights,
\begin{equation}
\bar{\gamma}_{m,s}=
\frac{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}\gamma_{m,j,i}}
{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}},
\end{equation}
where \(\bar{\gamma}_{m,s}\) is the energy-weighted injection Lorentz factor.
Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_\nu&=\frac{3e}{4\pi m_ec},\\
\bar\beta_c&=(1-\langle g_c\rangle^{-2})^{1/2},
\delta_c^{-1}&=\langle g_c\rangle(1-\bar\beta_c),\\
\beta_2&=(1-\Gamma_2^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\delta_2^{-1}&=\Gamma_2(1-\beta_2),\\
\gamma_{c,s}&=
\frac{6\pi m_ec(1+z)}
{\sigma_{\rm es} B_s^{\prime 2}\Gamma_2 t_{\rm obs}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Then the diagnostic break frequencies are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_{m,s}=
\frac{C_\nu B'_s\bar{\gamma}_{m,s}^2\delta_c}{1+z},\\
\nu_{c,s}=
\frac{C_\nu B'_s\gamma_{c,s}^2\delta_2}{1+z}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.  The
injection break uses the energy-weighted contact Doppler factor because the
newly heated electrons belong to the secondary contact.  The cooling break uses
the region-2 Doppler factor and the current \(t_{\rm obs}\).  These frequencies
are diagnostics of the secondary component, not a new multi-zone radiation
transport calculation.

\subsubsection{Supported branch boundary}

The supported branch boundary is narrow.  The multi-density reverse-shock path
supports \(p_r>2\), the one-dimensional \texttt{fullhide\_1d} or
\texttt{dg\_1d} electron solver, and the direct 1D observer path.  It does not
support hadronic processes, forward SSC cooling, reverse SSC, cross-zone IC, or
coupled electron-photon feedback in this branch.  The event windows are
diagnostic roots from linearly interpolated adjacent output states.  The
adaptive RK refinement controls the primary reverse-shock variables
\(\Gamma_2\), \(R\), and \(M_2\).  It does not provide a separate conservative
error norm for every secondary branch variable.  The secondary reverse-shock
output is therefore a density-jump diagnostic, not a general reverse-shock model
\citep{DaiLu2002,NakarGranot2007,UhmZhang2014,Laskar2016GRB160509A,Alexander2017GRB160625B,Laskar2019GRB181201A,PangDai2024OffaxisRS}.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig4_reverse_shock.pdf}
  \caption{Reverse-shock and density-jump secondary diagnostics.  (a) The magnetized reverse-shock state is recorded as a function of upstream \(\sigma\).  (b) The reverse-shock flux fraction at the optical peak gives the component contribution.  (c) Density-jump secondary reverse-shock event windows and the injected-to-dissipated energy ratio are computed from the returned branch state.}
  \label{fig:reverse-shock}
\end{figure*}

\section{Hadronic and Pair-Feedback Boundary} \label{sec:hadronic}

\subsection{One-dimensional activation boundary}

\subsubsection{Active physics boundary}

Hadronic transport is a one-dimensional shell branch.  In the forward-shock
feedback path it is activated only when the hadronic module is enabled and the
proton energy fraction \(\epsilon_p\) is positive.  The active branch evolves
protons, optional secondary species, and photon-survival factors on the same
radius grid as the forward-shock electrons and photons.  The formal feedback
state is shell-averaged.  Non-1D forward electron solvers are rejected because
ASGARD does not define \(\chi\)-local target photons, hadron densities, or
secondary feedback for this branch.

\subsubsection{Supported hadronic branches}

The supported hadronic branches determine which physical operators enter the
shell state.  The legacy branch transports protons and computes proton
synchrotron emission only.  The formal AM3/Hummer branch adds \(p\gamma\)
interactions, neutrino production, Bethe--Heitler pair production, hadronic
inverse-Compton emission, \(pp\) interactions, secondary species, and
pair-production feedback
\citep{Murase2007,Hummer2010,KelnerAharonian2008,Chodorowski1992,GouldSchreder1967,IceCube2017GRBNeutrinos,Sahu2022GRB190829AHadronic,Sahu2024GRB221009AHadronic}.
Inactive switches remove the corresponding operators before observer assembly.
Unsupported channels are absent from the returned state.

\subsection{Hadronic ABI boundary}

\subsubsection{Package binding}

The hadronic package initializer only selects compiled kernels.  It is an ABI
boundary, not a particle-transport model.  It defines two handles,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_{\rm FS}&\equiv\texttt{hadronic\_forward\_1d},\\
H_{\rm RS}&\equiv\texttt{hadronic\_reverse\_1d},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and maps the public names \texttt{hadronic\_1d} and
\texttt{reverse\_hadronic\_1d} to \(H_{\rm FS}\) and \(H_{\rm RS}\),
respectively.  This layer does not change the physics.  The particle equations,
interaction rates, and feedback terms are defined inside the selected
one-dimensional kernel.

\subsection{Hadronic base grid}

\subsubsection{Log-Lorentz grid}

The hadronic base grid stores particle spectra on a logarithmic Lorentz-factor
grid.  All process kernels use the same rest masses for electrons, protons,
neutrons, charged pions, neutral pions, and muons.  This keeps acceleration,
cooling, decay, and radiation rates on one mass scale.  The hadronic coordinate
is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta x&=\frac{x_{\max}-x_{\min}}{N_\gamma-1},\\
x_i&=x_{\min}+(i-1)\Delta x,\\
\gamma_i&=10^{x_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(x_i=\log_{10}\gamma_i\), \(x_{\min}=\log_{10}\gamma_{\min}\),
\(x_{\max}=\log_{10}\gamma_{\max}\), \(\gamma_{\min}>1\), and
\(\gamma_{\max}>\gamma_{\min}\).  This hadronic grid is separate from the
natural-log electron continuity grid.  A source term only acts over its allowed
part of the hadronic grid.  Its support is stored as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i_{\rm L}&=\min\{i\mid \gamma_i\ge\gamma_{\min}^{\rm src}\},\\
i_{\rm H}&=\max\{i\mid \gamma_i\le\gamma_{\max}^{\rm src}\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These two indices define the active injection or interaction range.  They do not
change the global transport grid.

\subsubsection{Cell faces and shell time}

Hadronic transport uses geometric faces between neighboring Lorentz-factor
centers.  For interior faces,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{i+1/2}=\sqrt{\gamma_i\gamma_{i+1}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the two boundary faces are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{1/2}=\gamma_1\sqrt{\gamma_1/\gamma_2},\qquad
\gamma_{N_\gamma+1/2}=\gamma_{N_\gamma}
\sqrt{\gamma_{N_\gamma}/\gamma_{N_\gamma-1}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The \(N_\gamma=1\) mode is a low-level diagnostic path and is not used for the
formal transport branch.  Each shell also needs a positive comoving time step:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta t_i' =
\begin{cases}
t'_1, & i=1,\\
t'_i-t'_{i-1}, & i>1,
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(\Delta t_i'>0\).  The time \(t'_i\) comes from the stored radius-ordered
shell state, not from a fitted observer grid.  The comoving dynamical time used
by the base module is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{\rm dyn}=\frac{R}{\Gamma c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Legacy maximum-energy estimate}

The legacy proton upper bound compares acceleration with the dynamical time and
synchrotron cooling.  The acceleration time is proportional to
\(\eta_{\rm acc}\), so a smaller \(\eta_{\rm acc}\) means faster acceleration.
Equating the acceleration time to \(t'_{\rm dyn}\) and to the proton synchrotron
cooling time gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm dyn}
&=\frac{eB't'_{\rm dyn}}{\eta_{\rm acc}m_pc},\\
\gamma_{\rm syn}
&=\frac{m_p}{m_e}
\left(\frac{6\pi e}{\eta_{\rm acc}\sigma_{\rm es}B'}\right)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max}=\min(\gamma_{\rm dyn},\gamma_{\rm syn}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B'\) is the comoving magnetic field, \(m_p\) and \(m_e\) are the proton
and electron masses, and \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross
section.  Other hadronic kernels use the same base module and require positive,
strictly increasing, logarithmically uniform energy grids.

\subsection{Quantum-synchrotron helper}

\subsubsection{QED invariant}

The quantum-synchrotron helper changes only the synchrotron cooling rate.  It
measures how close a charged particle is to the QED synchrotron regime.  For a
species of mass \(m_s\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_Q&=\frac{m_e^2c^3}{e\hbar},&
\hbar&=\frac{h}{2\pi},\\
\chi&=\gamma\,\frac{B'}{B_Q}\,\frac{m_e}{m_s}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B_Q\) is the electron critical field, \(B'\) is the local comoving field,
and \(\gamma\) is the particle Lorentz factor.  The helper returns the
multiplicative factor
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
f_q(\gamma,B',m_s)=\mathcal{Q}(\chi).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This same definition is used by the hadronic cooling and radiation helpers.

\subsubsection{Cooling suppression factor}

The cooling factor has a classical branch and a quantum-suppressed branch.  The
implemented AM3-style Landau factor is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{Q}(\chi)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \chi\le\chi_Q,\\
\left[1+\sqrt{2}\,\chi^{2/3}\right]^{-2}, & \chi>\chi_Q,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\chi_Q\) is the small-\(\chi\) evaluation switch.  If \(B'\le0\) or
\(\gamma\le1\), the helper returns \(f_q=1\).  If the quantum-synchrotron option
is disabled, the loss rate also keeps \(f_q=1\).  Thus the helper only reduces
the classical synchrotron loss rate when the selected run enters the
quantum-synchrotron regime \citep{Klinger2024AM3}.

\subsection{Hadronic species state}

\subsubsection{Returned species state}

The active hadronic branch returns particle spectra and secondary source terms.
The formal shell state is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal Y}_h=\{&
N_p,N_n,N_{\pi^0},N_{\pi^\pm},N_{\mu^\pm},\\
&N_\nu,Q_e^{\rm BH},Q_e^{pp},Q_e^{\gamma\gamma}\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_p\) is the proton spectrum, \(N_n\) is the neutron spectrum,
\(N_\pi\) and \(N_\mu\) are pion and muon spectra, \(N_\nu\) is the neutrino
output, and the \(Q_e\) terms are electron-positron source spectra from
Bethe--Heitler, \(pp\), and pair-production channels.  These arrays are
shell-local.  They couple to radiation only through the target photon field,
photon-survival factors, and the secondary-pair source passed back to the
electron layer.

\subsection{Proton process-operator equation}

The proton equation collects injection, continuous losses, interaction losses,
and baryon reinjection.  In compact radius-ordered form,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial N_p}{\partial R}
&=Q_{p,R}
+\mathcal{R}_{p\gamma}
-\mathcal{L}_{p\gamma}
-\mathcal{L}_{\rm BH}
-\mathcal{L}_{pp}
-\mathcal{L}_{p,{\rm c}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{p,R}\) is the proton source, \(\mathcal{R}_{p\gamma}\) is nucleon
reinjection from the photohadronic response, \(\mathcal{L}_{p\gamma}\) is
photohadronic loss, \(\mathcal{L}_{\rm BH}\) is Bethe--Heitler loss,
\(\mathcal{L}_{pp}\) is hadronuclear loss, and \(\mathcal{L}_{p,{\rm c}}\)
contains the continuous adiabatic and proton-synchrotron losses.  This is a
compact operator statement.  Each term is a separate shell operator, and inactive
switches remove the corresponding operator before observer assembly.

\subsection{Hadronic species identifiers}

\subsubsection{Mass and charge map}

The acceleration helper needs only the species mass and charge.  For the
supported hadrons and leptonic secondaries, it uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
(m_s,Z_s)&\in
\{(m_p,1),(m_n,0),(m_\pi,\pm1),(m_\mu,\pm1)\},\\
|q_s|&=|Z_s|e .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(m_s\) is the rest mass, \(Z_s\) is the charge number, and \(q_s\) is the
charge magnitude.  Neutral species do not enter the acceleration-limit
calculation because \(q_s=0\).

\subsection{Hadronic shell energy budget}

The hadronic source is normalized by the energy assigned to one shell.  The
transport kernel receives this shell energy as \(E_{s,i}\).  It then forms
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L'_{s,i}&=\frac{E_{s,i}}{\Delta t'_i},&
\Delta V_i&=\frac{4\pi}{3}(R_i^3-R_{i-1}^3),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(R_0=0\).  Here \(L'_{s,i}\) is the comoving source luminosity used by the
formal shell branch, and \(\Delta V_i\) converts particle content to density for
interaction rates.  The hadronic kernel does not infer \(E_{s,i}\) from a new
swept-mass formula.  It uses the shell energy supplied by the upstream dynamics
and runtime configuration.

\subsection{Hadronic injection spectrum}

The hadronic injection spectrum gives the shape before normalization.  For a
species \(s\), define the optional cutoff factor
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal C_s(\gamma)&=
\begin{cases}
\exp(-\gamma/\gamma_{\rm cut}),& {\rm cutoff\ active},\\
1,& {\rm cutoff\ inactive},
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and write the unnormalized shape as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_s(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
\gamma^{-p_s}\mathcal C_s(\gamma),&
\gamma_{\min}\le\gamma\le\gamma_{\rm inj},\\
0, & \gamma<\gamma_{\min}\ {\rm or}\ \gamma>\gamma_{\rm inj},
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(p_s\) is the injection index and \(\gamma_{\rm inj}\) is the upper
support of the injected particles.  For forward-shock protons, the lower support
is \(\max(\gamma_{\min},\Gamma)\), so the injected spectrum does not start below
the current bulk Lorentz factor.

\subsection{Hadronic source normalization}

The formal shell source uses a relativistic energy moment.  The moment and
normalized source are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_s&=\int S_s(\gamma)\gamma\,d\gamma,\\
Q_s(\gamma)=
\frac{L'_{s,i}S_s(\gamma)}{m_sc^2A_s}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This normalization enforces
\(m_sc^2\int Q_s(\gamma)\gamma\,d\gamma=L'_{s,i}\).  The integral is evaluated on
the same Lorentz-factor grid, and the injected shell content is
\(Q_s\Delta t'_i\).  The legacy proton-only branch uses the same power-law
support but normalizes the shell content with the kinetic-energy moment
\(\int S_p(\gamma)(\gamma-1)m_pc^2\,d\gamma\).  Both branches conserve the
declared shell energy in their own source convention.

\subsection{Hadronic acceleration limits}

The upper Lorentz factor is set by comparing acceleration with the dynamical and
synchrotron times.  For charged species, the acceleration and synchrotron times
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{\rm acc}
&=\eta_{\rm acc}\frac{\gamma m_sc}{|q_s|B'},\\
t'_{\rm syn}
&=\frac{6\pi m_s^3c}
{\sigma_{\rm es}m_e^2B'^2\gamma},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and \(t'_{\rm dyn}=R/(\Gamma c)\).  Equating \(t'_{\rm acc}\) to the dynamical
and synchrotron times gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm dyn}
&=\frac{|q_s|B'R}{\eta_{\rm acc}\Gamma m_sc^2},\\
\gamma_{\rm syn}
&=\left[\frac{6\pi |q_s|m_s^2}
{\eta_{\rm acc}\sigma_{\rm es}m_e^2B'}\right]^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\eta_{\rm acc}\) is the acceleration parameter, \(B'\) is the comoving
magnetic field, and \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.
Neutral species are not sent through this acceleration-limit calculation.

\subsection{External hadronic cooling limit}

An external cooling table can add one more upper-energy bound.  If such a table
is supplied, define the positive cooling rate
\(\mathcal{C}_{{\rm ext},i}\equiv
-(d\gamma/dt')_{\rm ext}|_{\gamma_i}>0\).  The corresponding cooling time is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{\rm ext}(\gamma_i)=\frac{\gamma_i}{\mathcal{C}_{{\rm ext},i}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the crossing of \(t'_{\rm acc}\) with \(t'_{\rm ext}\) gives
\(\gamma_{\rm ext}\).  The crossing is obtained by linear interpolation of
\(\ln(t'_{\rm acc}/t'_{\rm ext})\) against \(\ln\gamma\) on the supplied grid.
The accepted upper limit is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\max}=
\begin{cases}
\min(\gamma_{\rm dyn},\gamma_{\rm syn},\gamma_{\rm ext}),&
\gamma_{\rm ext}\ {\rm exists},\\
\min(\gamma_{\rm dyn},\gamma_{\rm syn}),&
\gamma_{\rm ext}\ {\rm is\ absent}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This bound sets the top of the species grid used by the active hadronic source.

\subsection{Bethe-Heitler pair-production kernel}

\subsubsection{Kinematic variables and threshold}

The Bethe--Heitler branch describes \(p\gamma\to pe^+e^-\) pair creation.  For a
proton energy \(E_p\), a target-photon energy \(\epsilon\), and a secondary
lepton energy \(E_e\), the branch first forms
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_p&\equiv \frac{E_p}{m_pc^2},\\
x_\gamma&\equiv \frac{\epsilon}{m_ec^2},\\
x_e&\equiv \frac{E_e}{m_ec^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(m_p\) and \(m_e\) are the proton and electron masses.  The interaction is
allowed only when the proton-frame photon energy is high enough to create a
pair, \(\gamma_px_\gamma>1\).  Cells below this threshold add no pair source, no
photon sink, and no Bethe--Heitler proton loss.

\subsubsection{Differential pair kernel}

The differential pair kernel gives the pair yield at one lepton energy for one
proton-photon cell.  For an allowed cell, ASGARD integrates the
Blumenthal--Gould Bethe--Heitler cross section over the proton-frame photon
energy \(\omega\) and the intermediate lepton energy \(\bar E\)
\citep{BlumenthalGould1970}:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm BH}&\equiv \frac{x_e}{2\gamma_p^3x_\gamma^2},\\
I_{\rm BH}
&\equiv \int_{\omega_{\min}}^{\omega_{\max}} d\omega
\int_{\bar E_{\min}}^{\omega-1} d\bar E\,
\frac{\omega\,\sigma_{\rm BH}(\omega,\bar E,\xi)}{\bar p},\\
\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}(x_e,\gamma_p,x_\gamma)
&\equiv C_{\rm BH}I_{\rm BH}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The prefactor \(C_{\rm BH}\) converts the integral to the chosen lepton-energy
variable, and \(\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}\) is the cell kernel used by the source and
loss sums.  The integration limits and local angular variable are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\omega_{\max}&=2\gamma_px_\gamma,\\
\omega_{\min}&=\frac{(\gamma_p+x_e)^2}{2\gamma_px_e},\\
\bar E_{\min}&=\frac{\gamma_p^2+x_e^2}{2\gamma_px_e},\\
\bar p&=(\bar E^2-1)^{1/2},\\
\xi&=\frac{\gamma_p\bar E-x_e}{\gamma_p\bar p}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
If the lower limit reaches the upper limit, the pair phase space is closed and
\(\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}=0\).

\subsubsection{Fit-domain and quadrature controls}

The Bethe--Heitler cross-section fit has a finite photon-energy domain.  ASGARD
evaluates \(\sigma_{\rm BH}\) only for
\(\omega_{\rm BH,L}<\omega<\omega_{\rm BH,H}\), and the differential kernel is
zero outside that fit window.  The lower edge is the pair-production threshold,
while the upper edge is the finite domain of the adopted analytic fit.  The two
integrals are evaluated with fixed quadrature controls \(N_\omega\) and
\(N_{\bar E}\), which set the resolution in \(\omega\) and \(\bar E\).

\subsubsection{Secondary-pair source}

The secondary-pair source adds the Bethe--Heitler leptons to the electron and
positron grid.  On the log-uniform proton and photon grids, ASGARD writes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Y_i&\equiv E_{p,i}N_p(E_{p,i}),\\
Z_k&\equiv \epsilon_k n_\gamma(\epsilon_k),\\
K_{jik}&\equiv
\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}(x_{e,j},\gamma_{p,i},x_{\gamma,k}),\\
k_{\rm BH}&\equiv \frac{3c\sigma_{\rm es}\alpha_{\rm f}}{16\pi},\\
Q_e^{\rm BH}(E_{e,j})
&=\frac{k_{\rm BH}\Delta\ln E_p\,\Delta\ln\epsilon}{E_{e,j}}
\sum_{i,k}Y_iZ_kK_{jik}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_p\) is the proton spectrum, \(n_\gamma\) is the target photon density,
\(\alpha_{\rm f}\) is the fine-structure constant, and
\(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.  The output
\(Q_e^{\rm BH}\) is a shell-local \(e^\pm\) injection rate per lepton energy.

\subsubsection{Target-photon sink}

The target-photon sink removes the photons that take part in the same
Bethe--Heitler interactions.  For each photon bin,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
D_k&\equiv \epsilon_k n_\gamma(\epsilon_k),\\
\Lambda_{\gamma}^{\rm BH}(\epsilon_k)
&=\frac{k_{\rm BH}\Delta\ln E_p\,\Delta\ln E_e}{D_k}
\sum_{i,j}Y_iZ_kK_{jik}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The positive rate \(\Lambda_{\gamma}^{\rm BH}\) is later converted to the
optical depth of the shell cell and to the corresponding photon survival factor.
If the target photon density in bin \(k\) is zero, the photon-loss rate in that
bin is set to zero.

\subsubsection{Proton cooling rate}

The proton cooling rate records the proton energy lost in Bethe--Heitler
interactions.  ASGARD uses the Chodorowski et al. energy-loss kernel
\(\phi_{\rm C}\) \citep{Chodorowski1992} and writes the positive cooling rate as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_k^{\rm C}
&\equiv \frac{\phi_{\rm C}(2\gamma_px_{\gamma,k})}
{2\gamma_p^2x_{\gamma,k}^2},\\
k_{p{\rm BH}}&\equiv
\frac{3\alpha_{\rm f}\sigma_{\rm es}c}{8\pi}\frac{m_e}{m_p},\\
\mathcal{C}_p^{\rm BH}(\gamma_p)
&\equiv -\left(\frac{d\gamma_p}{dt'}\right)_{\rm BH}\\
&=k_{p{\rm BH}}\Delta\ln\epsilon
\sum_k\Phi_k^{\rm C}Z_k.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The rate \(\mathcal{C}_p^{\rm BH}\) enters the proton transport equation as a
loss term.

\subsubsection{Chodorowski loss fit}

The loss kernel uses the Chodorowski et al. piecewise fit.  With
\(x_{\rm th}=2\), the fitted function is
\begin{equation}
\phi_{\rm C}(x)=
\begin{cases}
0, & x<x_{\rm th},\\
\Phi_{\rm L}(x;\mathbf{a}_{\rm C}), & x_{\rm th}\le x<x_{\rm C},\\
\Phi_{\rm H}(x;\mathbf{b}_{\rm C}), & x\ge x_{\rm C},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(x_{\rm C}\) is the matching point between the low- and high-energy
branches.  The coefficient sets \(\mathbf{a}_{\rm C}\) and
\(\mathbf{b}_{\rm C}\) are the published fit parameters.  The Bethe--Heitler
branch therefore returns three shell-local quantities: a secondary \(e^\pm\)
source, a positive proton cooling rate \(\mathcal{C}_p^{\rm BH}\), and a
positive target-photon loss rate \(\Lambda_{\gamma}^{\rm BH}\).

\subsection{Proton transport operator}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Log-\(\gamma_p\)}{Log-gamma-p} transport step}

The proton transport operator carries the injected shell protons through cooling
and hadronic interactions before secondary species are advanced.  The spectrum
is stored on the hadronic \(\log_{10}\gamma_p\) grid.  Cooling moves particle
content from high \(\gamma_p\) to lower \(\gamma_p\), so all continuous losses
are written as positive rates,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_p(\gamma_p)&\equiv
-\left(\frac{d\gamma_p}{dt'}\right)_{\rm loss},\\
\frac{\partial N_p}{\partial t'}
&-\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_p}
\left(C_pN_p\right)
=Q_p+Q_{p,{\rm r}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_p=dN_p/d\gamma_p\), \(Q_p\) is the primary proton source, and
\(Q_{p,{\rm r}}\) is the proton source returned by the \(p\gamma\) response.  In
the formal feedback branch, the continuous part of this equation is evaluated by
a conservative remap of cell content.  For a time step \(\Delta t'\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_i^{\rm c} &=
C_{\rm ad,i}+C_{{\rm syn},i}+C_{{\rm BH},i}+C_{{\rm pp},i},\\
\gamma_i^{\rm c} &= \gamma_i-C_i^{\rm c}\Delta t',\\
\Delta N_i &= N_{p,i}\Delta\gamma_i,\\
N_{p,a}^{\rm c} &=
\frac{1}{\Delta\gamma_a}
\sum_{\gamma_i^{\rm c}\in[\gamma_{a-1/2},\gamma_{a+1/2})}\Delta N_i
+\mathcal{Q}_{p,a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(C_i^{\rm c}\) is the continuous cooling rate in proton cell \(i\),
\(\gamma_i^{\rm c}\) is the cooled cell center, and
\(\mathcal{Q}_{p,a}\) is the proton content injected during the same shell step.
Content that cools outside the stored grid is not assigned to a grid cell.  The
legacy proton-only branch instead uses an upwind flux form of the same
conservation law; it is not the formal feedback path used for coupled
electron--photon--hadron runs.

\subsubsection{Baseline proton losses}

The baseline proton losses are adiabatic cooling and proton synchrotron cooling.
Before any collision operator is added, the code uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm ad}(\gamma_p)&=\frac{\gamma_p}{t'_{\rm dyn}},\\
C_{\rm syn}&=
\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B'^2}{6\pi m_ec}
\left(\frac{m_e}{m_p}\right)^3\gamma_p^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t'_{\rm dyn}\) is the comoving shell expansion time and \(B'\) is the
comoving magnetic field.  Bethe--Heitler and \(pp\) losses are added to the
continuous rate \(C_i^{\rm c}\) only when their branches are active.  The
\(p\gamma\) loss is handled after the remap as an exponential interaction sink
with a same-step reinjection source,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{p\gamma,i} &= \lambda_{p\gamma,i}\Delta t',\\
t'_{{\rm eff},i} &=
\begin{cases}
\left(1-e^{-\tau_{p\gamma,i}}\right)/\lambda_{p\gamma,i},
& \lambda_{p\gamma,i}>0,\\
\Delta t',&\lambda_{p\gamma,i}=0,
\end{cases}\\
N_{p,i}^{\rm new} &=
N_{p,i}^{\rm c}e^{-\tau_{p\gamma,i}}
+t'_{{\rm eff},i}V_i\mathcal{R}_{p\gamma,i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\lambda_{p\gamma,i}\) is the positive proton interaction rate, \(V_i\) is
the shell volume, and \(\mathcal{R}_{p\gamma,i}\) is the reinjection source after
the code-unit conversion used by the shell operator.  This step keeps the lost
and reinjected proton content in the same shell update.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(p\gamma\)}{p-gamma} shell survival operator}

The \(p\gamma\) survival operator tells the hadronic response which target
photons remain in one shell cell.  ASGARD uses two passes.  The first pass acts
on the unattenuated photon density and returns the photon-loss depth.  The second
pass acts on the survived photon field and returns interaction rates, secondary
yields, and \(Q_{p,{\rm r}}\).  With
\(\tau_g\equiv\tau_\gamma(\nu',R)\), the finite-cell survival factor is
\begin{equation}
S_\gamma(\nu',R)=
\frac{1-e^{-\tau_g}}{\tau_g},
\end{equation}
with \(S_\gamma\rightarrow1\) as \(\tau_g\rightarrow0\).  This factor is the
cell-averaged photon density seen by the second pass.  The returned photon
survival array is then applied to proton synchrotron, secondary synchrotron,
neutral-pion photons, Bethe--Heitler photons, hadronic IC photons, and pion/muon
radiation before observer projection.  The branch is shell-local and
one-dimensional.

\subsection{Secondary-species source operators}

Secondary-species source operators convert hadronic interactions into local
particle source terms.  These transported secondary species are supported only
in the formal one-dimensional path.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(p\gamma\)}{p-gamma} response products}

The \(p\gamma\) response products are the particles produced after target-photon
survival has been applied.  The survived-field response generates neutral-pion
gamma rays, charged pions, muons, neutrinos, neutron reinjection, and proton
reinjection.  These products are local shell outputs before observer projection;
the neutrino arrays are not interpreted as detection predictions without a
separate exposure calculation.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(pp\)}{pp} delta source}

The \(pp\) source operator supplies a compact pion source when baryonic shell
targets are active.  The branch uses a delta-approximation with the fixed
parameter set
\begin{equation}
\left(\kappa_{pp},\xi_\pi,f_{\pi^0}\right),
\end{equation}
where \(\kappa_{pp}\) is the inelasticity, \(\xi_\pi\) is the pion energy
fraction, and \(f_{\pi^0}\) is the neutral-pion fraction.  The output is a local
secondary source, not an independent radiation component.

\subsubsection{Bethe-Heitler pair handoff}

The Bethe--Heitler handoff tells the code where the produced pairs are counted.
Bethe--Heitler and \(pp\) pairs are accumulated as a secondary electron source.
In the separated branch, Bethe--Heitler secondary electrons can be merged into
the forward electron distribution.  In that case, the separate Bethe--Heitler
luminosity is not assembled, so the same injected energy is counted in one
place.

\subsection{Joint electron--photon--hadron feedback iteration}

\subsubsection{Local fixed-point sequence}

The joint feedback branch updates electrons, photons, and hadrons inside one
shell before observer projection.  One local iteration has the order
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_e^{(r)}
&\rightarrow n_\gamma^{(r)}
\rightarrow
\left\{Q_e^{{\rm sec},(r)},\tau_{\gamma\gamma}^{(r)}\right\}
\rightarrow N_e^{(r+1)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(r\) is the local iteration index.  The secondary source enters
\(Q_e^{\rm sec}\), the updated electron solution rebuilds the photon field, and
the hadronic operators are evaluated again on the survived target field.  The
observer calculation sees only the state after this local sequence has been
completed.

\subsubsection{Cascade branch boundary}

The cascade boundary states which electromagnetic feedback path is supported.
When the requested cascade count satisfies \(N_{\rm cas}>1\), ASGARD uses the
shell-sequence time-dependent \(\gamma\gamma\) pair/synchrotron cascade path.
The single-shell path is a diagnostic mode, and this branch does not implement
an inverse-Compton-mediated electromagnetic cascade.

\subsection{Shell-sequence pair-cascade algorithm}

\subsubsection{Pair grid and escape time}

The shell-sequence cascade is the supported time-dependent electromagnetic
cascade branch.  It carries the pair population from one radial shell to the next
and updates the local photon density inside each shell before observer
projection.  The calculation first writes the seed photon field as
\(n_\epsilon=dN_\gamma/d\epsilon\).  It then places pairs on an energy grid with
the same logarithmic spacing as the photon grid,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
j_0&=\left\lceil
\frac{\ln(m_ec^2/E_{\gamma,1})}{\Delta\ln E}
\right\rceil,\\
E_{e,j}&=E_{\gamma,1}\exp[(j-1+j_0)\Delta\ln E],\\
\gamma_{e,j}&=\frac{E_{e,j}}{m_ec^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(E_{\gamma,1}\) is the first photon-bin energy, \(\Delta\ln E\) is the
common logarithmic spacing, and \(j_0\) is the integer offset that makes the
first pair bin lie at or above \(m_ec^2\).  In shell \(i\), photons remain in the
cell for the escape time
\begin{equation}
t'_{{\rm esc},i}=\frac{R_i}{\eta_{\rm esc}\Gamma_i c},
\end{equation}
where \(R_i\) and \(\Gamma_i\) are the shell radius and Lorentz factor, and
\(\eta_{\rm esc}\) is the fixed shell-escape geometry factor.  This escape time
sets both the photon residence time and the source scale used in the photon
density update.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\gamma\)}{gamma-gamma} source evaluation}

The \(\gamma\gamma\) source evaluation converts absorbed photons into secondary
pairs.  At substep \(m\), the current photon density is passed to the same
angle-averaged pair-production operator used by the shell opacity calculation:
\begin{equation}
n_\epsilon^{(m)}
\rightarrow
\left\{\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}(\epsilon),
q_{\pm,\gamma\gamma}(E_e),P_{\rm abs},P_{\rm inj}\right\},
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}\) is the local photon loss rate,
\(q_{\pm,\gamma\gamma}\) is the combined \(e^-+e^+\) injection rate per energy,
and \(P_{\rm abs}\) and \(P_{\rm inj}\) are absorbed and injected powers used as
energy-closure diagnostics.  The source added to the pair \(\gamma\)-grid in one
substep is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_{\pm,j}^{(m)}&=\Delta V_i\,m_ec^2\,
q_{\pm,\gamma\gamma}(E_{e,j})\,\Delta t'_{\rm sub},\\
\Delta t'_{\rm sub}&=\frac{\Delta t'_i}{N_{\rm sub}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta V_i\) is the shell volume, \(\Delta t'_i\) is the comoving shell
time width, and \(N_{\rm sub}\) is the declared cascade substep count.  The
stored powers \(P_{\rm abs}\) and \(P_{\rm inj}\) are local diagnostics; they are
not observer-frame fluxes.

\subsubsection{Finite-grid pair cooling}

The finite-grid pair-cooling step moves the carried and newly injected pairs to
lower Lorentz factor.  In shell \(i\), the positive cooling rate is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{{\rm dyn},i}&=\frac{R_i}{\Gamma_i c},\\
C_{{\rm syn},i}(\gamma)
&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_i'^2}{6\pi m_ec}\gamma^2,\\
\mathcal{C}_{\pm,i}(\gamma)
&\equiv -\left(\frac{d\gamma}{dt'}\right)_{\pm,i}\\
&=C_{{\rm syn},i}(\gamma)+\frac{\gamma}{t'_{{\rm dyn},i}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t'_{{\rm dyn},i}\) is the comoving expansion time and \(B_i'\) is the
comoving magnetic field.  The discrete update stores the pair content in cell
\(j\) as \(\Delta N_j=N_j\Delta\gamma_j\), cools the cell center to
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_j^{\rm c}
&=\gamma_j-\mathcal{C}_{\pm,i}(\gamma_j)\Delta t'_{\rm sub},\\
N_j^{(m+1)}
&=
\frac{1}{\Delta\gamma_j}
\sum_{\gamma_\ell^{\rm c}\in[\gamma_{j-1/2},\gamma_{j+1/2})}
\Delta N_\ell^{(m)}
+S_{\pm,j}^{(m)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus the pair update is a finite-grid cooled-content remap plus the current
\(\gamma\gamma\) source.  If \(\gamma_j^{\rm c}\) lies outside the finite pair
grid, that content leaves the represented cascade population.

\subsubsection{Photon-density transfer}

The photon-density transfer closes the local pair-photon loop in one shell.  The
updated pair distribution is passed to the synchrotron state operator, which
returns a pair synchrotron luminosity \(L_{\nu',\pm}'\) and a local pair seed
photon density \(n_{\nu',\pm}'\).  The photon field then solves the substep
equation
\begin{equation}
\frac{dn_\epsilon}{dt'}=
S_\epsilon-\Lambda_\epsilon n_\epsilon,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Lambda_\epsilon&=\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}(\epsilon)+\frac{1}{t'_{\rm esc}},\\
S_\epsilon&=\frac{n_{\epsilon,{\rm prim}}+n_{\epsilon,\pm}}{t'_{\rm esc}},\\
n_{\epsilon,\pm}&=\frac{n_{\nu',\pm}'}{h},\\
A_\epsilon&=\exp(-\Lambda_\epsilon\Delta t'_{\rm sub}),\\
n_\epsilon^{(m+1)}=
A_\epsilon n_\epsilon^{(m)}
+\frac{S_\epsilon}{\Lambda_\epsilon}(1-A_\epsilon).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\epsilon,{\rm prim}}\) is the primary shell photon density, and
\(n_{\epsilon,\pm}\) is the photon density emitted by the cascade pairs.  The
recorded local optical depth is
\(\tau_{\gamma\gamma}=\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}t'_{\rm esc}\).  The outputs passed
to the observer module are the pair density, pair synchrotron luminosity, pair
seed photon density, and \(\gamma\gamma\) optical depth for each shell.

\subsection{Single-shell pair-synchrotron diagnostic}

\subsubsection{Cooling track}

The single-shell diagnostic is a local check of the \(\gamma\gamma\)
pair-synchrotron kernel.  It evaluates the pair operator in one photon field,
returns the photon loss rate and absorbed power, and then follows the injected
pairs along a synchrotron cooling track.  It does not carry pairs to the next
shell and does not update the shell photon density.  For \(B'>0\), a pair
injected at \(\gamma_i\) cools over the diagnostic path time \(t'_p\) to
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_B&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B'^2}{6\pi m_ec},\\
\gamma_i^{\rm c}&=
\frac{\gamma_i}{1+a_B\gamma_i t'_p},\\
t_{\rm cool}(\gamma)&=\frac{1}{a_B\gamma^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The diagnostic deposits the injected pair source \(q_{\pm,i}^{\rm d}\) on grid
cells between \(\gamma_i^{\rm c}\) and \(\gamma_i\),
\begin{equation}
N_{\pm,j}^{\rm d}
=
\sum_{\gamma_i^{\rm c}<\gamma_j\le\gamma_i}
q_{\pm,i}^{\rm d}\,t_{\rm cool}(\gamma_j)\,\Delta\ln\gamma .
\end{equation}
Here \(q_{\pm,i}^{\rm d}\) is the local pair source returned by the diagnostic
pair operator.  If \(B'\le0\), the diagnostic returns no pair-synchrotron
emissivity, although the photon-loss rate from the pair operator remains defined.

\subsubsection{Diagnostic emissivity}

The diagnostic emissivity maps the cooled diagnostic pairs to synchrotron
photons.  It uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_\nu&=\frac{3e}{4\pi m_ec},\\
\nu_s&=C_\nu B'\gamma^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the single-particle approximation
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
P_\nu&\simeq C_PB'\Phi_{\rm d}(\nu/\nu_s),\\
C_P&=\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3}{m_ec^2},\\
\Phi_{\rm d}(x)&=A_{\rm d}x^{1/3}e^{-x}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_{\rm d}\) is the fixed coefficient of the diagnostic emissivity kernel.
The diagnostic photon spectrum is then
\begin{equation}
L_\nu^{\rm d}
=
\sum_j P_\nu(\gamma_j)N_{\pm,j}^{\rm d}\gamma_j\Delta\ln\gamma .
\end{equation}
This spectrum is used to check the local synchrotron part of the cascade
operator.  The formal pair-cascade output remains the shell-sequence state in the
previous subsection.

\section{Observer Projection and Component Assembly} \label{sec:projection}

\subsection{Equal-arrival-time projection}

\subsubsection{Local angular map}

The observer projection is the last step of the calculation.  It reads the local
radius-ordered spectra and absorption factors, maps them to equal-arrival-time
surfaces, and then sums angular cells into \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\).  For an
angular cell at polar angle \(\theta\), azimuth \(\phi\), and viewing angle
\(\theta_v\), the local geometry is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu&=\cos\theta_v\cos\theta+\sin\theta_v\sin\theta\cos\phi,\\
t_{\rm arr}(R,\theta,\phi)
&=t_0(R)+\frac{(1+z)R(1-\mu)}{c},\\
\eta_\delta&\equiv\delta^{-1}=\Gamma(1-\beta\mu).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\mu\) is the angle cosine between the cell velocity and the line of sight,
\(t_0(R)\) is the on-axis shell-arrival time supplied by the local dynamical
solution, and \(\eta_\delta\) is the inverse Doppler factor stored by the
projection kernels.  This step changes how local photons are observed; it does
not change the local particle or photon state.

\subsubsection{Radius-to-observer interpolation}

The radius-to-observer interpolation places each local spectrum on the observer
time grid.  Along the equal-arrival-time surface, the code evaluates the comoving
frequency and angular weight as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu'&=\nu_{\rm obs}(1+z)\eta_\delta,\\
dW&=\eta_\delta^{-3}\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For an observer-time point between two neighboring arrival radii, the code
linearly interpolates the local SED in that radial segment.  It then interpolates
in frequency on the logarithmic grid, using log--log interpolation when both
neighboring SED values are positive and linear interpolation otherwise.
Density-jump and injection features are therefore broadened only by
equal-arrival-time geometry and by this stated interpolation rule.

\subsection{Structured-jet aggregation boundary}

\subsubsection{Backend selection}

The backend selection states which angular projection path is active.  A direct
top-hat model with a supported medium uses the direct kernel-backed path.
Structured jets are solved as angular patches by either the Fortran structured
backend or the Python patch backend.  The Fortran structured backend supports
uniform patch sampling and the one-dimensional \texttt{fullhide\_1d} or
\texttt{dg\_1d} electron solver.  It can carry forward-shock synchrotron, forward
SSC, reverse-shock synchrotron, and the supported structured \(p\gamma\)/neutrino
path when the required one-dimensional hadronic response is active.  It does not
support reverse-shock SSC, cross-zone IC, reverse-shock hadronic branches,
Bethe--Heitler, \(pp\), hadronic IC, or pair-cascade branches.  The
\texttt{dominant\_region\_ioka\_v1} and
\texttt{dominant\_region\_ioka\_time\_v1} samplers use the Python patch backend.

\subsubsection{Active-patch register}

The active-patch register tells the structured backend which angular cells are
physically solved.  The Python caller samples the jet on midpoint cells and
passes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{ab}&=E_{\rm iso}(\phi_b,\theta_a),\\
\Gamma_{0,ab}&=\Gamma_0(\phi_b,\theta_a),\\
I^E_{ab}&=\mathbf{1}(E_{ab}>0),\\
I^\Gamma_{ab}&=\mathbf{1}(\Gamma_{0,ab}\ge\Gamma_{\min}),\\
A_{ab}&=I^E_{ab}I^\Gamma_{ab},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(a\) and \(b\) label polar and azimuthal cells.  The quantity
\(\Gamma_{\min}\) is the relativistic-backend activation boundary, not an extra
physical parameter.  The compiled \texttt{structured\_jet\_1d} entry receives the
active grid, solves the local one-dimensional state for active patches, and then
projects each patch with its own angular weight.  The Python ring path can reuse
a solved state only when all active rings share the same \(E_{ab}\) and
\(\Gamma_{0,ab}\); otherwise the local patch states are solved separately.

\subsubsection{Local one-dimensional solve}

The local one-dimensional solve is the same physical solve used by a direct
top-hat model.  For an active patch \(q\), the aggregate replaces only the local
boundary entries
\begin{equation}
\Gamma_0\leftarrow\Gamma_{0,q},\qquad
E_{\rm iso}\leftarrow E_q
\end{equation}
and then runs the one-dimensional dynamics, electron, optional SSC, reverse
synchrotron, and selected hadronic component kernels described earlier.  The
structured aggregate changes angular weights and state reuse.  It does not
change the local shock, particle, or synchrotron equations.

\subsubsection{Shell prefactor}

The shell prefactor turns local component luminosities into the spectra that the
observer projector is allowed to read.  It first builds the shell absorption
factor from the active photon fields and optical depths.  It then applies
distance dilution and redshift to every active component.  For a component label
\(c\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{P}_{\nu'}(R)&=
\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}\,
\mathcal{A}_{\rm abs}(\nu',R),\\
\widetilde L_{\nu'}^{\,c}(R,q)=
\mathcal{P}_{\nu'}(R)L_{\nu'}^{\prime c}(R,q).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(d_L\) is the luminosity distance and \(\mathcal{A}_{\rm abs}\) is the
