combined local absorption factor returned by the photon--photon absorption path
after extra optical depths have been added.  The active labels can include
FS-synchrotron, FS-SSC, FS hadronic photons, Bethe--Heitler photons, hadronic IC,
pair-cascade photons, RS synchrotron, RS hadronic photons, RS-SSC, and cross-zone
IC.  Components that are not active remain absent from the observer component
list.

\subsubsection{Angular accumulation}

The angular accumulation projects each active component before the total is
formed.  Axisymmetric structured profiles use
\texttt{SED\_interpolation\_structured}, while explicit two-dimensional angular
grids use \texttt{sed\_structured\_phi}.  The direct top-hat path uses the
selected equal-arrival-time interpolation kernel.  After projection, the returned
total is the algebraic component sum
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F_{\nu}(t_{\rm obs})&=
F_{\nu}^{\rm FS,syn}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm FS,SSC}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm FS,had}\\
&\quad
+F_{\nu}^{\rm RS,syn}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm RS,had}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm RS,SSC}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm cross}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Terms that are not active are omitted from the sum.  The diagnostic radius track
returned with a structured solve is copied from the first patch that records the
track.  It is a local one-dimensional history, not a flux-weighted angular
average.

\subsection{Projection symbol binding}

The projection symbol binding only connects Python names to compiled projection
kernels.  It is not a radiation model and it does not choose an observer
geometry.  The \texttt{src.Interpolation} initializer maps the public names
\texttt{sed\_interpolation}, \texttt{sed\_adaptive\_theta},
\texttt{sed\_interpolation\_chi}, \texttt{sed\_chi\_electron},
\texttt{sed\_chi\_ring}, \texttt{sed\_chiring\_batchlum}, and
\texttt{sed\_chiring\_batchlum\_ray} to the compiled
\texttt{SED\_interpolation} module.  On first access, it imports that module and
copies the requested symbol into the package namespace.  Names outside this list
are not exported.  The binding therefore exposes the projection map
\begin{equation}
\{\widetilde L_{\nu'}(R),\tau_{\nu'}(R),R,\Gamma,t_{\rm obs},\nu_{\rm obs}\}
\longrightarrow F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}(t_{\rm obs}),
\end{equation}
while leaving the radiation process, optical depth, Doppler factor, arrival-time
map, and interpolation weights inside the selected Fortran kernel.

\subsection{Direct top-hat equal-arrival-time interpolation}

\subsubsection{Midpoint angular cells}

The midpoint angular cells divide a direct top-hat shell into small emitting
surface elements.  Each element keeps one angle pair while the radius changes.
The direct \texttt{SED\_interpolation} kernel uses these elements to build the
equal-arrival-time sum.  In the shell-level path, angular cell \(a\) has solid
angle
\begin{equation}
\Delta\Omega_a=
(\cos\theta_{a,-}-\cos\theta_{a,+})\,\Delta\phi .
\end{equation}
Here \(\theta_{a,-}\) and \(\theta_{a,+}\) are the lower and upper polar edges,
and \(\Delta\phi\) is the azimuthal width.  When the public top-hat grid stores a
collapsed azimuthal half-plane, the code uses the named collapsed-grid control
\(N_{\phi,c}\) to restore the full azimuthal weight without changing the local
arrival-time map.  For each angular cell, the direction cosine and arrival time
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu&=\cos\theta_v\cos\theta+\sin\theta_v\sin\theta\cos\phi ,\\
t_{\rm arr}(R)&=t_0(R)+\frac{(1+z)R(1-\mu)}{c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\theta\) and \(\phi\) are the cell midpoint angles, \(\theta_v\) is the
viewing angle, and \(t_{\rm arr}\) is the observer arrival time for radius \(R\).
The output of this angular block is a set of cell weights and arrival-time
tracks; it does not change the local spectrum stored on the radius grid.

\subsubsection{Radial arrival-time bracket}

The radial arrival-time bracket finds the radius that emits photons arriving at
one requested observer time inside one angular cell.  If \(t_{\rm obs}\) lies
between the arrival times of two adjacent stored radii, the code solves
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_R&=\frac{t_{\rm obs}-t_{\rm arr}(R_i)}
{t_{\rm arr}(R_{i+1})-t_{\rm arr}(R_i)},\\
R_R&=(1-\lambda_R)R_i+\lambda_RR_{i+1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\Gamma_R=\exp\!\left[(1-\lambda_R)\ln\Gamma_i+\lambda_R\ln\Gamma_{i+1}\right],
\end{equation}
and interpolates the local SED along the same radial segment.  Here
\(\lambda_R\) is the radial interpolation weight, \(R_R\) is the emitting radius
on the arrival-time surface, and \(\Gamma_R\) is the corresponding Lorentz
factor.  The bracket produces one local radius and one local Lorentz factor for
the frequency shift and flux weight below.

\subsubsection{Doppler shift and SED accumulation}

The Doppler shift converts the observer frequency to the comoving frequency of
the emitting cell.  At the interpolated radius, the physical Doppler factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_R&=(1-\Gamma_R^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\delta_R&=\left[\Gamma_R(1-\beta_R\mu)\right]^{-1},\\
\nu'&=\frac{1+z}{\delta_R}\nu_{\rm obs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The flux contribution from this angular cell is
\begin{equation}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}=
\frac{\Delta\Omega_a}{4\pi}\,
\delta_R^3\,
\widetilde L_{\nu'}(R_R).
\end{equation}
Here \(\widetilde L_{\nu'}\) is the local SED after shell-level survival and
distance prefactors have been applied.  Frequency interpolation is delegated to
the shared log-frequency SED accumulator.  When the two bracketing source bins
are positive, it uses log-log interpolation.  Otherwise it interpolates the
linear SED values, with empty brackets contributing zero.  This step outputs the
cell contribution to \(F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}(t_{\rm obs})\); it does not reweight the
stored shell spectrum outside the equal-arrival-time sum.

\subsection{Adaptive angular quadrature}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\theta\)}{theta}-cell refinement}

The adaptive angular quadrature changes only how polar angle is sampled.  It
keeps the same local arrival-time map, Doppler factor, and frequency
interpolation.  For a trial interval \([\theta_0,\theta_1]\), it compares a
midpoint estimate \(F_{\rm coarse}\) with the sum of two midpoint estimates
\(F_{\rm fine}\) on the two half intervals.  The interval is accepted when
\(F_{\rm fine}\) has zero norm or
\begin{equation}
\sum_{\nu,t}|F_{\rm fine}-F_{\rm coarse}|
\le \epsilon_{\rm ad}
\sum_{\nu,t}|F_{\rm fine}|,
\end{equation}
or when the requested recursion depth has been reached.  Here
\(\epsilon_{\rm ad}\) is the angular-quadrature tolerance.  This path changes
angular sampling density, not the arrival-time map, Doppler weight, or frequency
interpolation.  Its output is the same observer-frame flux array as the direct
top-hat path, evaluated with a refined polar grid where the trial error demands
it.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved finite-cell projection}

\subsubsection{Finite-cell arrival map}

The finite-cell arrival map treats each downstream cell as a separate emitting
zone.  The shock front and the downstream cell do not have the same radius, so
the equal-arrival-time delay must use both radii.  For the \(\chi\)-resolved path,
the observer arrival time is
\begin{equation}
t_\chi(R)=t_0(R)+\frac{1+z}{c}
\left[R_f(R)-\mu R_\chi(R)\right],
\end{equation}
where \(R_f\) is the shock-front radius and \(R_\chi\) is the radius of the
emitting downstream cell.  The corresponding Doppler factor and comoving
frequency are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\delta_\chi&=\left[\Gamma_\chi(1-\beta_\chi\mu)\right]^{-1},\\
\nu'&=\frac{1+z}{\delta_\chi}\nu_{\rm obs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Gamma_\chi\) and \(\beta_\chi\) are the local Lorentz factor and velocity
of that downstream cell.  The output of this step is one arrival-time track and
one Doppler shift for each angular cell and each downstream cell.

\subsubsection{Radial segment search}

The radial segment search finds which stored radii bracket an observer time for
one downstream cell.  If the cell arrival-time sequence is monotonic along the
radius grid, the code uses a single moving bracket.  If it is not monotonic, the
code tests each adjacent radial segment separately.  This changes only the search
order; it does not change the arrival-time equation or the local spectrum.

\subsubsection{Cell transfer factor}

The cell transfer factor accounts for synchrotron self-absorption in front of and
inside the emitting cell.  Let \(\tau_{\rm front}=\sum_{b<\chi}\tau_b\) be the
optical depth of cells closer to the shock front, and let \(\tau_\chi\) be the
optical depth of the emitting cell.  The local escape function is
\begin{equation}
\psi(x)\equiv\frac{1-e^{-x}}{x},\qquad \psi(0)=1 .
\end{equation}
The finite-cell escape factor is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_\chi(\nu)=
\exp[-\tau_{\rm front}(\nu)]\,\psi[\tau_\chi(\nu)] ,
\end{equation}
and the projected \(\chi\)-cell contribution is
\begin{equation}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs},\chi}=
\frac{\Delta\Omega}{4\pi}\,
\delta_\chi^3\,
W_\chi\,\widetilde L_{\nu',\chi}\,
\mathcal{E}_\chi(\nu').
\end{equation}
Here \(W_\chi\) is the finite-cell weight carried by the \(\chi\)-resolved state.
The direct-electron diagnostic first constructs \(\widetilde L_{\nu',\chi}\) and
\(\tau_{\nu',\chi}\) from the \(\chi\)-local electron spectrum.  It then calls
the same \(\chi\)-projection kernel.  This branch outputs a finite-thickness
observer flux; it does not replace the electron transport step.

\subsection{Structured rings and ray-list assembly}

\subsubsection{Ring summation}

The ring summation reuses the \(\chi\)-resolved projection on precomputed
\(\theta\) rings.  The ring inputs already contain local luminosity and optical
depth grids.  The batch-luminosity variant first applies the observer prefactor
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{P}_d=\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}.
\end{equation}
It then sums the projected ring luminosities.  This assembly changes the angular
tiling, not the local \(\chi\)-cell radiation state.

\subsubsection{Ray-event construction}

The ray-event construction is used when foreground SSA must be traced along the
line of sight.  At each observer time, the ray-traced SSA variant builds a list
of emitting events \(e=(a,\chi,i,b)\), where \(a\) is the ring, \(b\) is the
azimuthal leaf, and \(i\) is the radial bracket.  Each event stores its sky
position, angular weight, cell faces, and inverse Doppler factor,
\begin{equation}
\delta_e^{-1}=\Gamma_e(1-\beta_e\mu_e).
\end{equation}
The observer direction is then aberrated into the comoving frame:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n'_r&=\frac{\mu_e-\beta_e}{1-\beta_e\mu_e},\\
n'_\theta&=\frac{n_\theta}{\Gamma_e(1-\beta_e\mu_e)},\\
n'_\phi&=\frac{n_\phi}{\Gamma_e(1-\beta_e\mu_e)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n'_r\), \(n'_\theta\), and \(n'_\phi\) are the comoving ray-direction
components used to measure path lengths through finite cells.

\subsubsection{Foreground SSA path}

The foreground SSA path converts stored radial optical depths into line-of-sight
optical depths.  The stored radial optical depth is
\(\tau_r=\alpha'_{\nu'}\Delta r'\).  For event \(e\), the local ray factor
\begin{equation}
\ell_e=\frac{\Delta s'_e}{\Delta r'_e}
\end{equation}
is the chord through the comoving slab divided by the cell comoving radial
width.  A foreground event \(h\) is accepted only when its projected sky patch
contains the emitting point and \(h\) lies in front of \(e\) along the line of
sight.  The foreground optical depth is then
\begin{equation}
\tau_{\rm front}(\nu')=\sum_h \tau_{r,h}(\nu')\,\ell_h.
\end{equation}
The ray list is therefore a geometric opacity calculation over stored SSA grids,
not a new radiation solver.

\subsubsection{Ray flux accumulation}

The ray flux accumulation multiplies the local emission by foreground absorption
and by the emitting-cell escape factor.  The event contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}=
\mathcal{P}_d\,\frac{\Delta\Omega_e}{4\pi}\,\delta_e^3\,
\widetilde L_{\nu'}\,
\exp[-\tau_{\rm front}]\,
\psi(\tau_{r,e}\ell_e).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This ray path is still a projection operator over already computed synchrotron
luminosity and SSA grids; it does not recompute the electron transport or the
synchrotron kernel inside the observer loop.

\subsection{Legacy structured-jet projection}

\subsubsection{Structured angular quadrature}

The structured angular quadrature assigns observer weights to thin-shell jet
patches.  Each patch has its own radial history and local SED before the
equal-arrival-time projection is applied.  The axisymmetric entry receives one
radius history and one comoving SED per \(\theta\) patch.  For patch \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\theta_{a,-}&=(a-1)\Delta\theta,\\
\theta_{a,+}&=a\Delta\theta,\\
\bar\theta_a&=\frac{\theta_{a,-}+\theta_{a,+}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta\theta&=\frac{\theta_j}{N_\theta},\\
\Delta\Omega_a&=(\cos\theta_{a,-}-\cos\theta_{a,+})\Delta\phi .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\bar\theta_a\) is the midpoint angle and \(\Delta\Omega_a\) is the patch
solid angle.  Cells whose midpoint lies inside the supplied excluded narrow cone
are skipped.  For the retained half-plane quadrature,
\begin{equation}
\Delta\phi=\pi/N_\phi ,
\end{equation}
the final sum is multiplied by the mirror factor \(S_\phi\).  The collapsed
\(N_\phi=1\) diagnostic uses an internal azimuthal subdivision
\(N_{\phi,\mathrm{col}}\),
\begin{equation}
\Delta\phi=\frac{\pi}{N_{\phi,\mathrm{col}}},\qquad
S_\phi=2N_{\phi,\mathrm{col}},
\end{equation}
so that \(S_\phi\Delta\phi=2\pi\), the full azimuthal measure.  The
non-axisymmetric entry instead receives patch-dependent histories
\((R,t_0,\Gamma,\widetilde L_{\nu'})_{ab}\), uses
\(\Delta\phi=2\pi/N_\phi\), and applies no mirror factor.  The companion
\texttt{SED\_interpolation\_structured} source is used internally by
\texttt{structured\_jet\_1d}; it is not exported by the package-level
\texttt{src.Interpolation} lazy binding.

\subsubsection{Patch arrival-time accumulation}

Patch arrival-time accumulation maps each structured patch to the observer grid.
Both structured entries use the same local arrival-time and Doppler map as the
top-hat kernel, but with patch-dependent radial histories:
\begin{equation}
\mu_{ab}=
\cos\theta_v\cos\bar\theta_a
+\sin\theta_v\sin\bar\theta_a\cos\phi_b,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R)&=t_{0,ab}(R)
+\frac{(1+z)R_{ab}(R)(1-\mu_{ab})}{c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a\) labels the polar patch and \(b\) labels the azimuthal patch.  For an
observer time bracketed by two stored radii,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_R&=\frac{t_{\rm obs}-t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R_i)}
{t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R_{i+1})-t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R_i)},\\
\Gamma_R&=\exp\!\left[(1-\lambda_R)\ln\Gamma_i
+\lambda_R\ln\Gamma_{i+1}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda_R\) is the radial interpolation weight.  The comoving luminosity
is interpolated on the same radial segment,
\begin{equation}
\widetilde L_{\nu',R}=(1-\lambda_R)\widetilde L_{\nu',i}
+\lambda_R\widetilde L_{\nu',i+1}.
\end{equation}
Positive bins are passed to the shared log-frequency accumulator as
\(\ln \widetilde L_{\nu',R}\), while empty bins remain empty.  The Doppler
quantities are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_R&=(1-\Gamma_R^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\delta_R&=[\Gamma_R(1-\beta_R\mu_{ab})]^{-1},\\
\nu'_R&=\frac{(1+z)\nu_{\rm obs}}{\delta_R}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Each structured patch contributes
\begin{equation}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}=
S_\phi\frac{\Delta\Omega_a}{4\pi}\,
\delta_R^3\,
\widetilde L_{\nu'_R,R},
\end{equation}
where \(S_\phi=1\) for the explicit two-dimensional patch grid.  Thus the
structured file changes the angular weights and patch histories, not the
radiative transformation itself.  The output is the component flux already
summed over the structured angular grid.

\subsection{Shared frequency-grid accumulator}

\subsubsection{Log-SED accumulator}

The log-SED accumulator is the last frequency remap in the projection kernels.
It takes a source spectrum that has already been shifted by the Doppler and
redshift factors, and it adds that spectrum to the observer grid.  Let
\(x_i=\ln\nu_i\) be the shifted source-frequency coordinate, and let
\(X_j=\ln\nu_{{\rm obs},j}\) be an observer-grid coordinate.  For
\(x_i\le X_j\le x_{i+1}\), the interpolation weight is
\begin{equation}
q=\frac{X_j-x_i}{x_{i+1}-x_i}.
\end{equation}
If both neighboring source amplitudes are positive, the accumulator uses
log-log interpolation:
\begin{equation}
F(X_j)\leftarrow F(X_j)+
\exp\!\left[(1-q)\ln S_i+q\ln S_{i+1}\right],
\end{equation}
which is exact for a local power-law SED segment.  If either endpoint is an empty
source bin, that endpoint contributes zero and the same interval is added in
linear amplitude:
\begin{equation}
F(X_j)\leftarrow F(X_j)+(1-q)S_i+qS_{i+1},
\end{equation}
so that physical zeros remain zeros.  The accumulator therefore changes only the
frequency grid; it does not change the local luminosity state.

\subsubsection{Shifted-source accumulator}

The shifted-source accumulator handles kernels that keep the source grid fixed
and pass the frequency shift as a scalar.  It evaluates the source at the target
coordinate
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
X'_j&=X_j+\Delta x,\\
W_s&=\exp(\Delta w).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
It then adds the shifted source amplitude as
\begin{equation}
F(X_j)\leftarrow F(X_j)+W_s S(X'_j),
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta x\) is the logarithmic Doppler/redshift frequency shift and
\(\Delta w\) is the logarithmic projection weight.  The value \(S(X'_j)\) is
computed by the same positive-endpoint log-log rule and the same linear rule for
nonpositive endpoints.  Both helpers evaluate the closed source interval
\([x_1,x_N]\), so a frequency exactly on a stored source endpoint is treated as
that endpoint value.

\subsection{Public projection boundaries}

\subsubsection{Supported projection kernels}

The supported projection kernels define how public light curves are assembled
from already computed component arrays.  The public light-curve path returns the
total flux and the component-resolved flux matrices.  The accepted
geometry-kernel names are \texttt{sed\_legacy}, \texttt{sed\_adaptive\_theta},
and \texttt{chi\_eats\_2d}.  The \(\chi\)-resolved projection requires a
two-dimensional electron solver and stored \(\chi\)-resolved luminosity, optical
depth, radius, bulk Lorentz factor, and volume weights.  Its finite-cell
self-absorption transfer averages through the emitting cell, and its time search
handles nonmonotonic \(\chi\)-cell arrival ordering by searching each adjacent
radial segment independently.

The \(\chi\)-resolved path replaces only the forward-shock synchrotron plus SSA
light-curve projection.  SSC, hadronic, and pair-cascade components remain
shell-level components.  An off-axis top-hat projection requires
\(N_\phi\ge2\).  The collapsed single-azimuth grid is valid only for the on-axis
case.  These checks are projection boundaries, not physical claims about the
source.

\subsection{Ray-traced synchrotron self-absorption boundary}

\subsubsection{Ray-path optical depth}

The ray-path optical depth adds foreground synchrotron self-absorption to the
\(\chi\)-resolved synchrotron projection.  Each emitting event already has a
local luminosity, a local SSA optical depth, an arrival time, and a Doppler
weight.  The ray path asks one extra question: which cells lie in front of that
event on the same line of sight?  Only those foreground cells add opacity.

The optical depth must be measured in the comoving frame of the absorbing cell.
For a ray segment in that frame,
\begin{equation}
d\tau_{\nu'}=\alpha'_{\nu'}\,ds' .
\end{equation}
The stored SSA grid gives the radial optical depth
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_r&=\alpha'_{\nu'}\Delta r',\\
\Delta r'&=\Gamma\left(r_{\rm out}-r_{\rm in}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(r_{\rm in}\) and \(r_{\rm out}\) are the lab-frame radial faces of the
cell.  A ray segment of comoving length \(\Delta s'\) therefore contributes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\ell&\equiv\frac{\Delta s'}{\Delta r'},\\
\tau_{\rm ray}&=\tau_r\ell .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\ell\) is the ray length in units of the cell comoving radial width.  For
a foreground cell \(h\), the code first tests whether the projected sky patch of
that cell contains the emitting point and whether the cell is in front of the
emitter.  If both conditions hold, the lab-frame ray segment has length
\(\Delta s_h=s_{\rm out}-s_{\rm in}>0\).  The comoving length is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu_h&=\hat{\boldsymbol n}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol e}_{r,h},\\
\Delta s'_h&=
\Gamma_h\left(1-\beta_h\mu_h\right)\Delta s_h .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\hat{\boldsymbol n}\) is the observer ray direction and
\(\hat{\boldsymbol e}_{r,h}\) is the local radial direction of the foreground
cell.  The foreground optical-depth contribution is
\begin{equation}
\tau_h=
\tau_{r,h}\,
\frac{(1-\beta_h\mu_h)\Delta s_h}
{r_{h,+}-r_{h,-}} .
\end{equation}
The foreground optical depth for emitting event \(e\) is
\begin{equation}
\tau_{{\rm front},e}=\sum_h\tau_h .
\end{equation}

The emitting-cell transfer factor averages emission through the emitting cell.
The emitting cell is treated as a uniform source and absorber over its own ray
segment.  With \(\ell_e=\Delta s'_e/\Delta r'_e\),
\begin{equation}
\psi(\tau_e\ell_e)=
\frac{1-\exp(-\tau_e\ell_e)}{\tau_e\ell_e},
\qquad
\psi(0)=1 .
\end{equation}
The final ray-traced synchrotron contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}(t_{\rm obs})&=
\sum_e{\cal W}_e \widetilde L_{\nu'_e,e}W_{\chi,e}\\
&\quad \times
\exp(-\tau_{{\rm front},e})
\psi(\tau_e\ell_e).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The event weight is
\begin{equation}
{\cal W}_e=
\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}
\frac{d\Omega_e}{4\pi}
\delta_e^3 .
\end{equation}
Here \(\widetilde L_{\nu'_e,e}\) is the local cell luminosity after frequency
interpolation, \(W_{\chi,e}\) is the finite-cell volume weight, and
\({\cal W}_e\) contains distance, angular, and Doppler factors.  This expression
is nonlocal in angle because one angular cell can absorb emission from another
angular cell.  Therefore convergence must be checked with global resolution
changes in \(\theta\), \(\phi\), \(\chi\), and radius.  A local additivity test
between two angular pieces is not a valid test for this ray-traced SSA branch.

\section{Fitting, Runtime, and Reproducibility} \label{sec:fitting}

\subsection{Active-variable map}

The active-variable map is the only place where the fitting wrapper changes a
model parameter.  The sampler proposes a coordinate \(u_j\), and the afterglow
calculation receives the physical parameter \(\vartheta_j\).  Before any radius
step is solved, the code applies
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\vartheta_j&=
\begin{cases}
10^{u_j}, & j\in{\cal P}_{\log},\\
u_j, & j\in{\cal P}_{\rm lin},\\
\vartheta_{j,0}, & j\in{\cal P}_{\rm fix},
\end{cases}\\
a_j&\le u_j\le b_j .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \({\cal P}_{\log}\), \({\cal P}_{\rm lin}\), and \({\cal P}_{\rm fix}\)
are the log, linear, and fixed parameter sets.  The numbers \(a_j\) and \(b_j\)
are the sampling bounds.  After this map, the same public model path builds the
runtime configuration, solves the radius sequence, and projects the local state
to the requested observer grid.  The fitting wrapper does not change the
dynamics, particle transport, or projection kernels.

\subsection{Observation-block residuals}

The observation block groups data points that can be evaluated on one observer
grid.  A block may contain flux densities, spectra, or band fluxes.  After one
model solve for that block, the residual code reads the required entries from
the total-flux matrix.  For flux-density and spectral data it uses
\begin{equation}
\ln\mathcal{L}
=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_i
\left[
\frac{F_i^{\rm obs}-F_i^{\rm mod}}{\sigma_i}
\right]^2 ,
\end{equation}
where \(F_i^{\rm obs}\) is the measured flux, \(F_i^{\rm mod}\) is the model
flux, and \(\sigma_i\) is the reported uncertainty.  The fixed normalization is
omitted because the implemented use compares parameter choices with fixed
uncertainties.  The likelihood changes only the score assigned to observer
products.  It does not change the hydrodynamics, electron transport, or
projection kernels.

\subsection{Band-flux quadrature}

The band-flux product is the model spectrum integrated over an observed
frequency band.  For a band observation at observer time \(t_b\), the model value
is
\begin{equation}
F_b^{\rm mod}
=\int_{\nu_1}^{\nu_2}F_\nu(t_b,\nu;\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\,d\nu
\simeq
\sum_q w_q^{\rm trap}
F_\nu(t_b,\nu_q;\boldsymbol{\vartheta}) ,
\end{equation}
where \(\nu_1\) and \(\nu_2\) are the band edges, \(\nu_q\) are the declared
logarithmic band points, and \(w_q^{\rm trap}\) are trapezoid weights.  The
residual formula above is then applied to \(F_b^{\rm mod}\).  The quadrature
therefore defines the observed product; it is not a new radiation calculation.

\subsection{Exposure quadrature}

The exposure product is the model flux averaged over a finite observing time.
For an exposure centered at \(t_e\) with duration \(\Delta t_e\), the model value
is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar F_{\nu,e}
&=\frac{1}{\Delta t_e}
\int_{t_e-\Delta t_e/2}^{t_e+\Delta t_e/2}
F_\nu(t,\nu_e;\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\,dt\\
&\simeq
\sum_q w_q^{\rm G}
F_\nu(t_{e,q},\nu_e;\boldsymbol{\vartheta}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\nu_e\) is the observed frequency, \(t_{e,q}\) are the mapped
Gauss--Legendre nodes, and \(w_q^{\rm G}\) are normalized by
\(\sum_q w_q^{\rm G}=1\).  Repeated \((t_{e,q},\nu_e)\) pairs are queried once
and then read from a cache.  The pair cache changes runtime cost only; it does
not change the physical model value.

\subsection{Runtime query cache and public products}

The runtime query cache stores observer products that have already been solved.
Its keys are the observer grid, projection kind, observer geometry, and runtime
configuration.  The cached objects are flux matrices and diagnostic tracks.  They
are read back for flux-density, spectrum, band-flux, exposure-averaged flux, and
component queries.  The cache does not evolve, renormalize, or reinterpret any
physical state.

\section{Verification and Benchmark Evidence} \label{sec:verification}

\subsection{Figure-backed state checks}

The source-data figures test whether ASGARD keeps the local state needed to
explain an observer product.  Figure~\ref{fig:radius-state} shows the radius
record, dynamical variables, and observer-time map, so it checks the coordinate
chain before any spectrum is projected.  Figure~\ref{fig:forward-api} follows the
forward-shock path from \(n(R)\), \(\Gamma(R)\), and \(B'(R)\) to spectra, light
curves, and component outputs.  Figure~\ref{fig:electron-transport} shows the
stored electron distribution, seed field, absorption state, and \(\chi\)-resolved
projection quantities.  Figure~\ref{fig:reverse-shock} shows the primary RS state
and density-jump RS registers before their flux is added to the observer total.
Appendix Figure~\ref{fig:hadronic-feedback} shows the activation thresholds and
survival factors for optional hadronic and pair branches.  Together these figures
show traceability from \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\) back to local stored quantities;
they do not by themselves establish cross-code agreement.

\subsection{Bounded validation metrics}

The validation dashboard measures bounded numerical behavior for the documented
paths.  Figure~\ref{fig:validation} uses tracked source data for four checks.
Panel (a) compares event-split dynamics with reference \(\Gamma(R)\) and
\(M_{\rm sw}(R)\) tracks for tabulated media and density jumps.  Panel (b)
measures direct angular projection against a declared angular reference grid.
Panel (c) compares the RS adaptive observer grid with the direct observer grid at
the same frequencies.  Panel (d) records the flux change introduced by transport
remapping in one-dimensional and two-dimensional characteristic diagnostics.
These metrics support the numerical paths named in the captions.  They do not
validate jet spreading, custom media, non-\(k=2\) winds, \(\chi\)-resolved
hadronic transport, or IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades.

\subsection{Cross-code benchmark evidence}

The cross-code benchmark layer tests only public-output subsets with declared
common assumptions.  The formal source tables are the FigB1, FigB2, and FigB3 CSV
files under \texttt{paper/source\_data/}.  FigB1 is the common-minimum FS
synchrotron case.  FigB2 separates an ASGARD--\vegas\ RS/SSC subset from a
Gaussian off-axis FS geometry subset.  FigB3 is an ASGARD-only state dashboard
for hadronic, pair-feedback, and \(\chi\)-resolved quantities.  Thus FigB1 and
FigB2 audit matched public outputs, while FigB3 audits state visibility inside
ASGARD.  Unsupported branches are written as unsupported and are not assigned an
error value.

\section{Discussion} \label{sec:discussion}

\subsection{Degeneracy}

Afterglow light curves are degenerate because several local processes can change
the same observer flux.  A break in \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\) may trace blast-wave
deceleration, density structure, a cooling transition, angular projection, an RS
component, SSC cooling, local absorption, or secondary particles.  ASGARD
addresses this degeneracy by storing the radius-local state before observer
projection mixes angular and radial contributions.  Figures~\ref{fig:radius-state}
--\ref{fig:reverse-shock} and Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark-complex-state} show the
local records that can be inspected before a light-curve feature is interpreted.
Figures~\ref{fig:benchmark-fs} and \ref{fig:benchmark-rs-ssc-geometry} then test
only the public-output subsets whose assumptions can be matched across codes.
This evidence supports diagnostic decomposition, not a claim that one light curve
has a unique physical cause.

\subsection{State}

The radius-ordered state is the object that makes the benchmark interpretable.
It links the dynamics, electron distribution, photon field, absorption factors,
optional RS state, optional hadronic and pair products, and observer components
at the same shock radius.  The state figures show this link inside ASGARD, and
the cross-code figures show which projected outputs can be compared to public
codes.  This separation matters because a public flux ratio does not expose the
local electron spectrum, photon field, or pair source that produced the flux.
Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark-complex-state} therefore complements
Figures~\ref{fig:benchmark-fs}--\ref{fig:benchmark-rs-ssc-geometry}: it checks
state completeness where a fair public-code ratio is not available.

\subsection{Boundaries}

The supported-state record also defines where the method stops.  A coupled
physical branch is supported only when ASGARD has a local state, a projection
rule, and validation evidence for that branch.  Table~\ref{tab:cross-code-capabilities}
and Section~\ref{sec:limits} make this rule explicit for comparison codes,
runtime configurations, and coupled-physics branches.  Unsupported combinations
are listed as unsupported rather than inferred from partial fields.  The boundary
list is therefore part of the method, not a software footnote.

\section{Model Boundaries} \label{sec:limits}

\subsection{Runtime configuration boundaries}

The runtime configuration boundary states which inputs have a complete
radius-ordered state sequence and observer projection.  Let \(N_{\rho,\max}\) be
the tabulated-medium capacity, and let \(N_{J,\max}\) be the density-jump
register length.  A setup outside the following list is not a supported physical
model in this manuscript.
\begin{itemize}
\item External media are supported as uniform media, \(k=2\) stellar winds, or
tabulated radial profiles.  User-defined medium kernels are unsupported.
\item A tabulated medium requires \(2\le N_\rho\le N_{\rho,\max}\) positive
radius-density points, ordered by increasing radius.
\item Density jumps require \(N_J\le N_{J,\max}\) Gaussian jumps on an analytic
medium.
\item Jet spreading is unsupported.  The angular elements keep the opening
angles specified by the initial jet profile.
\item Adaptive observer-time grids are supported only for direct top-hat
kernel-backed models.
\item The structured Fortran backend requires uniform patch sampling and either
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} or \texttt{dg\_1d}.
\item Non-uniform dominant-region sampling is a patch-backend option; it is not
supported by the structured Fortran backend.
\item Sky-image and synchrotron-Stokes products are unsupported by the current
manuscript evidence set.
\item Thermal electrons are supported only by the one-dimensional implicit
electron solvers.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Coupled-physics boundaries}

The coupled-physics boundary states which extra radiation branches have both a
local state and an observer projection.  A branch without both objects is not
used as a model prediction.
\begin{itemize}
\item Formal hadronic transport requires a one-dimensional forward-electron
solver.
\item The legacy hadronic branch does not support \(p\gamma\) interactions or
neutrino channels.
\item Joint electron-photon coupling requires the one-dimensional implicit
electron solver, the AM3-based one-dimensional hadronic solver, the
Hummer--2010 photohadronic response, Bethe-Heitler physics, numeric IC/KN
cooling, no reverse shock, no \(\chi\)-resolved transport, and fixed electron
substeps.
\item Multi-density reverse-shock v1 requires \(p_r>2\).  It does not support
hadronic processes, forward SSC, reverse SSC, cross-zone IC, or joint
electron-photon coupling.
\item The current formal hadronic path is shell-averaged; it is not
\(\chi\)-resolved.
\item Pair-cascade iterations support shell-sequence \(\gamma\gamma\) pair/synch
feedback.  IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades are unsupported.
\item The \(\chi\)-resolved projection replaces only forward-shock synchrotron
plus SSA projection.  It does not make SSC, hadronic emission, or pair cascades
\(\chi\)-local.
\end{itemize}
These limits are part of the published method.  A coupled case outside this list
has no manuscript-level support unless it has its own local state, projection
operator, and validation evidence.

\section{Summary} \label{sec:summary}

\asgard\ is a bounded GRB afterglow method for tracing observer features back to
local physical state.  At each radius, the state \(\mathcal{S}(R)\) records the
blast-wave dynamics, electron distribution, photon fields, optional hadronic
species, reverse-shock variables, absorption factors, and observer components.
The code updates these objects before applying the equal-arrival-time projection.
This ordering is the main contribution of the paper: a complex light curve can be
read as local dynamics, particles, photons, absorption, components, and
projection rather than only as a final \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\).

The evidence for this method is reproducible from tracked source data.  The
figures test the radius state, forward-shock API products, electron and photon
transport, \(\chi\)-resolved projection, reverse-shock diagnostics,
hadronic/pair activation checks, the bounded validation dashboard, matched
public-output subsets, and the ASGARD-only state dashboard.  The capability table
states which entries have numerical rows and which entries are explicit
boundaries.  The PyBlastAfterglowMag source is pinned, its repo-local
\texttt{pba.out} is compiled, and its numerical rows are quoted only for the
matched FS and Gaussian-geometry subsets.

The supported use cases are exactly the cases with state, projection, and
validation evidence.  ASGARD supports the runtime and coupled-physics domains
listed in Section~\ref{sec:limits}.  It does not support jet spreading,
user-defined medium kernels, wind profiles with \(k\ne2\), \(\chi\)-resolved
hadronic transport, IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades, or coupled branches
without a validated observer projection.  These boundaries are part of the
method because they state which physical interpretations can be drawn from the
exported state.

\appendix
\setcounter{figure}{0}
\renewcommand{\thefigure}{A\arabic{figure}}
\renewcommand{\theHfigure}{A\arabic{figure}}

\section{Electron Solvers} \label{app:electron-solvers}

This appendix records the electron-solver mechanics that are too detailed for
the main method flow.  The main text keeps the physical continuity equation,
source terms, cooling terms, one representative update, outputs, and boundaries.
Here we keep the coordinate maps, quadrature rules, remaps, sweep coefficients,
and branch-specific transport formulas.  All electron energy grids in this
appendix use the natural-log reference coordinate \(x_g=\ln\gamma_e\), unless a
different storage coordinate is defined locally.

\subsection{Initial-Spectrum Quadrature and Coordinate Maps}

The initial electron spectrum must be converted into cell averages before any
conservative transport step.  Let \(x_g=\ln\gamma_e\), and let
\(N_x=dN_e/dx_g\) be the electron density per natural-log energy interval.  For
any continuous segment of \(N_x\), the fixed Gauss--Legendre rule is
\begin{equation}
\int_a^b F(x)\,dx\simeq
\frac{b-a}{2}\sum_{q=1}^{N_{\rm G}} w_q^{\rm G}
F\!\left[\frac{a+b}{2}+\frac{b-a}{2}\xi_q\right],
\end{equation}
where \(F(x)\) is the segment being integrated and
\((\xi_q,w_q^{\rm G})\) are fixed nodes and weights.  If a solver stores a
coordinate \(y\) rather than \(x_g\), the same physical spectrum is stored as
\begin{equation}
N_{y,j}^{\rm init}=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}
N_x[x_g(y)]\,\frac{dx_g}{dy}\,dy .
\end{equation}
This equation is only a change of variables.  It does not change the injected
electron spectrum.

The log-gamma branch stores the natural-log energy coordinate directly.  In that
branch,
\begin{equation}
y=x_g,\qquad \gamma_e(y)=\exp(y),\qquad \frac{dx_g}{dy}=1.
\end{equation}
The four-velocity branch stores a different coordinate so that low-energy and
high-energy parts of the spectrum can be represented on one grid.  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
s\equiv\gamma_s^2-1,\\
\mathcal{Y}(\gamma)\equiv
\ln\left[1+\frac{\gamma^2-1}{s}\right].
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The stored coordinate is \(y=\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_e)\).  The inverse map and the
Jacobian used in the cell-average conversion are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_e(y)&=\left[1+s(\exp(y)-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
x_g(y)&=\ln\gamma_e(y),\\
\frac{dx_g}{dy}&=\frac{s\exp(y)}{2\gamma_e^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\gamma_s\) sets where the coordinate changes from logarithmic
four-velocity behavior to logarithmic energy behavior.  It is a coordinate
parameter, not a radiative cutoff.

The electron grid is uniform in the storage coordinate \(y\).  A grid with
\(N_\gamma\) cells has
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y_{i+1/2}&=y_L+\frac{i}{N_\gamma}(y_H-y_L),\\
y_L&=\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_{\min}),\qquad
y_H=\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_{\max}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The diagnostic arrays returned to the rest of the code are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x_{g,i+1/2}&=x_g(y_{i+1/2}),\\
\gamma_i&=\gamma_e\!\left[(y_{i-1/2}+y_{i+1/2})/2\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The conditions \(\gamma_s>1\) and \(\gamma_{\max}>\gamma_{\min}\) give a nonzero
coordinate interval.  The output of this setup is a conservative storage grid
and the Jacobian needed to recover \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\).

\subsection{Conservative Transport Helper Details}

Diagnostics need \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\), even when the solver stores cell averages in
\(y\).  For this diagnostic conversion, the code reconstructs a positive
exponential profile inside each cell:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN_e}{dy}=A_j\exp[\sigma_j(y-y_j)],
\qquad
y_j=\frac{y_{j-1/2}+y_{j+1/2}}{2}.
\end{equation}
For an interior cell with positive neighboring averages, define
\begin{equation}
\ell_j^-=\frac{\ln N_{y,j}-\ln N_{y,j-1}}{y_j-y_{j-1}},
\qquad
\ell_j^+=\frac{\ln N_{y,j+1}-\ln N_{y,j}}{y_{j+1}-y_j}.
\end{equation}
The monotone log-slope is
\begin{equation}
\sigma_j=\operatorname{minmod}(\ell_j^-,\ell_j^+),
\end{equation}
and \(\sigma_j=0\) for cells that do not have positive neighboring averages.
The cell average fixes the amplitude:
\begin{equation}
A_j=
\begin{cases}
N_{y,j}, & z_j=0,\\
N_{y,j}\,z_j/\sinh z_j, & z_j\ne0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
z_j=\frac{1}{2}\sigma_j\Delta y_j .
\end{equation}
The centered diagnostic electron density is then
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j
=
\frac{A_j}{d\gamma_e/dy}
=
\frac{A_j}{\gamma_j(dx_g/dy)_j}.
\end{equation}
This conversion is diagnostic only.  It lets one-dimensional, \(\chi\)-resolved,
and DG drivers export a shell-centered \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) record without changing
the transported cell averages.

A cooling sweep moves electrons from high energy to low energy while preserving
cell contents in conservative form.  Let \(N_j\) be the electron number in energy
cell \(j\).  A face coefficient \(\lambda_{j+1/2}\) gives the number flux across
the face between cells \(j\) and \(j+1\), and \(b_0\) gives the diagonal weight of
the implicit step.  The sweep coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j&=b_0-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\qquad p_1=p_2,\\
c_j&=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j+p_{j+1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Starting at the highest populated bin, the solver evaluates
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_N^{n+1}&=r_N,\\
N_j^{n+1}&=\Pi_+\!\left(r_j-c_jN_{j+1}^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}
\end{equation}
Here \(r_j\) is the known right-hand side after source injection and diagonal
division.  \(\Pi_+\) projects the computed cell content onto the non-negative
domain used by this conservative electron solver.  The output is the cooled
cell-average spectrum \(N_j^{n+1}\).

Electron remaps move conserved cell content between old and new energy cells.  In
this subsection \(x\) denotes the natural-log coordinate \(x_g=\ln\gamma_e\).
Each remap asks how much old spectrum lies between two traced cell faces.  For
point data, the local second-order interpolant is
\begin{equation}
P_2(x)=\sum_{a=0}^{2}y_a\ell_a(x),
\qquad
\ell_a(x)=\prod_{b\ne a}\frac{x-x_b}{x_a-x_b}.
\end{equation}
Here \(y_a\) are the three neighboring point values and \(\ell_a\) are the
Lagrange basis functions.  For cell averages, the code reconstructs the content
inside each cell with a parabolic profile.  In the local coordinate
\(\xi=(x-x_{j-1/2})/\Delta x_j\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Q_j(\xi)&=Q_{L,j}
+(Q_{R,j}-Q_{L,j}+Q_{6,j})\xi
-Q_{6,j}\xi^2,\\
Q_{6,j}&=6Q_j-3(Q_{L,j}+Q_{R,j}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Q_j\) is the cell average, \(Q_{L,j}\) and \(Q_{R,j}\) are the left and
right interface values, and \(Q_{6,j}\) is chosen so that the cell average is
exactly \(Q_j\).  The interface values are restricted to neighboring extrema.  A
local extremum uses \(Q_{L,j}=Q_{R,j}=Q_j\).  If the parabolic profile would put
negative content inside a positive cell, the interface deviations are rescaled by
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\theta_j&=\frac{Q_j}{Q_j-Q_{\min,j}},\\
Q_{L,R}&\leftarrow Q_j+\theta_j(Q_{L,R}-Q_j),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{\min,j}\) is the minimum of the parabolic profile in the cell.  This
operation keeps the cell average unchanged.  The integral over any part of the
cell is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal M}_j(\xi_l,\xi_r)
&=\Delta x_j\left[
Q_LD_1+\frac{Q_R-Q_L+Q_6}{2}D_2\right.\\
&\left.\hspace{3.0em}
-\frac{Q_6}{3}D_3
\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_m\equiv\xi_r^m-\xi_l^m\).  A prefix sum gives
\(M(x)=\int_{x_{1/2}}^xQ(x')\,dx'\).  The remap uses \(M(x)\) to move exactly the
old cell content that lies between two traced faces.

Analytic source terms are reconstructed separately from the transported
spectrum.  This matters because an injected power law can span many decades in
\(\gamma_e\).  If three neighboring source-cell averages are positive, the
logarithmic slope is
\begin{equation}
\sigma_j=\operatorname{minmod}\left[
\frac{\ln Q_j-\ln Q_{j-1}}{x_j-x_{j-1}},
\frac{\ln Q_{j+1}-\ln Q_j}{x_{j+1}-x_j}
\right],
\end{equation}
and \(\sigma_j=0\) otherwise.  With this slope, the reconstructed source in cell
\(j\) is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Q_j(x)&=A_j\exp[\sigma_j(x-x_j)],\\
A_j&=Q_jG(z_j),\qquad z_j=\frac{\sigma_j\Delta x_j}{2},\\
G(z)&=
\begin{cases}
1, & z=0,\\
z/\sinh z, & z\ne0.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_j\) is chosen so that the cell average remains \(Q_j\).  The integral of
this source profile is
\begin{equation}
{\cal S}_j(x_l,x_r)=
\begin{cases}
A_j\Delta x_{lr}, & \sigma_j=0,\\
A_j[E_j(x_r)-E_j(x_l)]/\sigma_j, & \sigma_j\ne0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta x_{lr}\equiv x_r-x_l\) and \(E_j(x)\equiv\exp[\sigma_j(x-x_j)]\).
For diagnostics, the natural-log coordinate content is converted to a centered
physical spectrum by
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j
=\frac{A_j}{\gamma_j},
\end{equation}
where \(x=\ln\gamma_e\), \(\gamma_j=\exp(x_j)\), and \(A_j\) is the
reconstruction amplitude of the transported \(dN_e/dx\) cell.

\subsection{Characteristic and Legacy Remap Details}

The source-coupled characteristic quadrature places source samples along the
radial step.  It uses \(N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}\) Gauss points and the map
\begin{equation}
s_m=t_m^{q_{\rm C}},\qquad
w_m=q_{\rm C}W_mt_m^{q_{\rm C}-1},
\end{equation}
where \(q_{\rm C}\) is the declared source-time clustering index and
\((t_m,W_m)\) are the Gauss nodes and weights on \([0,1]\).  The output is a set
of source locations \(s_m\) and weights \(w_m\) inside the radial interval.

The direct characteristic solver traces radiative cooling in
\(u=1/\gamma_e\).  With \(u_j=1/\gamma_{e,j}\), it reconstructs
\begin{equation}
w(u)=uD(u)
\end{equation}
inside each \(u\) cell as \(w(u)=A_u+S_u u\).  Adding adiabatic cooling gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{du}{dR}&=A_u+(S_u+b_{\rm ad})u,\\
b_{\rm ad}&=R_s^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The analytic backward map is applied inside the current \(u\) cell.  If the
traced point reaches a cell edge, the remaining radial lag is evaluated in the
adjacent cell with the new coefficients.  The result is the departure position
for each arrival face.

The conservative characteristic remap then uses two prefix integrals.  Let
\(M_N(x)\) be the prefix integral of the parabolic reconstruction of the
transported spectrum \(N_x\), and let \(M_Q(x)\) be the prefix integral of the
source reconstruction \(Q_x^{\rm C}\).  For arrival cell \(j\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal I}_N(j,\ell)&=
\frac{M_N[x_{j+1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
-M_N[x_{j-1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]}{\Delta x_j},\\
{\cal I}_Q(j,\ell)&=
\frac{M_Q[x_{j+1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
-M_Q[x_{j-1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]}{\Delta x_j}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The updated cell average is
\begin{equation}
N_{x,j}^{n+1}={\cal I}_N(j,\Delta R)
+\Delta R\sum_{q=1}^{N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}}w_q{\cal I}_Q(j,s_q\Delta R).
\end{equation}
The first term carries the old electrons along cooling characteristics.  The
second term adds source electrons at the quadrature locations along the same
radial step.

The uniform-grid semi-Lagrangian branch moves the natural-log spectrum by tracing
cell faces.  Its source label is \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}\).  For any prefix integral
\(M\), define
\begin{equation}
\mathcal I_j(M;x_-,x_+)\equiv\frac{M(x_+)-M(x_-)}{\Delta x}.
\end{equation}
Here \(x_-\) and \(x_+\) are the two departure faces of cell \(j\), and
\(\mathcal I_j\) returns the average content between them.  The branch first adds
half of the source and traces the faces back:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{1/2}&=\Pi_+\!\left(N_j^n+\frac{\Delta R}{2}Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}\right),\\
x_{j\pm1/2}^{\rm dep}
&=\Pi_x\!\left(x_{j\pm1/2}+\Delta R\,f_{j\pm1/2}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(f_{j\pm1/2}\) is the face drift and \(\Pi_x\) keeps the departure point
inside the computational \(x\) domain.  The final cell average is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}
=\Pi_+\!\left[
\mathcal I_j(M_{1/2};x_{j-1/2}^{\rm dep},x_{j+1/2}^{\rm dep})
+\frac{\Delta R}{2}Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}
\right].
\end{equation}
This formula says that old electrons are integrated from the traced departure
interval, while the source is split on the two sides of the cooling remap.

The three-level natural-log solver uses the triangular cooling sweep with two
sets of coefficients.  Its source label is \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm 3}\).  During startup,
the backward-Euler diagonal, source-loaded right-hand side, and coupling
coefficient are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j^{(1)}&=1-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\qquad p_1^{(1)}=p_2^{(1)},\\
r_j^{(1)}&=\frac{N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm 3}}{p_j^{(1)}},\\
c_j^{(1)}&=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j^{(1)}+p_{j+1}^{(1)}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
After two history states are available, the branch uses the second-order history
state.  The coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j^{(3/2)}&=\frac{3}{2}-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\qquad
p_1^{(3/2)}=p_2^{(3/2)},\\
r_j^{(3/2)}&=
\frac{2N_j^n-\frac{1}{2}N_j^{n-1}+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm 3}}
{p_j^{(3/2)}},\\
c_j^{(3/2)}&=
\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j^{(3/2)}+p_{j+1}^{(3/2)}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These coefficients feed the same backward sweep in
Equation~\ref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}.  The output is a conservative
natural-log cell-average spectrum.

The WENO-Z natural-log branch reconstructs the explicit face flux from a
five-cell stencil.  Its source label is \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm W}\).  For a left-biased
interface, let \(f_m=F_{i+m}\).  The three candidate fluxes are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat f_0&=\frac{1}{3}f_{-2}-\frac{7}{6}f_{-1}+\frac{11}{6}f_0,\\
\hat f_1&=-\frac{1}{6}f_{-1}+\frac{5}{6}f_0+\frac{1}{3}f_1,\\
\hat f_2&=\frac{1}{3}f_0+\frac{5}{6}f_1-\frac{1}{6}f_2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The Jiang--Shu smoothness indicators are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_0&=\frac{13}{12}(f_{-2}-2f_{-1}+f_0)^2
+\frac{1}{4}(f_{-2}-4f_{-1}+3f_0)^2,\\
\beta_1&=\frac{13}{12}(f_{-1}-2f_0+f_1)^2
+\frac{1}{4}(f_1-f_{-1})^2,\\
\beta_2&=\frac{13}{12}(f_0-2f_1+f_2)^2
+\frac{1}{4}(3f_0-4f_1+f_2)^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The right-biased flux used for \(v_x>0\) is the mirror of the same formula over
\((f_{i-1},f_i,f_{i+1},f_{i+2},f_{i+3})\).  Constant ghost cells at both energy
ends provide the five-point stencil; they are numerical boundary states and are
not returned as physical electron bins.

The WENO radial update applies the reconstructed flux in three explicit stages:
\begin{equation}
N_j^{(1)}=N_j^n+\lambda_W\mathcal{D}_j(N^n)+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm W},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
N_j^{(2)}=\frac{3}{4}N_j^n+
\frac{1}{4}\left[N_j^{(1)}+
\lambda_W\mathcal{D}_j(N^{(1)})\right]
+\frac{1}{4}\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm W},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}=\frac{1}{3}N_j^n+
\frac{2}{3}\left[N_j^{(2)}+
\lambda_W\mathcal{D}_j(N^{(2)})\right]
+\frac{2}{3}\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm W}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\lambda_W=\Delta R/\Delta x\) and
\(\mathcal D_j=F_{j+1/2}-F_{j-1/2}\).  The output is an explicit
finite-difference update of the natural-log electron spectrum, with source
injection added at each stage.

The DG active mesh chooses which electron-energy elements need polynomial
transport.  It keeps a high-energy tail only when the old spectrum still carries
enough high-energy moment.  For a trial upper cutoff \(\gamma_u\), the source
support endpoint is
\begin{equation}
x_{\rm src}^{\max}=
\ln\left[\min(\gamma_{\max}^{\rm grid},\eta_{\rm tail}\gamma_u)\right],
\end{equation}
and the active high-energy edge is kept when
\begin{equation}
\frac{\mathcal{M}_2(x_{\rm src}^{\max})}{\mathcal{M}_2^{\rm tot}}
>\epsilon_{\rm tail},
\qquad
\mathcal{M}_2^{\rm tot}\equiv\int N_y\gamma_e^2\,dy .
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal M_2\) is a diagnostic high-energy moment of the stored spectrum.
It selects the active DG domain; it is not a physical electron cutoff.

The density-jump radial substepper chooses smaller radial steps near declared
density structures.  It starts from
\begin{equation}
\Delta R_{\rm DG}=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{N_{\rm DG}}.
\end{equation}
For a Gaussian density jump with width \(\sigma_j=w_jR_j\), candidate steps are
cut at the marked jump window,
\begin{equation}
R_j+\sigma_j\mathcal{Z}_{\rm J},
\end{equation}
and inside the active jump window the step obeys
\begin{equation}
\Delta R\le \frac{\sigma_j}{N_{\rm J}}.
\end{equation}
The final step bound uses the local density-gradient scale:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
g_n(R)&=1+\sum_j(f_j-1)
\exp\left[-\frac{(R-R_j)^2}{2\sigma_j^2}\right],\\
\Delta R&\le \frac{\eta_{\nabla n}}{|d\ln g_n/dR|}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These bounds control the radial DG work near rapid external-density changes.

The DG source template is normalized to the conservative finite-cell source
integral in the same \(y\) measure:
\begin{equation}
\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}\leftarrow
\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}
\frac{\sum_j \bar Q_{y,j}\Delta y_j}
{\int \widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}\,dy}.
\end{equation}

The DG source projection stores that source as Legendre moments inside each
active element.  With \(P_m\) denoting the local Legendre polynomial and \(r(y)\)
the element coordinate, the coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\widehat Q_{am}&=
\frac{2m+1}{\Delta y_a}
\int_{y_{a,L}}^{y_{a,R}}Q_y(y)P_m[r(y)]\,dy,\\
Q_{a,i}&=\sum_{m=0}^{p_{\rm DG}}\widehat Q_{am}P_m(r_i).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The integral is evaluated by Gauss--Legendre quadrature.  The reverse-shock
kinetic source uses a normalized shape in the same coordinate:
\begin{equation}
\Phi_y^{\rm K}=A_{\rm K}\gamma_e
(\gamma_e-1)^{-p}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma_e)
\frac{dx_g}{dy}\,\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_e\ge\gamma_m},
\end{equation}
where \(A_{\rm K}\) is fixed by the requested shell-integrated kinetic-source
content.

The DG implicit transport block advances the polynomial coefficients across one
radial step.  It is assembled from the Legendre--Gauss--Lobatto derivative
matrix.  For element \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_{a,ij}&=\delta_{ij}
-\Delta R
\frac{2}{\Delta y_a}
\frac{w_j}{w_i}D_{ji}v_{y,j},\\
\sum_j A_{a,ij}N_{a,j}^{n+1}
&=N_{a,i}^{n}+\Delta R\,Q_{a,i}
+\mathcal{F}_{a,i}^{\rm up},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_{ji}=\left.dL_i/dr\right|_{r_j}\), \(w_i\) are the LGL quadrature
weights, and \(\mathcal{F}_{a,i}^{\rm up}\) is the upwind boundary
contribution.  This block is the local form of the compact main-text operator
\(\mathcal A_a\).

The DG conservative remap carries the same electron Lorentz factor from the old
coordinate to the new coordinate.  The nodal density is mapped as
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm n}(y_{\rm n})=
N_{\rm o}(y_{\rm o})
\frac{dy_{\rm o}}{dy_{\rm n}}
=
N_{\rm o}(y_{\rm o})
\frac{(dx_g/dy)_{\rm n}}{(dx_g/dy)_{\rm o}} .
\end{equation}
After a projection or radial step, the element-local positivity operator keeps
the element average
\begin{equation}
\bar N_a=\frac{1}{2}\sum_iw_iN_{a,i}
\end{equation}
fixed and replaces nodal values by
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_{a,i}&\leftarrow
\bar N_a+\theta_a(N_{a,i}-\bar N_a),\\
\theta_a&=\min\left[1,\frac{\bar N_a}{\bar N_a-N_{a,\min}}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
when \(N_{a,\min}<0<\bar N_a\).  A final number-conservation factor matches the
stored electron number \(N_{\rm tot}\) after the remap.  These operations act on
DG nodal values; they do not define a new physical cooling process.

The DG characteristic projector uses the analytic affine cooling map.  Define
\(\eta_b=\exp(-b_{\rm ad}\Delta R)\) and \(\zeta_b=1-\eta_b\).  The forward map
over a radial lag \(\Delta R\) is
\begin{equation}
\gamma_e(R+\Delta R)=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle \frac{\gamma_e(R)}
{1+a_{\rm rad}\gamma_e(R)\Delta R}, &
b_{\rm ad}=0,\\[1.1ex]
\displaystyle
\frac{\gamma_e(R)\eta_b}
{1+(a_{\rm rad}/b_{\rm ad})\gamma_e(R)
\zeta_b}, & {\rm otherwise},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
and the inverse map for an arrival energy \(\gamma_{\rm now}\) is
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{\rm back}=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle \frac{\gamma_{\rm now}}
{1-a_{\rm rad}\gamma_{\rm now}\Delta R}, &
b_{\rm ad}=0,\\[1.1ex]
\displaystyle \frac{\gamma_{\rm now}}
{\eta_b-(a_{\rm rad}/b_{\rm ad})\zeta_b\gamma_{\rm now}}, &
{\rm otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Gauss--Legendre quadrature and Legendre modal moments then project the upstream
polynomial onto the arrival element.  The output is a DG representation of the
cooled electron spectrum on the arrival grid.

The implicit flux-split solver writes cooling as a flux through cell faces.  With
face velocity \(v_{j+1/2}\), the conservative update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{n+1}&=N_j^n+\Delta RQ_j+F_{j+1/2}-F_{j-1/2},\\
F_{j+1/2}&=\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x_j}
\left(v_{j+1/2}^{+}N_{j+1}^{n+1}
+v_{j+1/2}^{-}N_j^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(F_{j+1/2}\) is the flux through the right face of cell \(j\).  The signs
\(v^+\) and \(v^-\) select the upwind cell for the implicit step, and
\(\Delta x_j\) is the local cell width.  A pre-integrated sequence uses the same
matrix after replacing \(\Delta R\,v_{j+1/2}\) by the face displacement
\({\cal F}_{j+1/2}\) and \(\Delta RQ_j\) by the source content \({\cal Q}_j\).

The natural-log \texttt{fullhide} branch uses the same high-to-low triangular
solve for a fixed grid.  Its face speed and sweep coefficient are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
v_{j+1/2}&=D_{j+1/2}+\frac{1}{R},\\
\lambda_{j+1/2}&=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}v_{j+1/2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_{j+1/2}\) is the radiative cooling coefficient at the cell face and
the \(1/R\) term is the adiabatic drift in \(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  The linear system
is written as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j&=1-\lambda_{j-1/2},\\
c_j&=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j+p_{j+1}},\\
r_j&=\frac{N_j^n+\Delta RQ_j}{p_j},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and is solved by the high-to-low sweep in Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}.
The space--radius \texttt{fullhide} branch applies the same solve over a
sequence of source and coupling columns.  For substep \(m\),
\begin{equation}
(1+\lambda_{j-1/2}^{m})N_j^{m}
-\lambda_{j+1/2}^{m}N_{j+1}^{m}
=N_j^{m-1}+S_j^{m},
\end{equation}
where \(S_j^m\) is the source added during that substep.  The lowest- and
highest-energy rows use their single adjacent face.  After all
\(m=1,\ldots,N_{\rm step}\) columns are solved, the last column is the updated
electron spectrum.

The transport routines also return diagnostic support ranges for later radiation
calls.  These ranges tell the radiation kernels which energy and \(\chi\) cells
are populated; they do not define new physical cutoffs.  A spectral peak sampled
on three positive neighboring bins is estimated by a log-parabola through
\((\ln\nu_{l,c,r},\ln P_{l,c,r})\):
\begin{equation}
\ln\nu_{\rm pk}
=x_c+\frac{(y_l-y_r)(x_c-x_l)}
{2(y_l-2y_c+y_r)},
\end{equation}
with the result restricted to the bracketing interval.  The active energy and
\(\chi\) ranges used by the two-dimensional driver are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i_{\rm act}&=\max\left[i_{\rm lo},\,i_{\rm src},\,
\min(N_\gamma,i_{\rm pop}+1)\right],\\
Q_i&>\epsilon_\gamma Q_{\rm pk},\qquad
N_i>\epsilon_\gamma\max(N_{\rm pk},Q_{\rm pk}),\\
a_{\rm act}&=\min(N_\chi,a_{\rm pop}+1),\\
N_{\chi,a}&>\epsilon_\chi N_{\chi,\rm pk}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(i_{\rm lo}\) is the lower transported energy index.  The quantities
\(Q_{\rm pk}\), \(N_{\rm pk}\), and \(N_{\chi,\rm pk}\) are peak source,
energy-population, and \(\chi\)-population amplitudes.  The thresholds
\(\epsilon_\gamma\) and \(\epsilon_\chi\) limit the transport scan to populated
cells.  The radial substep estimate uses the largest active energy drift,
\begin{equation}
\xi_{\max}=\max_{a\le a_{\rm act},\,i<i_{\rm act}}
\left|D_{ia}+A_{{\rm ad},a}\right|,
\end{equation}
where \(D_{ia}\) is the radiative drift and \(A_{{\rm ad},a}\) is the adiabatic
drift.  If the active-population scan finds no active drift, the routine reports
the substep size from the stored grid rather than from a smaller active subset.

\subsection{\texttt{fullhide\_1d} Fixed-Substep Details}

The \texttt{fullhide\_1d} fixed-substep branch resolves source injection and
cooling across one radial shell.  With \(\Delta R_i=R_i-R_{i-1}\), the substep
count and width are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_i&=\max\!\left[
N_{\rm fh,min},
\min\!\left(N_{\rm fh,max},
\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta R_i}{\Delta R_0}\right\rfloor\right)
\right],\\
\Delta R&=\frac{\Delta R_i}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{\rm fh,min}\) and \(N_{\rm fh,max}\) are declared algorithm controls.
Each substep has radial width \(\Delta R\), and the output of the branch is the
electron spectrum at \(R_i\).

In a uniform medium without the thermal-electron branch, the fixed substeps can
be accumulated into one implicit solve.  The substep radii are
\begin{equation}
R_r=R_{i-1}+r\Delta R,\qquad r=1,\ldots,L_i .
\end{equation}
The total nonthermal source normalization over the shell is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
=a_{p,i}\sum_{r=1}^{L_i}\Delta R\,q_R(R_r,\Delta R),
\qquad a_{p,i}\equiv a_p(R_i).
\end{equation}
The source shape on the \(y\) grid is
\begin{equation}
\phi_y(R,y)\equiv
\gamma_e^{1-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma_e)
\frac{dx_g}{dy}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal{C}_{\max}\) is the high-energy cutoff factor.  The source content
added to cell \(j\) is
\begin{equation}
\bar Q_{y,j}^{\rm int}
=\frac{\mathcal Q_i}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\phi_y(R_i,y)\,dy ,
\end{equation}
and the integrated cooling displacement through a face is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}=
\frac{\Delta R_i\bar D_{j+1/2}
+\sum_{r=1}^{L_i}\Delta R/R_r}
{(dx_g/dy)_{j+1/2}} .
\end{equation}
The merged solve uses the same tridiagonal matrix as the one-substep update.  It
replaces
\(\Delta R\,v_{j+1/2}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}\) and
\(\Delta R\,Q_j\rightarrow\bar Q_{y,j}^{\rm int}\), so that the shell-integrated
source and cooling displacement enter one conservative solve.

Non-uniform density and thermal electrons use explicit radial substeps.  At each
substep endpoint \(R_r\), the solver re-evaluates \(n(R_r)\), \(B'\),
\(\gamma_{\max}\), and \(\gamma_m\).  The local nonthermal source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{y,j}^{(r)}
=\frac{q_R(R_r,\Delta R)a_p(R_r)}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\phi_y(R_r,y)\,dy ,
\end{equation}
and a Maxwell--Juttner source is added when \texttt{thermal\_electrons} is
enabled.  The radiative cooling coefficient is assembled once from the shell
photon field and then scaled with the local density:
\begin{equation}
D_j(R_r)=D_j(R_{i-1})\frac{n(R_r)}{n(R_{i-1})}.
\end{equation}
This scaling is exact for local synchrotron cooling when \(\bar\Gamma_i\) and the
shell photon shape are fixed.  For the full cooling array, it is the supported
density-response rule.

\subsection{Coupled \texttt{fullhide} Electron--Photon Coefficients}

The coupled electron--photon pass uses the same triangular primitive as the
separated \texttt{fullhide\_1d} solve.  For a fixed radial substep, the diagonal
coefficient and the source-loaded right-hand side are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j&=1-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\qquad p_1=p_2,\\
r_j&=\frac{N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}}{p_j},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{x,j}\) includes primary injection and secondary pairs on the natural
logarithmic grid.  Adjacent cells are coupled by
\begin{equation}
c_j=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j+p_{j+1}} .
\end{equation}
These coefficients feed the high-to-low sweep in
Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}.  They change the local electron
spectrum only through the supplied cooling field and secondary source.

\subsection{Hybrid Thermal \texttt{fullhide} Details}

The hybrid thermal branch builds one injection source from a thermal part below
\(\gamma_m\) and a nonthermal tail above \(\gamma_m\).  It needs normalization
integrals, not a new transport equation.  The upper incomplete gamma function
used for the cutoff power-law tail is
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(s,x)=\int_x^\infty t^{s-1}e^{-t}\,dt,
\end{equation}
and the modified Bessel functions used for the Maxwell--Juttner moments are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
K_\nu(z)&=\int_0^\infty e^{-z\cosh u}\cosh(\nu u)\,du,\\
\nu&\in\{0,1\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The numerical routines \texttt{dgamic}, \texttt{dbsk0e}, and \texttt{dbsk1e}
evaluate these functions.  They provide the cutoff power-law normalization
\begin{equation}
\int_{\gamma_m}^{\infty}\gamma^{-p}e^{-\gamma/\gamma_{\max}}\,d\gamma
=\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}
\Gamma\!\left(1-p,\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_{\max}}\right),
\end{equation}
and the Maxwell--Juttner moments used in the thermal source.

The thermal source uses only the Maxwell--Juttner content below \(\gamma_m\).
With \(\gamma=\cosh u\),
\begin{equation}
\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}\,d\gamma=\sinh^2u\,du .
\end{equation}
The complete relativistic moments are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
J_1(\Theta)&=2\Theta^2K_1(\Theta^{-1})
+\Theta K_0(\Theta^{-1}),\\
J_2(\Theta)&=(6\Theta^3+\Theta)K_1(\Theta^{-1})
+3\Theta^2K_0(\Theta^{-1}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Theta\) is the dimensionless thermal temperature used by the source
normalization.  The code subtracts the part above \(\gamma_m\).  With
\(a=\gamma_m/\Theta\), the tail expansion is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}
&=\gamma\sum_{r=0}^{N_b} c_r\gamma^{-2r},\\
c_r&=(-1)^r\binom{1/2}{r},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
so \(b_r=-c_r\) are the subtraction coefficients.  The moments retained below
\(\gamma_m\) are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_m(\Theta)&=\sum_{r=0}^{N_b}
b_r\Theta^{m+2-2r}\Gamma(m+2-2r,a),\\
I_1(\Theta)&=J_1(\Theta)+H_1(\Theta),\\
I_2(\Theta)&=J_2(\Theta)+H_2(\Theta).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_b\) is the fixed thermal-tail expansion order.  The terms are
accumulated in increasing \(r\), and the series stops when the next correction is
smaller than \(\epsilon_{\rm th}\max(I,I_{\rm floor})\) for the moment \(I\).
For non-positive orders, the recurrence
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(s-1,a)=\frac{\Gamma(s,a)-a^{s-1}\exp(-a)}{s-1}
\end{equation}
steps downward from \(\Gamma(0,a)\).  These controls set the special-function
evaluation; they do not define a physical cutoff in the electron distribution.

The thermal temperature is fixed by matching the requested thermal content.  The
solve starts from a logarithmic mesh in \(\Theta\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Theta_r&=s_r\gamma_m,\\
s_r&=s_{\min}
\left(\frac{s_{\max}}{s_{\min}}\right)^{(r-1)/(N_s-1)},\\
r&=1,\ldots,N_s .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The quantities \(s_{\min}\), \(s_{\max}\), and \(N_s\) are fixed seed controls.
The bracket search shifts the trial scales by the fixed factor \(q_{\rm br}\)
until the lower and upper signs enclose the solution.  A secant interpolation
between the adjacent sign-changing entries gives the first iterate.  The
temperature is the root of
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(\Theta)&=\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta}+\ln I_1(\Theta)-\ln C,\\
\frac{dF}{d\Theta}
&=-\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta^2}
+\frac{1}{I_1(\Theta)}\frac{dI_1}{d\Theta}
=-\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta^2}+\frac{I_2(\Theta)}{\Theta^2I_1(\Theta)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(C\) is the requested normalization constant for the thermal part.  The
fractional temperature correction is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\Delta\Theta}{\Theta}
=\frac{F(\Theta)}
{\gamma_m/\Theta-\Theta^{-1}I_2(\Theta)/I_1(\Theta)} .
\end{equation}
The iteration uses \(\Theta\leftarrow\Theta-\Delta\Theta\) and stops when
\(|\Delta\Theta/\Theta|<\epsilon_\Theta\).  If the condition is not met within
\(N_\Theta\) updates, the temperature solve has failed.

After \(\Theta\) is fixed, the branch evaluates the hybrid injection density.
The logarithmic normalizations are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_{\rm th}&=\ln(1-f_e)-\ln I_1,\\
A_{\rm pl}&=\ln f_e-\ln I_{\rm pl},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(f_e\) is the nonthermal fraction and \(I_{\rm pl}\) is the nonthermal
tail integral.  The density is
\begin{equation}
\phi_{\rm h}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
\exp[L_{\rm th}(\gamma)],
&1<\gamma<\gamma_m,\\
\exp[L_{\rm pl}(\gamma)],
&\gamma\ge\gamma_m,\\
0,&\gamma\le1.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\rm th}&=\ln[\gamma(\gamma^2-1)^{1/2}]
-\gamma/\Theta+A_{\rm th},\\
L_{\rm pl}&=-p\ln\gamma-\gamma/\gamma_{\max}+A_{\rm pl}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_{\rm th}\) and \(L_{\rm pl}\) are logarithmic densities for the thermal
and nonthermal pieces.  Values with \(L_{\rm th}\) or \(L_{\rm pl}\) below the
declared exponent cutoff \(\ell_{\rm exp}\) are outside the evaluated source
support and contribute zero to the cell integral.  The output of this branch is
the hybrid source profile \(\phi_{\rm h}\) on the electron grid.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-Resolved Downstream State Details}

The downstream-state appendix explains how the two-dimensional electron branch
turns a shell spectrum into local cells behind the forward shock.  The active
coordinate is \(q\), not \(\chi\).  The shock front is at \(q=0\), and the
active downstream interval ends at
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_\ast&=1-\left(1-\frac{1}{r_{\rm sh}}\right)^{r_{\rm sh}},\\
\Delta q&=\frac{q_\ast}{N_\chi},\\
q_{a+1/2}&=a\Delta q,\qquad
q_a=\left(a-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Delta q .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
At the first radius the code places the one-zone spectrum into the first
downstream cell,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
U_{i1}(R_1)&=\frac{N_i^0}{\Delta q},\\
U_{ia}(R_1)&=0,\qquad a>1 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_i^0\) is the initial shell spectrum in electron bin \(i\), and
\(U_{ia}\) is the cell average in \(q\).  The shell spectrum is recovered by
\(\sum_a U_{ia}\Delta q\).

The downstream geometry gives each \(q\) cell a radius and a bulk Lorentz
factor.  For an external density profile \(n\propto R^{-k}\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar q_a&=1-q_a,\\
\alpha_\chi&=\frac{4-k}{3-k},\\
\chi_{{\rm BM},a}&=\bar q_a^{-\alpha_\chi}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(k\) is the external density index.  With shock-front four-speed
\(u_f=(\Gamma_f^2-1)^{1/2}\), speed \(\beta_f=u_f/\Gamma_f\), and bridge weight
\(\omega_f=\beta_f^2\), the two logarithmic radius offsets are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_{{\rm BM},a}&=-\frac{\chi_{{\rm BM},a}-1}
{4(4-k)\Gamma_f^2},\\
\lambda_{{\rm p},a}&=\frac{\ln \bar q_a}{r_{\rm sh}(3-k)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first offset is the Blandford--McKee limit.  The second offset is the
planar strong-shock limit.  The local cell radius is
\begin{equation}
r_a=R\exp\!\left[\omega_f\lambda_{{\rm BM},a}
+(1-\omega_f)\lambda_{{\rm p},a}\right].
\end{equation}
This map only assigns a local state to the transported electron content; it does
not add a new dynamical equation.

The downstream bulk speed is assigned with the same two-limit bridge.  The
limiting four-speeds are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_{{\rm BM},a}&=\frac{u_f}{\chi_{{\rm BM},a}^{1/2}},\\
\beta_{{\rm p},a}&=\beta_f e^{\lambda_{{\rm p},a}},\\
u_{{\rm p},a}&=\frac{\beta_{{\rm p},a}}
{(1-\beta_{{\rm p},a}^2)^{1/2}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The local downstream four-speed and Lorentz factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_a&=\sinh\!\left[\omega_f\sinh^{-1}u_{{\rm BM},a}
+(1-\omega_f)\sinh^{-1}u_{{\rm p},a}\right],\\
\Gamma_a&=(1+u_a^2)^{1/2},\qquad
\beta_a=\frac{u_a}{\Gamma_a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These quantities are stored with the local radius and later enter cooling,
emission, absorption, and projection.

The magnetic age measures how long a downstream cell has moved away from the
shock pattern.  The shock-pattern four-speed is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_{\rm s,BM}&=(2\Gamma_f^2-1)^{1/2},\\
u_{\rm s,p}&=\frac{r_{\rm sh}}{r_{\rm sh}-1}u_f,\\
\Psi_{\rm s}&=\omega_f\sinh^{-1}u_{\rm s,BM}
+(1-\omega_f)\sinh^{-1}u_{\rm s,p},\\
u_{\rm s}&=\sinh\Psi_{\rm s},\qquad
\beta_{\rm s}=\frac{u_{\rm s}}{(1+u_{\rm s}^2)^{1/2}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The relative shock speed, comoving cell width, cumulative distance, and age are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{{\rm rel},a}&=\frac{\beta_{\rm s}-\beta_a}
{1-\beta_{\rm s}\beta_a},\\
\Delta x'_a&=\Gamma_a\Delta x_{{\rm lab},a},\\
x'_a&=\sum_{b=1}^a\Delta x'_b,\\
t'_a&=\frac{x'_a}{\beta_{{\rm rel},a}c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t'_a\) is the comoving age used only by the magnetic-decay branch.

The local magnetic field is computed after the cell age is known.  When magnetic
decay is active, the magnetic fraction is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{B,a}=\epsilon_{B,\min}
+(\epsilon_B-\epsilon_{B,\min})
\left(1+\frac{t'_a}{t_B}\right)^{\alpha_t},
\end{equation}
and otherwise \(\epsilon_{B,a}=\epsilon_B\).  The local comoving energy density
and magnetic field are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e'_a&=r_{\rm sh}\Gamma_f(\Gamma_f-1)n(R)m_p c^2,\\
B'_a&=(8\pi\epsilon_{B,a}e'_a)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The exported field \(B'_a\) is the field used by the local synchrotron,
self-absorption, cooling, and diffusion kernels.

The downstream diffusion coefficient is evaluated from the same local magnetic
field.  For electron bin \(i\) in downstream cell \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_{{\rm L},ia}&=\frac{\gamma_i m_ec^2}{eB'_a},\\
\kappa_{ia}&=\frac{c\,r_{{\rm L},ia}}{3}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This Bohm coefficient is used only by the \(\chi\)-resolved transport branch.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-Resolved Photon-History Details}

The photon-history branch constructs the seed-photon field that reaches each
downstream cell from older cells.  The comoving shock time is accumulated along
the radius grid,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_1&=0,\\
t'_j&=t'_{j-1}+
\frac{R_j-R_{j-1}}
{\bar\beta_j\bar\Gamma_j c},\\
\bar\Gamma_j&=\frac{\Gamma_j+\Gamma_{j-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(j\) labels the target radius and \(m\) labels an earlier source radius.
For a source cell \(s=(m,a)\) and a target cell \(t=(j,b)\), the source is used
only when it lies farther behind the shock and light has enough time to reach
the target,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x'_{m,a}&\ge x'_{j,b},\\
c(t'_j-t'_m)&\ge x'_{m,a}-x'_{j,b}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The finite source fraction is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
w_s&=\min\left(1,\frac{c\Delta t'_m}{\Delta x'_{m,a}}\right),\\
\Delta t'_m&=t'_m-t'_{m-1},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(\Delta t'_1=t'_2-t'_1\) at the first interval.  These conditions define
which old photons enter the local cooling and SSC seed field.

The photon-history branch maps each accepted source spectrum into the target
comoving frame.  The relative velocity and Doppler factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{st}&=\frac{\beta_t-\beta_s}{1-\beta_t\beta_s},\\
\Gamma_{st}&=(1-\beta_{st}^2)^{-1/2},\\
{\cal D}_{st}&=\Gamma_{st}(1+\beta_{st}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The target frequency \(\nu'\) reads the source field at
\(\nu'_s=\nu'/{\cal D}_{st}\).  Between two positive source bins
\(\nu'_l<\nu'_s<\nu'_{l+1}\), a source luminosity or photon-density field \(Q\)
is interpolated by
\begin{equation}
Q_s(\nu'_s)=Q_{s,l}
\exp\!\left[
\frac{\ln\nu'_s-\ln\nu'_l}
{\ln\nu'_{l+1}-\ln\nu'_l}
\ln\!\left(\frac{Q_{s,l+1}}{Q_{s,l}}\right)
\right].
\end{equation}
The mapped luminosity is weighted by \(w_s{\cal D}_{st}^3\), while the mapped
photon density is weighted by \(w_s{\cal D}_{st}^2\).  A source interval with a
nonpositive interpolation endpoint contributes zero.

The path attenuation multiplies the accepted photon contribution by the
finite-cell transfer weight.  For one optical-depth segment,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal W(\tau)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \tau\to0,\\
(1-e^{-\tau})/\tau, & \tau>0 .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
For a partial comoving path of length \(\Delta x'\) inside downstream cell \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm seg}
=\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm prop}
\frac{\Delta x'}{\Delta x'_a}.
\end{equation}
Let \(a_+\) and \(a_-\) denote the cells containing the upstream and downstream
endpoints \(x'_+\) and \(x'_-<x'_+\).  The full attenuation factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal A}_{\nu'}(x'_+,x'_-)
&=\mathcal W(\tau_{\nu',a_+}^{\rm seg})
\mathcal W(\tau_{\nu',a_-}^{\rm seg})e^{S_{\nu'}},\\
S_{\nu'}
&=\sum_{a=a_-+1}^{a_+-1}
\ln\mathcal W(\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm prop}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The propagation optical depth on the reduced cooling grid is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm prop}
&=\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm SSA}
+\tau_{\nu',a}^{\gamma\gamma},\\
\tau_{\nu',a}^{\gamma\gamma}
&=\Delta x'_a\int \sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu',\tilde\nu')
n_{\tilde\nu',a}\,d\tilde\nu' .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus the stored history field includes SSA and local pair opacity before it is
used as a cooling or SSC target.

The streamed light-cone update advances the same history field one radial step
at a time.  It stores \(P_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}\) and
\(n_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}\) from radius \(j-1\) to radius \(j\), so the target
cell \(b\) only needs the interval
\begin{equation}
[x'_{j,b},\,x'_{j,b}+c(t'_j-t'_{j-1})].
\end{equation}
New emission from previous-shell cell \(a\) contributes over the overlap
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x'_{{\rm L},ab}
&=\max(x'_{a-1/2,j-1},x'_{j,b}),\\
x'_{{\rm R},ab}
&=\min(x'_{a+1/2,j-1},x'_{j,b}+c\Delta t'_j),\\
w_{a\to b}^{(j)}
&=\frac{[x'_{{\rm R},ab}-x'_{{\rm L},ab}]_+}
{\Delta x'_{a,j-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \([z]_+=\max(z,0)\).  The streamed update applies the same
\({\cal D}_{st}\), positive-field interpolation, and path attenuation to both the
carried history field and the newly emitted previous-shell field.

\subsection{Downstream \texorpdfstring{\(q\)}{q}-Space Advection--Diffusion Details}

The downstream \(q\)-transport appendix gives the finite-volume update for the
cell average \(U_{ia}\).  The transport speed in \(q\) is
\begin{equation}
A_q(q,R)=\frac{(3-k)(q_\ast-q)}{R},
\end{equation}
where \(k\) is the external density index.  The local conservation law advanced
by the implicit branch is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial(A_qU_i)}{\partial q}
=\frac{\partial}{\partial q}
\left({\cal K}_i\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial q}\right)
+Q_i^{(1)}(R)\delta_{a1}.
\end{equation}
Here \(Q_i^{(1)}\) is the shock-front source already divided by the first
\(q\)-cell width.  In the characteristic energy branch, the same first-cell
source is applied during the energy-characteristic update, while the \(q\)
remap itself moves existing electron content.

The characteristic \(q\)-remap moves cell faces along the local flow in \(q\).
Inside downstream cell \(a\), the face speed is reconstructed as
\begin{equation}
A_q(q)=\alpha_a+\zeta_a q .
\end{equation}
A face at \(q_f^{n+1}\) is traced back over \(\Delta R\) by
\begin{equation}
q_f^n=
\begin{cases}
\left(q_f^{n+1}+\alpha_a/\zeta_a\right)e^{-\zeta_a\Delta R}
-\alpha_a/\zeta_a, & \zeta_a\ne0,\\
q_f^{n+1}-\alpha_a\Delta R, & \zeta_a=0 .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
If the back-traced face crosses a cell boundary, the same analytic step is
started again in the next downstream cell until the radial step is exhausted.

The conservative remap reads the electron content from a reconstructed
piecewise-parabolic profile.  For an input profile \(U_i(q)\), define the prefix
integral
\begin{equation}
M_i(q)=\int_0^q U_i(q')\,dq' .
\end{equation}
The remapped cell average is
\begin{equation}
U_{ia}^{\rm adv}
=\frac{M_i(q_{a+1/2}^{n})-M_i(q_{a-1/2}^{n})}{\Delta q}.
\end{equation}
This operation changes where the electrons sit behind the shock.  It does not
change their energy.

The diffusion metric converts Bohm diffusion in physical downstream distance
