into diffusion on the \(q\) grid.  With the bridged radius law,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
s(q)&=R-r(q),\\
J_q(q)&\equiv\frac{\partial q}{\partial s}
=\left[-R\frac{\partial(r/R)}{\partial q}\right]^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The diffusive face coefficient is
\begin{equation}
{\cal K}_{i,a+1/2}
=\frac{\kappa_{i,a+1/2}}{\beta_{\rm sh}c}
J_{q,a+1/2}^{2},
\end{equation}
and the face flux is
\begin{equation}
{\cal G}_{i,a+1/2}
={\cal K}_{i,a+1/2}
\frac{U_{i,a+1}-U_{ia}}{\Delta q}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\kappa_{i,a+1/2}\) is the face-averaged Bohm coefficient and
\(\beta_{\rm sh}\) is the shock speed.

The implicit diffusion solve uses centered face differences.  For a right-hand
state \(U_{ia}^{\rm rhs}\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\delta_{a+}U_i^{n+1}
&=U_{i,a+1}^{n+1}-U_{ia}^{n+1},\\
{\cal G}_{a+}^{n+1}
&={\cal K}_{i,a+1/2}
\frac{\delta_{a+}U_i^{n+1}}{\Delta q},\\
\Delta_a{\cal G}^{n+1}
&={\cal G}_{a+}^{n+1}-{\cal G}_{a-}^{n+1},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}
-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta q}\Delta_a{\cal G}^{n+1}
&=U_{ia}^{\rm rhs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The joint implicit driver solves the same tridiagonal system after adding the
upwind advection coefficients from \(A_q\).  If the optional absorbing outer
boundary is active, the last cell uses \(U_{i,N_\chi+1}=0\), so the final
diagonal receives
\begin{equation}
{\cal K}_{i,N_\chi+1/2}U_{i,N_\chi}^{n+1}.
\end{equation}
Otherwise the outer diffusive flux is closed.  The solved cell averages are
projected to nonnegative values after the tridiagonal solve.

\subsection{Downstream Energy-Space Update Details}

The downstream energy update cools each \(q\) column on the natural-log electron
grid \(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  In the default log-gamma branch, the local cooling speed
is
\begin{equation}
\xi_{i+1/2,a}=D_{i+1/2,a}+\frac{1}{R},
\end{equation}
where \(D_{i+1/2,a}\) is the radiative cooling coefficient and \(1/R\) is the
shell adiabatic term.  With
\(\lambda_{i+1/2,a}=-(\Delta R/\Delta x)\xi_{i+1/2,a}\), the implicit
backward-sweep coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_{ia}&=1-\lambda_{i-1/2,a},\\
C_{ia}&=\frac{2\lambda_{i+1/2,a}}{H_{ia}+H_{i+1,a}},\\
\rho_{ia}&=\frac{U_{ia}^{n}}{H_{ia}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The solution is recovered from high to low electron energy,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
U_{N_\gamma,a}^{n+1}&=\rho_{N_\gamma,a},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}
&=\Pi_+\!\left(\rho_{ia}-C_{ia}U_{i+1,a}^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The projection \(\Pi_+\) acts on the solved cell average only and does not mix
neighboring energy bins.  The output is the cooled column \(U_{ia}^{n+1}\) passed
to the radiation kernels.

The PWN/CR transport option uses a local downstream adiabatic term instead of the
global \(1/R\) shell term.  In that branch
\begin{equation}
\xi_{i+1/2,a}=D_{i+1/2,a}+A_{{\rm ad},a},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
A_{{\rm ad},a}=
\frac{1}{3\beta_f}
\left(
\frac{2\beta_a}{r_a}
+\left.\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial r}\right|_a
\right).
\end{equation}
Here \(r_a\) and \(\beta_a\) come from the downstream geometry.  The same
backward sweep then updates each column, so this option changes only the
adiabatic coefficient, not the energy-grid solve.

The stochastic energy-diffusion option adds a conservative diffusion step in
\(x=\ln\gamma_e\).  With
\begin{equation}
\lambda_x=\frac{\Delta R\,\alpha_{\rm st}}{R\,\Delta x^2},
\end{equation}
the interior equation is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal L}_{ia}^{n+1}
&=-U_{i-1,a}^{n+1}+2U_{ia}^{n+1}-U_{i+1,a}^{n+1},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}+\lambda_x{\cal L}_{ia}^{n+1}
&=U_{ia}^{n}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The low- and high-energy edge diagonals are \(1+\lambda_x\), so the stochastic
energy flux through both grid boundaries is zero.

\section{Photon, SSC, and SSA Kernels} \label{app:photon-kernels}

This appendix gives the technical radiation kernels that support the main
particle--radiation transport section.  The physical objects are the local
synchrotron luminosity, the seed photon density, the SSC luminosity, the
self-absorption heating coefficient, and the photon-survival factors.  The main
text states how these objects enter cooling, emission, and observer projection;
the appendix gives the quadrature, interpolation, and kernel formulas used to
evaluate them.

\subsection{Radiation ABI Binding}

The radiation ABI layer maps public radiation products to compiled kernels.  On
first access it binds \texttt{annihilation}, \texttt{cal\_ebl},
\texttt{ssc\_spec}, and \texttt{ssc\_spec\_nonuniform}.  These names identify
runtime entry points only.  They do not add photon states beyond the SSC
luminosity, local pair absorption, and external EBL attenuation defined in the
main text.

The Fan-style analytic Klein--Nishina closure estimates how much synchrotron
photon energy can scatter in the Thomson regime.  For electron node \(i\), the
threshold is the electron Lorentz factor \(\hat\gamma_i\) whose synchrotron
photons meet the Klein--Nishina limit for that node.  The upper electron cutoff is
\(\gamma_M=\gamma_{\max}\).  The output of the formulas below is
\(\eta_i^{\rm KN}\), the photon-energy fraction below the threshold.

In fast cooling, \(\gamma_m>\gamma_c\), the relevant spectrum starts at
\(\gamma_c\).  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_f&=\frac{(p-1)\gamma_m}{p-2}-\gamma_c,\\
B_f&=\gamma_m^{p-1}\gamma_M^{2-p}
-(p-1)\gamma_m-(2-p)\gamma_c,\\
C_f&=(p-1)\gamma_m-(p-2)\gamma_c,\\
H_f(\hat\gamma_i)&=
\gamma_m^{p-1}(\gamma_M^{2-p}-\hat\gamma_i^{2-p}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The fraction below \(\hat\gamma_i\) is
\begin{equation}
\eta_i^{\rm KN}=
\begin{cases}
0, & \hat\gamma_i<\gamma_c,\\[0.6ex]
\dfrac{\hat\gamma_i-\gamma_c}{A_f},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_m,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
\dfrac{(2-p)(\hat\gamma_i-\gamma_c)}{B_f},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_m,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{\gamma_m^{p-1}\hat\gamma_i^{2-p}}{C_f},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{B_f}{H_f(\hat\gamma_i)},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1, & \hat\gamma_i\ge\gamma_M.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Thus the branch returns zero when the threshold is below the lowest fast-cooling
break and unity when it is above the cutoff.

In slow cooling, \(\gamma_m\le\gamma_c\), the relevant spectrum starts at
\(\gamma_m\).  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_s&=\frac{\gamma_c^{3-p}}{p-2}-\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
B_s&=(3-p)\gamma_c\gamma_M^{2-p}
-\gamma_c^{3-p}-(2-p)\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
C_s&=\gamma_c^{3-p}-(p-2)\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
U_i&=\hat\gamma_i^{3-p}-\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
V_i&=(3-p)\gamma_c\hat\gamma_i^{2-p},\\
W_i&=(3-p)\gamma_c(\gamma_M^{2-p}-\hat\gamma_i^{2-p}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The slow-cooling fraction is
\begin{equation}
\eta_i^{\rm KN}=
\begin{cases}
0, & \hat\gamma_i<\gamma_m,\\[0.6ex]
\dfrac{U_i}{A_s},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_c,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
\dfrac{(2-p)U_i}{B_s},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_c,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{V_i}{C_s},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{W_i}{B_s},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1, & \hat\gamma_i\ge\gamma_M.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
This piecewise value enters the main-text relation
\(Y_i^{\rm F}(1+Y_i^{\rm F})=
\eta_{\rm rad}\eta_i^{\rm KN}\epsilon_e/\epsilon_B\).  The branch is an analytic
closure for inverse-Compton cooling; it does not replace the tabulated photon
field used by the full IC/KN path.

The SSA heating quadrature computes the same absorbed-photon energy integral as
the main text.  For each electron node \(i\), the code first finds the photon
cells that contain \(\nu_{\min,i}\) and \(\nu_{u,i}\).  It clips those two cells
to the physical integration interval and treats all complete cells between them
with cached logarithmic Gauss weights.  For a low-frequency complete cell
\(\ell\), let \(\nu_{\ell q}\) and \(w_{\ell q}\) be the \(N_{\rm G}\)-point
nodes and weights in \(x=\ln\nu\), including the Jacobian \(d\nu=\nu\,dx\).  The
cached low-frequency prefix is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal P_k
=hc\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{q=1}^{N_{\rm G}}
w_{\ell q}n_\nu(\nu_{\ell q})\nu_{\ell q}^{-2/3}.
\end{equation}
If the full low-frequency cells run from \(k_1\) through \(k_2\), their
contribution to the heating coefficient is
\begin{equation}
A_{i,L}^{\rm abs}
=\kappa_1\frac{B_Q}{B'}(\chi_{\rm L}\nu_{\min,i})^{5/3}
\left(\mathcal P_{k_2}-\mathcal P_{k_1-1}\right).
\end{equation}
The high-frequency complete cells use the exponential part of the same
single-particle fit:
\begin{equation}
A_{i,H}^{\rm abs}
=\kappa_2\frac{B_Q}{B'}\gamma_i^{-3}\nu_B hc
\sum_{\ell,q} w_{\ell q}n_\nu(\nu_{\ell q})
\exp(-\nu_{\ell q}/\nu_{u,i}).
\end{equation}
The clipped boundary-cell integrals use the same two branches on the truncated
frequency range.  The returned value is
\(A_i^{\rm abs}=A_{i,L}^{\rm abs}+A_{i,H}^{\rm abs}\) plus the two clipped-cell
terms.  The shared interpolation primitive supplies \(n_\nu\) inside each cell;
positive endpoints use log--log interpolation and nonpositive endpoints use the
linear branch.

The grid-level inverse-Compton cooling path integrates over seed photons and
scattered photons on the same local frequency grid.  If the stored grid edges are
\(\nu_j\), the cell-centered measure is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar\nu_j&=(\nu_j\nu_{j+1})^{1/2},\\
\Delta\nu_j&=\bar\nu_j\left(\ln\nu_{j+1}-\ln\nu_j\right),\\
\epsilon_j&=\frac{h\bar\nu_j}{m_e c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The shared power-law interpolation gives \(n_{\nu,j}=n_\nu(\bar\nu_j)\).  For an
electron node \(i\), a seed cell \(j\), and a scattered cell \(\ell\), define
\[
b_{ij}=4\gamma_i\epsilon_j,\qquad
q_{i\ell j}=\frac{\epsilon_\ell}{b_{ij}(\gamma_i-\epsilon_\ell)} .
\]
The branch kernel used in the cooling integral is
\begin{equation}
G_{i\ell j}=
\begin{cases}
\bar\nu_\ell/\bar\nu_j-(4\gamma_i^2)^{-1},
& \bar\nu_j/(4\gamma_i^2)<\bar\nu_\ell\le\bar\nu_j,\\[1ex]
G_{\rm J}(q_{i\ell j},b_{ij}),
& 0<q_{i\ell j}<1,\quad \epsilon_\ell<\gamma_i,\quad
  \epsilon_\ell\le\epsilon_{\max,ij},\\[1ex]
0, & {\rm otherwise},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\epsilon_{\max,ij}=4\gamma_i^2\epsilon_j/(1+b_{ij})\).  The cooling
coefficient is accumulated as
\begin{equation}
A_i^{\rm IC}\simeq
\frac{3h\sigma_{\rm es}}{4m_e c\gamma_i^3}
\sum_j\frac{n_{\nu,j}}{\bar\nu_j}\Delta\nu_j
\sum_\ell \bar\nu_\ell G_{i\ell j}\Delta\nu_\ell .
\end{equation}
This is the discrete form of the main-text IC loss integral.

The coupled-budget inverse-Compton path uses the same physical kernel but changes
the quadrature weights used by the implicit joint solve.  With \(x_\nu=\ln\nu\),
uniform spacing \(\Delta x_\nu\), and Simpson weights \(w_j^{\rm S}\), it returns
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_i^{\rm IC,b}&=
\frac{3h\sigma_{\rm es}}{4m_e c\gamma_i^3}
\left(\frac{\Delta x_\nu}{3}\right)^2\\
&\quad\times
\sum_\ell w_\ell^{\rm S}\nu_\ell^2
\left[\sum_j w_j^{\rm S}n_{\nu,j}K_{i\ell j}\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For seed cells below the scattered cell, \(K_{i\ell j}=G_{\rm J}(q_{i\ell j},b_{ij})\).
For seed cells at or above the scattered cell,
\[
K_{i\ell j}=
\max\left[\frac{\nu_\ell}{\nu_j}-\frac{1}{4\gamma_i^2},0\right].
\]
The output \(A_i^{\rm IC,b}\) is an energy-budget coefficient for the joint
electron--photon solve; it does not define a separate IC cooling law.

The fast synchrotron emissivity paths use an analytic approximation to the
single-particle synchrotron kernel.  For an electron with
\(\nu_c=C_\nu B'\gamma_e^2\), define \(x=\nu/\nu_c\).  The kernel used by the
radiation loop is
\begin{equation}
\Phi(x)=
\begin{cases}
A_\Phi x^{1/3}, & x<x_{\rm L},\\[1ex]
A_\Phi e^{-x}\left(x^{-2/3}+B_\Phi^2\right)^{-1/2},
& x_{\rm L}\le x\le x_{\rm H},\\[1ex]
0, & x>x_{\rm H}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The low-\(x\) branch keeps the synchrotron asymptote, the middle branch carries
the exponential high-frequency decline, and the last branch truncates the finite
kernel table.  The constants \(A_\Phi\), \(B_\Phi\), \(x_{\rm L}\), and
\(x_{\rm H}\) are fixed coefficients of this single-particle approximation; they
are not physical model parameters.

The shared radiation interpolation primitive evaluates tabulated radiation
fields inside one frequency cell.  For adjacent nodes
\((\nu_0,Y_0)\) and \((\nu_1,Y_1)\), positive endpoint values use the local
power-law segment
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
s_Y&=\frac{\ln(Y_1/Y_0)}{\ln(\nu_1/\nu_0)},\\
Y(\nu)&=Y_0\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_0}\right)^{s_Y}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Y\) can be a synchrotron luminosity, an absorbed luminosity, an optical
depth, or a photon density.  If either endpoint is nonpositive, the helper uses
the linear segment between the two stored values.  The rule only evaluates the
stored field; it does not change the electron spectrum or photon state.

The logarithmic Gauss helper integrates a radiation field over one positive
frequency cell.  With \(x=\ln\nu\), \(x_m=(\ln\nu_0+\ln\nu_1)/2\), and
\(\Delta x=(\ln\nu_1-\ln\nu_0)/2\), the fixed \(N_{\rm G}\)-point rule uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_a&=\exp(x_m+\Delta x\,\xi_a),\\
w_a&=\Delta x\,\omega_a\,\nu_a,\\
\int_{\nu_0}^{\nu_1}Y(\nu)\,d\nu
&\simeq \sum_{a=1}^{N_{\rm G}} w_a\,Y(\nu_a).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\xi_a\) and \(\omega_a\) are the Gauss--Legendre nodes and weights on
\([-1,1]\).  The factor \(\nu_a\) in \(w_a\) is the Jacobian from
\(d\nu=\nu\,dx\).  The reduced cooling table uses the same positive-frequency
coordinate; for at least \(N_{\rm red}\) support points, it places the last point
at \(\nu_{\max}\) and the preceding points between \(\nu_{\min}\) and a named
tail location set by \(f_{\rm tail}\).

The SSA heating segment returns the absorbed photon energy over one frequency
interval.  It uses the same interpolation \(n_\nu(\nu)\) and the same logarithmic
Gauss nodes as the photon-field helpers.  For a segment with local coefficients
\(\Sigma_{\rm L}\), \(\Sigma_{\rm H}\), and \(\nu_u\), the absorption cross
section is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\sigma_{\rm SSA}(\nu)&=
\begin{cases}
\Sigma_{\rm L}\nu^{-5/3}, & \nu<\nu_u,\\[1ex]
\Sigma_{\rm H}\dfrac{\nu_B}{\nu}\exp(-\nu/\nu_u), & \nu\ge\nu_u .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
If \(\nu_q\) and \(w_q\) are the logarithmic Gauss nodes and weights on the
segment, the segment contribution is
\begin{equation}
A_{\rm seg}^{\rm abs}
=c\sum_q w_q\,h\nu_q\,n_\nu(\nu_q)\sigma_{\rm SSA}(\nu_q).
\end{equation}
The coefficients are fixed by the local electron bin and magnetic field.  This
helper only evaluates one piece of \(A_i^{\rm abs}\); it does not change the
stored photon field.

The self-absorption-frequency quadrature evaluates \(\tau_\nu\) at trial
frequencies.  On an electron cell \([x_i,x_{i+1}]\), with \(x=\ln\gamma_e\), the
emissivity integrand uses a linear reconstruction of \(N_e\).  The optical-depth
integrand uses the conservative endpoint difference of
\(q=N_e/\gamma_e^2\).  For one cell, two Gauss rules give estimates
\((P^{(2)},\tau^{(2)})\) and \((P^{(3)},\tau^{(3)})\).  The cell is split when
\begin{equation}
\max\left[
\frac{|P^{(3)}-P^{(2)}|}{|P^{(3)}|},
\frac{|\tau^{(3)}-\tau^{(2)}|}{|\tau^{(3)}|}
\right]>\epsilon_a .
\end{equation}
Here \(P^{(m)}\) and \(\tau^{(m)}\) are the emissivity and optical-depth
estimates from the \(m\)-point rule.  The tolerance \(\epsilon_a\) controls only
this quadrature evaluation of \(\tau_\nu\).

The self-absorption root search solves \(\tau_\nu(\nu_a)=1\).  It starts from a
named trial frequency \(\nu_{a,0}\), expands a logarithmic bracket by \(q_a\), and
keeps the search inside
\begin{equation}
\nu_{a,\min}\le\nu\le
\nu_{\rm cap},\qquad
\nu_{\rm cap}=\max\left[\nu_{a,\rm floor},
\min\left(\nu_{a,\max},\eta_a\max_i\nu_{c,i+1/2}\right)\right].
\end{equation}
Here \(\nu_{c,i+1/2}=C_\nu B'\gamma_{i+1/2}^2\) is the characteristic
synchrotron frequency at an electron-cell face.  Inside a bracket
\((\nu_l,\nu_h)\), with positive optical depths \(\tau_l\) and \(\tau_h\), the
logarithmic secant estimate is
\begin{equation}
\ln\nu_\ast=
\ln\nu_l-\ln\tau_l
\frac{\ln\nu_h-\ln\nu_l}{\ln\tau_h-\ln\tau_l}.
\end{equation}
The bracket is updated with a new \(\tau_\nu(\nu_\ast)\) evaluation until its
logarithmic width is below \(\epsilon_{\ln\nu}\), or until \(N_a\) updates have
been used.  If a stored optical-depth grid already brackets \(\tau_\nu=1\), the
same log-log root formula is applied between the two adjacent grid frequencies.

The intrinsic polarization helper computes a local synchrotron polarization
diagnostic from the transported electron spectrum.  For
\(x=\nu/(C_\nu B'\gamma_e^2)\), it first evaluates two standard single-particle
kernels:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(x)&=x\int_0^\infty
e^{-x\cosh u}\frac{\cosh(5u/3)}{\cosh u}\,du,\\
G(x)&=x\int_0^\infty e^{-x\cosh u}\cosh(2u/3)\,du .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These kernels give the two linear-polarization emissivity kernels
\[
P_\perp(x)=\frac{F(x)+G(x)}{2},\qquad
P_\parallel(x)=\frac{F(x)-G(x)}{2}.
\]
The helper then integrates these kernels over the same local electron spectrum:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
I_\perp(\nu)&=\int N_e(\gamma_e)P_\perp(x)\,d\gamma_e,\\
I_\parallel(\nu)&=\int N_e(\gamma_e)P_\parallel(x)\,d\gamma_e,\\
\Pi_\nu^{\rm loc}&=\frac{I_\perp-I_\parallel}{I_\perp+I_\parallel}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The output is the intrinsic shell-frame diagnostic
\(\Pi_\nu^{\rm loc}\), together with split local luminosities
\((1+\Pi_\nu^{\rm loc})L_\nu^{\rm syn}/2\) and
\((1-\Pi_\nu^{\rm loc})L_\nu^{\rm syn}/2\).  This helper does not perform
observer-frame polarization projection.

The polarization kernel uses analytic limits outside the direct quadrature
window.  For \(x<x_{\rm p,L}\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(x)&=2^{2/3}\Gamma(2/3)x^{1/3},\\
G(x)&=2^{-1/3}\Gamma(2/3)x^{1/3}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For \(x>x_{\rm p,H}\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(x)&=\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)^{1/2}e^{-x}
\left(1+\frac{55}{72x}\right),\\
G(x)&=\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)^{1/2}e^{-x}
\left(1+\frac{7}{72x}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For \(x_{\rm p,L}\le x\le x_{\rm p,H}\), the helper integrates the defining
\(u\)-space expressions over
\[
0\le u\le \sinh^{-1}(\zeta_{\rm p}/x)
\]
with \(N_{\rm p}\) cells and an \(N_{\rm G,p}\)-point Gauss rule in each cell.
The controls \(x_{\rm p,L}\), \(x_{\rm p,H}\), \(\zeta_{\rm p}\), \(N_{\rm p}\),
and \(N_{\rm G,p}\) define the special-function evaluation window only.

The thermal Bessel table supplies the modified Bessel function \(K_2(z)\) used in
Maxwell--Juttner electron normalizations.  It uses the small-\(z\) limit
\(K_2(z)=2/z^2\), a piecewise-linear table for \(z_{\rm L}\le z\le z_{\rm H}\),
and the large-\(z\) expansion
\begin{equation}
K_2(z)\simeq
\left(\frac{\pi}{2z}\right)^{1/2}e^{-z}
\left(1+\frac{15}{8z}+\frac{105}{128z^2}\right)
\end{equation}
above the tabulated range.  The table is a special-function evaluator; it does
not add a new thermal-electron transport branch.

The adaptive radiation-grid helper selects the electron points used only by
radiation quadrature.  It starts from the transported spectrum
\(N_e(\gamma_e)\), finds the first and last positive bins
\(i_-\le i\le i_+\), and keeps the full active grid if
\(N_{\rm act}=i_+-i_-+1\le N_{\rm rad}\).  If the active grid is larger than
this radiation budget, it requests \(m_{\rm rad}\) support points and relabels
the active segment by \(1\le i\le N_{\rm act}\).  The logarithmic coordinates are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x_i&=\ln\gamma_i,\\
y_i&=\ln N_{e,i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These coordinates are defined only on positive electron bins.  The helper does
not change \(N_e\); it only returns a shorter list of \(\gamma_e\) points for
synchrotron, SSC, and absorption integrals.

The helper estimates \(dy/dx\) and \(d^2y/dx^2\) because radiation integrals need
more points near sharp spectral bends.  It first computes finite-difference
slopes,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
D_1&=\frac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1},\\
D_{N_{\rm act}}&=\frac{y_{N_{\rm act}}-y_{N_{\rm act}-1}}
{x_{N_{\rm act}}-x_{N_{\rm act}-1}},\\
D_i&=\frac{y_{i+1}-y_{i-1}}{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
then converts them to curvature estimates,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
K_1&=\frac{D_2-D_1}{x_2-x_1},\\
K_{N_{\rm act}}&=\frac{D_{N_{\rm act}}-D_{N_{\rm act}-1}}
{x_{N_{\rm act}}-x_{N_{\rm act}-1}},\\
K_i&=\frac{D_{i+1}-D_{i-1}}{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here the interior equations apply for \(2\le i\le N_{\rm act}-1\).  The local
log-grid width is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
h_1&=x_2-x_1,\\
h_{N_{\rm act}}&=x_{N_{\rm act}}-x_{N_{\rm act}-1},\\
h_i&=\frac{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The curvature measure is \(q_i=|K_i|h_i\).  After averaging \(q_i\) over
\(N_{\rm av}\) neighboring points to obtain \(\bar q_i\), the sampling weight is
\begin{equation}
w_i=\max\left(\bar q_i,\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\rm act}}\bar q_j}
{m_{\rm rad}g_{\rm rad}}\right).
\end{equation}
The factor \(g_{\rm rad}\) keeps finite coverage in low-curvature parts of the
spectrum.

The helper turns these point weights into log-cell weights and samples their
cumulative sum.  The cell weights are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
W_1&=w_1(x_2-x_1),\\
W_{N_{\rm act}}&=w_{N_{\rm act}}(x_{N_{\rm act}}-x_{N_{\rm act}-1}),\\
W_i&=w_i\frac{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}}{2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the cumulative coordinate is \(C_i=\sum_{j=1}^i W_j\).  The target locations
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
s_\ell&=\frac{(\ell-1)C_{N_{\rm act}}}{m_{\rm rad}-1},\\
\ell&=1,\ldots,m_{\rm rad}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Each \(s_\ell\) maps to the first source index with \(C_i\ge s_\ell\).  Duplicate
indices are removed, missing slots are filled from unused active indices, and
the final list is sorted.  The caller also appends the active endpoints and their
immediate outside neighbors when they exist.  The output is therefore a compact
radiation grid, with denser support near cooling breaks, injection turnovers,
and high-energy cutoffs.

\section{Hadronic and Pair Feedback} \label{app:hadronic-pair-feedback}\label{app:hadronic-figure}

The hadronic and pair-feedback appendix records the optional shell-averaged
high-energy state.  When this branch is active, the code reads the local magnetic
field, shell volume, target photon field, and nonthermal proton budget from the
one-dimensional radius sequence.  It then computes proton transport, photon
losses, neutrino and secondary-pair production, and photon survival factors
before observer projection.  This appendix documents that branch as a local
transport object; it does not claim that hadronic feedback controls every
afterglow light curve.

The hadronic branch has a hard dimensional boundary.  The formal forward-shock
feedback path is one-dimensional and shell averaged.  It is not a \(\chi\)-local
hadronic calculation, because the target photon field, hadron density, secondary
feedback, and observer projection are not defined as a closed \(\chi\)-resolved
state.  If \(N_{\rm cas}>1\), the pair branch follows a shell-sequence
\(\gamma\gamma\) pair/synchrotron cascade.  IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades
are unsupported in the present state contract.

Figure~\ref{fig:hadronic-feedback} shows the three quantities that set the scale
of this optional branch: the target photon density, the interaction thresholds,
and the finite-shell transfer factor.  These are diagnostics of the active
one-dimensional branch, not evidence that every hadronic channel is enabled in
every calculation.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/figA1_hadronic_feedback.pdf}
  \caption{Hadronic and pair-feedback branch record.  (a) Representative target photon field after conversion from radiation energy density to photon number density.  (b) \(p\gamma\), Bethe-Heitler, and \(\gamma\gamma\) threshold scales written from particle rest-mass energies.  (c) Pure attenuation \(e^{-\tau}\) and finite-shell transfer \((1-e^{-\tau})/\tau\).  The panels document the optional one-dimensional branch; unsupported \(\chi\)-resolved hadronic transport and IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades are not inferred from this figure.}
  \label{fig:hadronic-feedback}
\end{figure*}

\section{Reverse-Shock Technical Details} \label{app:reverse-shock-technical}

This appendix holds the reverse-shock algebra that is too detailed for the main
method section.  It records the finite-strength MHD jump, magnetized upstream
bookkeeping, event splitting, post-crossing evolution, secondary density-jump
shock diagnostics, and reacceleration remaps.  The main text keeps the ejecta
state, activation boundary, jump outputs, reverse-electron injection, radiation
products, and supported boundaries.

\subsection{Finite-Strength MHD Jump Cubic}

The finite-strength MHD jump gives the downstream shock-frame four-speed used by
the magnetized reverse shock.  The upstream state is described by the relative
Lorentz factor \(g=\gamma_{34}\) and the ejecta magnetization \(\sigma\).  The
local algebra uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
g&\equiv\gamma_{34},\\
\hat{\gamma}&=\frac{4}{3}+\frac{1}{3g},\\
h_1&=\hat{\gamma}-1,\qquad
h_2=\hat{\gamma}-2,\qquad
y&\equiv u_d^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(u_d\) is the downstream four-speed in the reverse-shock frame.  The
parameter \(\sigma_{\rm cut}\) is an algorithmic branch parameter; it is not a
physical magnetization boundary.

The low-\(\sigma\) branch returns the hydrodynamic-strength root of the same
shock-frame problem,
\begin{equation}
y=\frac{(g-1)h_1^2}{-\hat{\gamma}h_2(g-1)+2}.
\end{equation}
For \(\sigma>\sigma_{\rm cut}\), the MHD branch instead solves
\begin{equation}
a_3y^3+a_2y^2+a_1y+a_0=0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_3={}&-\hat{\gamma}h_2(g-1)+2,\\
a_2={}&-(g+1)\left[-h_2(\hat{\gamma}g^2+1)+\hat{\gamma}h_1g\right]\sigma\\
&-(g-1)\left[-\hat{\gamma}h_2(g^2-2)+2g+3\right],\\
a_1={}&(g+1)\left[\hat{\gamma}\left(1-\frac{\hat{\gamma}}{4}\right)
(g^2-1)+1\right]\sigma^2\\
&+(g^2-1)\left[2g+h_2(\hat{\gamma}g-1)\right]\sigma
+(g+1)(g-1)^2h_1^2,\\
a_0={}&-\frac{1}{4}(g-1)(g+1)^2h_2^2\sigma^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These coefficients encode the finite upstream magnetic pressure and enthalpy in
the jump condition.

The cubic is solved by reducing it to a depressed form.  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
b_c&=\frac{a_2}{a_3},\qquad
c_c=\frac{a_1}{a_3},\qquad
d_c=\frac{a_0}{a_3},\\
p_c&=c_c-\frac{b_c^2}{3},\\
q_c&=\frac{2b_c^3}{27}-\frac{b_cc_c}{3}+d_c,\\
a_c&=\left(-\frac{p_c}{3}\right)^{1/2},\\
\psi_c&=\frac{1}{3}\cos^{-1}\!\left(\frac{3q_c}{2p_ca_c}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The physical root used by the shell dynamics is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y&=2a_c\cos\left(\psi_c-\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)-\frac{b_c}{3},\\
u_d&=y^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The upstream shock-frame four-speed is then
\begin{equation}
u_u=\left[(1+u_d^2)(g^2-1)\right]^{1/2}+g u_d .
\end{equation}
Thus the jump returns \(u_d\), the compression ratio \(r_\sigma=u_u/u_d\), and
the magnetized downstream heat used by the primary reverse-shock state.

\section{Projection, Fitting, and Runtime Details} \label{app:projection-runtime}

This appendix records runtime details that support projection, fitting, and
reproducibility.  It gives the equal-arrival-time interpolation, ray-traced SSA
path bookkeeping, structured-patch accumulation, fitting wrappers, query caches,
and source-data mechanics.  The main text keeps the physical projection
equations, observer products, likelihood products, runtime outputs, and public
boundaries.

\subsection{Forward-Dynamics Runtime Details}

The forward-dynamics runtime record is an ABI-ordered container.  The public
model first builds one boundary vector, and the compiled dynamics kernel unpacks
only the slots it needs for the forward shock:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf b_{\rm dyn}\mapsto
\begin{gathered}
\left(
\Gamma_0,\epsilon_e,\epsilon_B,p,z,n_{\rm ISM},A_\ast,E_{\rm iso},
\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\rm end},f_e
\right),\\
\left(
t_1,t_2,L_0,q,R_{\rm tr},f_{\rm jump},w_{\rm jump},R_0
\right).
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Here \(t_1,t_2,L_0\), and \(q\) describe the optional luminosity source, while
\(R_{\rm tr}\), \(f_{\rm jump}\), \(w_{\rm jump}\), and \(R_0\) describe the
analytic density jump and inner wind cap.  The container itself is not a new
physical model; it only fixes how public inputs are passed to the compiled
kernel.  The kernel then starts from
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}_0=(\Gamma_0-\Delta\Gamma_{\rm init},
Y_{2,{\rm init}},U_{\rm init},R_{\rm init}),
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta\Gamma_{\rm init}\), \(Y_{2,{\rm init}}\), \(U_{\rm init}\), and
\(R_{\rm init}\) are fixed integration-start parameters.  These values set the
first numerical state before the radius-ordered shock history is written.

The forward-dynamics output grid is built in the source-frame on-axis time
coordinate.  The first positive grid time is
\begin{equation}
t_{{\rm on},\min}=\min(t_{\rm floor},\eta_{\rm dec}t_{\rm dec}) ,
\end{equation}
where \(t_{\rm floor}\) and \(\eta_{\rm dec}\) are fixed grid parameters.  The
grid and time-ordered update are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\ell_i&=\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\min}
+\frac{\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\rm end}
-\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\min}}{N_R-1}(i-1),\\
t_{{\rm on},i}&=10^{\ell_i},\\
\Delta t_i&=t_{{\rm on},i}-t_{{\rm on},i-1},\qquad t_{{\rm on},0}=0,\\
\mathbf{y}_i&=\mathbf{y}_{i-1}
+\int_{t_{{\rm on},i-1}}^{t_{{\rm on},i}}
F(t_{\rm on},\mathbf{y})\,dt_{\rm on}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(F\) is the selected forward-dynamics right-hand side from
Section~\ref{sec:dynamics}.  After each accepted step, the stored state is
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}_i=(Y_{\Gamma,i},Y_{2,i},Y_{U,i},Y_{R,i}).
\end{equation}
The public output arrays then record \((1+z)t_{{\rm on},i}\), \(Y_{\Gamma,i}\),
\(Y_{R,i}\), and the swept mass.  Branches that evolve a swept number column
convert it to mass before writing the public output.

The RK substep variable controls the positive-time integration interval.  It
obeys
\begin{equation}
0\le s\le \ln(t_{{\rm on},i}/t_{{\rm on},i-1}),
\qquad
|\Delta s|\le\Delta s_{\max},
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta s_{\max}\) is a fixed integration parameter.  The refinement
compares a trial state with the previous accepted trial and accepts only states
inside the dynamical domain \(\Gamma>1\) with positive evolved components.

\section{Software and Data Availability} \label{app:data}

The software and data record defines the evidence path for this methods paper.
The \asgard\ source code and manuscript code baseline are identified by the
software record in \citet{ASGARDSoftware}.  The figure evidence used here is the
tracked source tables in \texttt{paper/source\_data/}.  The formal figure entry
point is \texttt{tests/generate\_paper\_figures.py}, which writes the plotted
products under \texttt{paper/figures/}.  Exploratory output outside these tracked
manuscript paths is not evidence for the paper claims.  The implementation uses
NumPy, SciPy, Matplotlib, and Astropy
\citep{Harris2020,Virtanen2020,Hunter2007,Astropy2022}.
